3.4.1 DIVERSIFICATION  
    1. Reduced Concentration Risk. We agree that private credit assets significantly enhance portfolio 
diversification for insurers, particularly when markets are volatile. Instead of relying heavily on public corporate or 
government bonds, life insurers can diversify into uncorrelated or less correlated exposures, such as private 
corporate loans, structured finance, and infrastructure debt. 
 
    2. Financial Stability from Diverse Sources of Credit. The use of private capi tal can enhance financial stability 
by diversifying the sources of credit in an economy, thereby reducing systemic reliance on traditional lending 
sources.  
 
PROPOSED LANGUAGE. We suggest adding a second paragraph to 3.4.1  as follows: “Likewise, the use of 
private capital can enhance financial stability by diversifying the sources of credit in an economy, thereby 
reducing systemic reliance on traditional lending sources.  Diversification is equally critical at the system level: 
having both banks and institutional investors (e.g., insurers) provide credit can reduce systemic reliance on any 
single channel of lending. This ‘pluralization’ of credit sources bolsters resilience during economic downturns.”  
 
3.4.2 HIGHER POTENTIAL RETURNS 
    1. Capturing the Illiquidity Premium. Private credit investments often offer an illiquidity premium, benefiting 
long-term investors such as insurers and pension funds that can match stable, long -term funding with similarly 
long-dated, incremental-yielding assets.  (Appendix, slide 16). 
 
    2. Enhancing Policyholder Value.  In the U.S. alone, over $38 billion of private capital has flowed into the life 
sector since 2010, fueling more than $2.5 trillion in new deployment to the real economy and generating an 
estimated $46 billion in incremental value for policyholders since 2020. These higher returns help insurers 
maintain or improve guaranteed products such as annuities, which directly benefit retirees.  (Appendix, slide 2).  
 
   3. Imbalanced Reference to “Niche” Risks. The Issues Paper makes a broad claim that there are niche risks 
associated with “alternative assets”.  Any such suggestion should be deleted given its vague and substantiated 
nature, especially given that it is inappropriately placed in the section on benefits of  “alternative” assets.   
 
PROPOSED LANGUGAGE. We suggest revising the last sentence of 3.4.2 as follows: “Additionally, as is true in 
public markets, there is the theoretical risk that the sector is concentrated on a particular market segment, where

only the insurance sector and a few others are exposed.”  Additional language should then be added stating: 
“Risk management systems are in place to avoid such concentration risks. No insurer has been identified that is 
carrying excess market segment investment concentration risk.”  
 
3.4.3 MARKET SOPHISTICATION AND TRANSPARANCEY  
    1. Ongoing Normalization of Private Assets.  (See response to Q4.)  
 
    2. Growing Liquidity & Data Availability. While certain private credit instruments remain less liquid t han broadly 
traded public bonds, we see expanding liquidity in areas such as 144A securitizations (e.g., CLO tranches), and 
private placements with secondary trading. Simultaneously, regulatory frameworks (e.g., in the U.S., Bermuda, 
and Europe) increasingly require standardized disclosures that help supervisors and investors monitor exposures 
effectively. 
 
    3. Focus on Financing Relationship.  Private capital lenders tend to be focused on the lending relationship 
(i.e., what is being financed) rather than acquiring customers to provide a wider array of services (e.g., advisory, 
cash management, hedging). This results in rigorous underwriting and long-term relationships with borrowers 
(versus an “originate to distribute” model). 
 
3.4.4 CAPITAL OPTIMIZATION 
    1. Match Funding for Long-Dated Liabilities.  Life insurers’ liabilities often extend decades into the future, 
making them ideal holders of similarly long-duration, incremental-yielding assets. In many jurisdictions, solvency 
frameworks allow insurers to receive partial capital relief when these assets are demonstrably “matched” to 
stable liabilities. Such capital optimization not only supports better returns but also frees capacity to underwrite 
more annuities and other retirement products.  (See also response to Q5.) 
 
    2. Addressing Bank Retrenchment. Especially post-Global Financial Crisis, banks hampered by tighter capital 
rules and liquidity constraints scaled back certain forms of corporate lending. Insurers, with capital bases and 
long-dated funding, can step into these gaps, enhancing the broader economy’s access to credit while optimizing 
their own risk-based capital usage. 
 
3.4.5 LONG TERM NATURE  
    1. Stability Through Economic Cycles.  Private credit often involves multi-year or multi-decade lending 
arrangements to finance infrastructure, real estate, and corporate expansion, sectors that benefit from “patient 
capital.”  The Issues Paper importantly notes that because insurers hold liabilities with similarly predictable, long -

dated cases, they can more comfortably bear the illiquidity and reduce forced selling during market stress.  
 
    2. Addresses Unpredictable Performances Declines. The structure and strategy of insurers’ investment 
portfolios, particularly in private credit, are designed to mitigate investment risks. Historical data shows that 
investment grade CLOs have experienced lower credit losses than equivalently rated corporate bonds across 
major financial downturns, including the dotcom bubble, the 2008-2009 Global Financial Crisis, and the COVID-
19 pandemic (Appendix, slides 21, 22). This resilience is due to the structural protections embedded in 
investment grade structured credit, such as credit enhancement, diversification, and mechanisms that prioritize 
cash flows to senior tranches during stress scenarios. Insurers operate with long -term, stable liabilities, which 
means they are not subject to the same liquidity pressures as banks; there is no maturity transformation, no 
access to the Federal Reserve’s discount window, and no reliance on government guarantees. This long-term 
funding model allows insurers to absorb temporary asset underperformance without being forced into premature 
reinvestment decisions. 
 
    3. Reduced Contagion Risk. Private debt funds are typically structured and managed in such a way that 
reduces the risk of contagion in the event of failure. These funds are significantly less leveraged than banks, 
typically under 1.5x compared to 10-12x for banks, reducing the potential for broader financial instability in case 
of asset failures. They are also organized as separate and distinct legal entities, which helps contain risk and 
protect investors even in the case of institutional distress.  
 
    4. Retirement Gap Solutions.  Many regions, including the U.S. and parts of Europe, face a sizeable pension 
or retirement savings shortfall.  In the U.S., 1/3 of pre-retirees are concerned about of money in retirement. 
Private credit strategies allow insurers to offer attractive guaranteed-income products supported by stable, long-
horizon returns that align well with policyholders’ lifetime income needs. 
 
3.4.6 INFLATION HEDGE  
Better Asset-Liability Management (ALM).  In inflationary or rising-rate environments, certain private credit 
vehicles (e.g., floating rate private loans, certain structured finance) can help insurers protect net interest 
margins, as coupons often reset higher. This can be more challenging in fixed coupon public bonds.  (See also 
response to Q5.) 
 
3.4.7 INVESTINF IN THE REAL ECONOMY 
    1. Mobilizing Capital for Growth. Perhaps the most direct benefit is the substantial flow of capital into 
businesses, infrastructure, real estate, and other segments of the “real economy.” As the IAIS has noted, stable,

well-supervised private credit helps fund SMEs, large corporates, housing markets, and development projects, 
sectors that drive job creation, innovation, and GDP growth. 
 
    2. Concrete Outcomes and Data 
        - U.S. Example: Since 2010, $2.5 trillion of new credit has been extended, fueling growth in industrial, 
consumer, and technology sectors, and catalyzing over $46 billion in incremental policyholder benefits.  
       - Global Industrial Renaissance: As recent IMF reports highlight, the global econom y may need over $75 
trillion in financing for energy, infrastructure, and digital expansion over the next decade. Private credit is well -
positioned to be a safe cornerstone source of that capital.

We recommend adding the narrowing of protection gaps to the list of benefits, which is perhaps the most 
prominent benefit associated with alternative assets and is not addressed in the Issues Paper. Many regions, 
including the U.S. and parts of Europe, face a sizeable pension or retirement savings shortfall. Life insurers (and 
pension funds) have the unique capacity to match the liquidity profiles of long-dated retirement liabilities with 
similarly illiquid, long-dated investments. Because the benefits of the illiquidity premium apply mostly to 
consumers, alternative investments help close these protection gaps.  
 
Section 3.4.1 Diversification: This should be enhanced by describing the potential for reduced systemic risk, as a 
broader set of long-term, stable funding sources (such as insurers and large institutional funds, in addition to 
banks) lower the overall vulnerability of the system to a single source of funding or liquidity runs.  
 
Section 3.4.2 Higher potential returns: This section can be enhanced by observing that many alternative assets 
offer an illiquidity premium, rewarding long-term investors such as insurers and pension funds that can match 
stable, illiquid liabilities with similarly long-dated, higher-yielding assets.    
 
Section 3.4.3 Market sophistication: This section can be enhanced by describing how private credit lenders tend 
to focus on the lending relationship (rather than potential cross-selling) and can tailor covenants, amortisation 
profiles, and drawdown schedules to match specific project needs. This fosters rigorous underwriting and 
deeper, ongoing relationships between borrower and lender.  
 
Section 3.4.4 Capital optimization: The Issues Paper suggests that insurers that focus “solely” on capital 
optimization may “overlook potential downsides for policyholders.” This statement is too strong and overlooks the 
reality that insurers manage with multiple objectives in mind and within multiple constraints. That said, life 
insurers’ liabilities often extend decades into the future, making them ideal holders of similarly long-duration, 
higher-yield assets. In many jurisdictions, solvency frameworks create capital incentives for insurers to match 
assets and liabilities. Such capital optimisation not only supports better returns but also fr ees capacity to 
underwrite more long-term products.  It's also important to note that many (re)insurers are highly rated by 
credible rating agencies. Accordingly, this section should be modified to recognize these realities and would 
benefit from a distinction between appropriate and inappropriate incentives. 
 
Section 3.4.5 Long-term nature: We support the Issues Paper’s view that alternative assets may align with the 
duration and cash flow characteristics of many insurer liabilities. For example, private credit often involves multi -
year or multi-decade lending arrangements to finance infrastructure, real estate, and corporate expansion —

sectors that benefit from “patient capital.” Although the Issues Paper asserts that alternative assets “may align 
well with insurers’ long-term liabilities,” it then claims that long-term assets “may decline unpredictability over 
long periods of time,” and if that were to happen, reinvestment risk “inevitably resurfaces.” This verbiage is 
inaccurate. Any long-term asset may “decline” over time, and it seems inaccurate to describe this dynamic as 
“reinvestment risk,” which is typically considered in the context of interest rates. We recommend removing this 
language. 
 
Section 3.4.6 Inflation hedge: This section could be enhanced by also referencing the inflation benefits of certain 
private credit arrangements, such as those in which coupons adjust with inflation, or floating rate private loans.  
 
Section 3.4.7 Investing in real economy: This discussion should be enhanced by explicitly mentioning 
infrastructure and by highlighting that, unlike banks reliant on demand deposits, insurers hold stable liabilities 
and can provide capital for a 10-, 20-, or even 30-year horizon. This reduces maturity mismatches and can be a 
countercyclical force when public markets or banks retreat from lending markets.

In section 3.5.5. (Page 26): “Credit rating agencies aim to remain independent and objective in their

Insurers typically rely on credit ratings to gauge the risk and value of their investments, especially in complex and 
less transparent assets. However, in the case of credit rating shopping, whereby issuers seek the most 
favourable ratings from various agencies, there could be potential risks posed by this type of activity. When 
issuers engage in credit rating shopping, it can lead to inflated ratings that do not accurately reflect the true risk 
of the asset.” 
With respect to the paragraphs above, while ‘credit rating shopping’ can take place, it may be useful to note that 
in most jurisdictions, only ratings from certain credit rating agencies are recognised, subject to them meeting the 
criteria set by the supervisors. Given so, credit rating agencies would also hold themselves to high standards to 
ensure that they continue to meet the standards set by the supervisors and remain recognised by the 
supervisors. This would also mitigate the risk of credit rating shopping. We propose that this point should be 
included within the section.

We understand the importance of considering liquidity risk from the liability side, particularly for life insurance and 
annuity contracts. These products inherently have a low probability of mass outflows due to features like 
surrender penalties, tax incentives, and underwriting frictions (such as medical exams for re-underwriting). 
Therefore, we believe that liquidity stress narratives developed for open-ended funds or daily dealing vehicles 
would not directly apply to life insurers. Insurers’ existing prudential practices already incorporate stress testing, 
with insurers typically maintaining buffers of high-quality liquid assets and conducting comprehensive cash flow 
and lapse stress testing under various jurisdictional rules. Any supervisory discussion o f liquidity should build 
upon these established frameworks rather than duplicating them. A holistic view that integrates liability 
Revisions done. 
Although 
including 
empirical 
examples from 
supervisory or 
market studies is 
outside the scope

optionality, collateral calls, and alternative asset sale assumptions provides a more accurate picture than 
standalone asset liquidity metrics. When such a framework is in place, holding some less liquid assets is not 
necessarily riskier; it can even help reduce reinvestment risk for long-dated liabilities. 
 
When considering investment groups, we believe it is important to clarify the scope beyond just private equity 
ownership. Alternative asset activity now encompasses a broad range of entities, including traditional insurers, 
pension fund managers, asset management subsidiaries of insurers, and diversified credit platforms. To avoid 
ambiguity, we suggest using a neutral term like "sponsor-controlled investment groups" and clearly defining it to 
distinguish between insurers owned by private equity sponsors or funds and those that may be affiliated with PE 
firms or simply allocate to externally managed private equity funds which can be affiliated or not. A primary 
supervisory concern in this area revolves around potential conflicts of interest. These issues can arise when a 
parent entity can influence strategic asset allocation towards affiliated funds, when investment time horizons or 
risk tolerances diverge from those required to honor policyholder promises, or when fee structures or internal 
ratings create information asymmetry. We believe existing supervisory frameworks can already address these 
concerns by focusing on look-through concentration limits and emphasizing independent board oversight and 
transparent governance, rather than solely on the corporate label of the sponsor.  
 
We recognize that the true vulnerability regarding alternative assets lies not in their intrinsic nature, but rather in 
their use by organizations that may lack the necessary skills, data, and governance to manage them effectively. 
This crucial point is already thoroughly addressed within existing supervisory principles.  
 
Our policy stance emphasises understanding and adaptation, rather than blunt restrictions. We believe that 
supervisory policy should encourage innovation that directs long-term savings into productive projects that 
benefit society. It should also set clear and proportionate risk management expectations, including 
comprehensive cash flow testing, liquidity stress testing, conflict of interest policies, and transparent disclosure. 
Furthermore, it is important to differentiate between well-managed and poorly managed exposures, rather than 
suggesting uniform quantitative caps. This approach allows for flexibility, avoiding penalties for jurisdictions with 
deep and liquid capital markets while still providing supervisors in less liquid markets the discretion to apply 
tighter guardrails where necessary. 
 
While section 3.5.1 addresses valuation challenges, we believe it would be strengthened by incorporating 
empirical examples of valuation conflicts from supervisory experience or market studies. We also think it's 
important to explore how regulatory incentives might influence valuation outcom es.

For section 3.5.4, we suggest renaming it to "Links to sponsor-controlled investment groups and associated

In section 3.5.4, concerning conflicts of interest, we feel the paper needs better balance. The suggestion that a

dynamic, is mitigated by existing risk management and regulatory incentives, such as capital charges for 
mismatched assets and liabilities. Similarly, optimizing Internal Rate of Return should not be seen as inherently 
problematic, as this metric considers regulatory capital, which, in an economic regime, accounts for the insurer's 
risk position, including asset-liability management. Concerns about capital extraction and 'early exits' should also 
be viewed within the context of regulatory safeguards. Disposal of a shareholder's stake would trigger a change -
of-control review, and private equity-backed reinsurers generally lack an economic incentive to liquidate assets 
backing long-dated liabilities. Significant portfolio sales are already subject to supervisory scrutiny, and 
supervisors have the authority to restrict dividend distributions and review intra -group fees, further safeguarding 
against excessive upstreaming of funds.

“Alternative” assets, when properly structured and supervised, are match -funded, investment grade, and long-
term in nature. Regulatory frameworks should reflect the actual risk profile and avoid treatments that are not 
supported by data or analysis. Holistic supervision of “alternative” assets is critical – the IAIS must consider both 
sides of the balance sheet. This approach ensures that the regulatory treatment aligns with the true risk 
characteristics of the assets and liabilities, thereby promoting financial stability and resilience. 
 
I. Comment on Section 3.5.3. Liquidity Risks 
Revision done.

Section 3.5.3 claims that “optionality embedded in policies could prompt policyholders to surrender their policies 
in search of better yields” in response to substantial increases in interest rates. The statement is accompanied by 
footnote 44, noting that “At the same time, features of most life insurance and annuity products make it less likely 
for a large-scale run scenario to occur, because surrender charges, losing a tax advantage, and having to repeat 
the underwriting process all limit the likelihood of lapses.” ACLI recommends elevating footnote 44 to the full text 
body in Section 3.5.3. 
 
Footnote 44 is an objectively true statement that adds balance to the supposition that optionality embedded in 
policies could prompt policyholders to surrender their policies in search of better yields.  
 
Section 3.5.3 presumes that liquidity risk on the liability side can become “significant” with unexpected 
surrenders or lapses. ACLI recommends including language from Section 5.1 into Section 3.5.3. Section 5.1 
acknowledges that the “impact of policyholder behaviour may vary depending on jurisdictional differences with 
regard to regulatory frameworks and liquidity in combination with local characteristics in terms of overall insurer 
allocations.” (Section 5.1)  
 
For example, in the U.S., all life insurers must maintain sufficient capital to weather liquidity and other shocks to 
their finances. In 2020, the NAIC implemented a regulatory liquidity stress-testing framework (LST) for large life 
insurers that meet certain characteristics. The LST considers the interplay between assets and liabilities and 
incorporates expert assumptions on critical issues, including lapse assumptions under a variety of stress 
scenarios. The LST is designed to assess two critical issues: whether individual life insurers have sufficient 
liquidity under stress and whether insurer asset sales under stress would lead to market disruptions, aka “fire 
sales.”  
 
In addition, the structural design and features built into U.S. insurance products, which have long-term liabilities 
such as annuities and pension obligations, makes a run scenario highly unlikely and lessens the likelihood of 
forced asset fire sales during stress events. 
 
Even if a stress event occurs, life insurers may change to a new investment strategy, but they are unlikely to sell 
a substantial amount of existing assets in a stressed environment over a short time period.  Additionally, life 
insurers have strategic asset allocations to highly liquid investments such as treasuries and public investment 
grade credit that would be tapped first to be sold in a stress event.

The issues paper criticizes amortized cost valuation, which again has the appearance of being targeted at the 
US market. With respect to fair value versus amortized cost valuation, it is important to recognize that insurer 
allocations to less liquid investments are generally less exposed to short-term market volatility, given the role 
they play within insurer ALM strategies – i.e., backing less liquid, longer duration liabilities (as discussed further 
below). Furthermore, the IAIS should recognize that these investments typically generate regular cash interest 
payments and principal repayments, which are driven by credit performance rather than fluctuations in market 
pricing. Amortized cost accounting better captures their inherently stable, long -term character while still 
incorporating provisions for credit impairments that are not temporary. Additionally, unlike public markets where 
bonds are standardized and offer limited flexibility, private credit transactions allow lenders to customize 
covenants, amortization structures, and drawdown terms to align with the specific needs of that transaction. This 
bespoke approach encourages more meaningful and durable relationships between borrowers and lenders.  
 
Regarding transparency and timing of valuation, we would note that private credit investors typically receive 
comprehensive monthly reporting packages that include detailed information on each loan held within a fund. 
These reports typically provide key data on borrower financial performance, covenant compliance, payment 
status, collateral valuations, and risk ratings, along with portfolio-level metrics such as exposure by sector, 
maturity, and credit quality. Unlike public markets, where investors primarily rely on daily pricing that may reflect 
short-term sentiment or liquidity distortions, the granular, borrower-specific data in private credit reporting offers 
deeper insights into the underlying credit fundamentals and risk profile of the portfolio. This level of transparency 
enables more informed, forward-looking risk assessments and supports robust internal oversight and 
governance. Figure 37 of the ACC's Financing the Economy report provides a good summary of the frequency of 
private credit reporting to investors:

leverage between bank lending and private credit lending. As noted in Figure 32 of the 2024 ACC Financing the

figures indicate that over 80% of private credit funds maintain no to very conservative leverage practices. 
Notably, these leverage levels have remained consistent over the past decade, even as assets under 
management and capital deployment volumes have increased. This stability in levera ge usage underscores a 
cautious and disciplined approach within the private credit industry. 
 
With respect to liquidity concerns regarding an insurer's holding of Illiquid assets, when integrated into a well -
managed portfolio, longer-term assets contribute meaningfully to both best practice in ALM and retirement 
security. Insurers are particularly well matched to these assets due to their long-dated liability structures, which 
align naturally with long-horizon investments. We believe the paper should recognize the importance of overall 
liquidity risk management across an insurer's entire portfolio, and frame and consider potential risks associated 
with increased allocations to alternative assets through this broader lens versus its current narrow emphasis on  
specific asset-level concerns. Jurisdictions like the United States and Bermuda impose stringent stress testing 
requirements that model liquidity positions as a whole under severe scenarios. Thus far, neither jurisdiction has 
determined there are any concerns with their respective markets, and both jurisdictions wield ample supervisory 
tools to address concerns in the future without needing amendments to the ICPs, or local law.  
 
Life insurers' liabilities—such as annuities with surrender charges and pension obligations—are typically long-
term and stable, distinguishing them from bank deposit bases that are vulnerable to rapid withdrawals. Insurers 
do not face daily redemption risk, and even during periods of extreme market stress, lapse rates have remained 
manageable. Historical experience reinforces this resilience. Across major financial crises, including the 2008 
Global Financial Crisis and the COVID-19 pandemic, insurers largely avoided asset fire sales, illustrating that 
their funding model is inherently stable and capable of managing liquidity risk effectively. Lastly, we would note 
that even during the Great Depression in the 1930s, life insurers did not experience significant outsized lapses, 
and industry-wide surrender rates would have needed to be much higher to trigger industry-wide significant asset 
sales. 
 
Both these historical precedents and the aforementioned portfolio-wide liquidity assessments consistently 
demonstrate that insurers can and are maintaining sufficient liquidity even under challenging market conditions.  
 
Regarding NAV lending, we disagree with the assertion that "the origination of a NAV loan traditionally indicates 
that a portfolio company or companies are underperforming." This statement does not accurately reflect the 
economic realities of the private credit market. For many private credit firms, NAV lending serves as a permanent

structural component of their strategy and is utilized irrespective of portfolio performance.  
 
Regarding credit risk and ratings, we would note that contrary to many assumptions, the vast majority of private 
credit held on insurance balance sheets is investment-grade or near-investment-grade. In the US, private credit 
holdings are publicly disclosed in statutory filings, and private letter ratings are submitted to supervisors for 
review. Bermuda is implementing new rules to require public disclosure of assets, which will be effective at year-
end 2025. 
Everlake Life, US 
As noted above, rather than attempting to develop a list of assets of concern and attaching a label to such a list, 
we suggest focusing on the asset characteristics of concern (e.g., liquidity, valuation uncertainty, etc.) and how 
those risks are managed by the life insurance industry and then defining a scope based on those characteristics.  
And as the IAIS well understands, any asset characteristics should also be evaluated relative to the associated 
liabilities. 
 
The Consultation would be improved if it developed the concept that the so-called “Alternative” assets, when 
properly risk managed structured and supervised, are often investment grade and long -term in nature. 
Regulatory frameworks should reflect the actual risk profile and avoid treatments that are not supported by data 
or analysis. Holistic supervision of the risk characteristic of all assets - regardless of label - is critical.  In order to 
do this, the Consultation the IAIS must consider both sides of the balance sheet and the life insurance r isk 
management and governance for all assets. This approach ensures that the regulatory treatment aligns with the 
true risk characteristics of the assets and liabilities, thereby promoting financial stability and resilience.

Athene, USA 
As public markets become more concentrated and private markets play a rising role in global finance, life 
insurers are well-positioned to harness the illiquidity premium due to their long-dated, predictable liabilities. The 
IAIS’s concerns about valuation, hidden leverage, liquidity, conflicts of interest, and information gaps can be – 
and in many cases are being – addressed by existing supervisory frameworks, governance, and transparent 
disclosures. By recognizing that “private” does not automatically equate to “higher risk” and embracing the 
nuanced supervisory mechanisms in place and being developed in the U.S. and Bermuda, the IAIS can foster a 
balanced view that acknowledges private credit’s stability benefits, its role in addressing global retirement n eeds, 
and its capacity to fund real-economy growth. 
 
ADDRESSING CONCERNS AROUND PRIVATE CREDIT 
Regulators have highlighted potential risks, including insufficient disclosures, leveraged exposures, conflict -of-
interest concerns, and illiquid holdings. These risks can be effectively supervised through: 
1. Strong governance and internal controls. Leading private-credit managers follow effective policies for conflict 
checks, trading oversight, and fair allocations among client funds. 
Revisions made. 
Inputs could be 
also considered 
for further 
analysis.

2. Third-party valuations and frequent audits. Valuation risk can be mitigated by recognized pricing models, third -
party appraisals, and transparent assumptions. 
3. Tailored capital requirements. The largest private funds are often used by insurers, which themselves face 
strict solvency oversight by insurance regulators. This capital structure fosters prudent credit investments rather 
than unregulated risk-taking. 
 
3.5.1 VALUATION 
Athene, USA’s experience and recent supervisory findings demonstrate that valuation of illiquid assets can be 
appropriately managed with effective governance and procedures.  As a backdrop, many private assets, 
including those originated by non-banks, are distributed across multiple large, sophisticated investors. Valuation 
teams across these investors observe industry-wide valuation practices and marks and validate via third party 
valuation services. As investment grade private credit assets become more widely held ( as has been occurring 
over time), valuation dispersion across firms will continue to naturally decrease.  (Appendix, slide 11).  
 
At the firm-level, in our experience, strong valuation procedures, including requisite independence and third -party 
assurance, produce consistent and reliable valuations. Private asset pricing is supported by full access to 
management and detailed private information – frequent and in-depth company reporting and monitoring is often 
required via stricter covenants. In addition, valuations are often provided or reviewed by third-party pricing 
services, as well as by external auditors on a recurring basis. Such procedures prevent overreliance on broker 
quotes, which may not necessarily reflect the fundamental value, market value or de pth of an asset, potentially 
creating “false security” for holders and policymakers.  
 
The UK FCA recently evaluated valuation practices of private market participants and highlighted general good 
practice in the summary of its findings:  
 
“We were encouraged to find many examples of good practice in firms' valuation processes, including the quality 
of reporting to investors, documenting valuations, using third-party valuation advisers to introduce additional 
independence and expertise, and consistent application of established valuation methodologies. Generally, firms 
recognized the importance of maintaining robust processes. We saw practices that reflected consideration of 
investor protections given the judgement required and risks present in valuing priva te assets.” 
 
While we acknowledge that private assets do not have daily exchange pricing, the existing governance 
frameworks and independent valuation services mitigate the valuation risk described by the IAIS. We suggest 
that the Issues Paper be revised to include the following mitigants as part of its discussion of concerns:

1. Entity-Level Liquidity Measurement. The insurer’s liquidity is what matters, not the liquidity of any particular 
asset. Liquidity should therefore be measured at the entity level, not the asset level.  
 
Athene, USA’s liabilities are protected against sudden liquidity shocks:  
     - 85% of Athene, USA’s liabilities are non-surrenderable or carry a surrender charge. 
     - Insurers like Athene, USA and other “buy and hold” investors like pensions are market stabilizers.  
     - Bermuda reinsurers like Athene, USA’s maintain high solvency ratios and liquidity even under adverse 
conditions, buffering potential shocks. 
 
    2. Rigorous Valuation Models. Private credit and securitized assets are typically valued through internal 
models (e.g., Discounted Cash Flow), supplemented by independent pricing services and tested by external 
auditors. 
 
    3. Information-Rich Process. Unlike public equities, where valuations often hinge on sometimes -volatile market 
prices, private credit valuations benefit from direct access to borrower data, covenants, and management 
insights. 
 
    4. Long-Term Liability Matching. Insurers, particularly those with stable liabilities, are less exposed to short -
term pricing volatility. A bank may mark assets to market daily for liquidity purposes; insurers generally hold 
assets to maturity under asset-liability matching (ALM) frameworks. 
 
    5. Scenario-Based Approach. Bermuda’s Scenario-Based Approach (SBA) similarly uses forward-looking 
models that incorporate extensive credit and cash flow assumptions, consistent with fundamental valuation 
strategies in multiple jurisdictions. 
 
    6. Mitigated Liquidity Risk. Because insurers’ liabilities are predictably long -term, forced sales under stress are 
less probable. As a result, any discount from “mark-to-market” conditions is less likely to threaten solvency. 
Athene, USA 
3.5.2 HIDDEN LEVERAGE  
While certain private strategies do employ leverage, current industry practices (underwriting, reformed 
securitization structures, and prudent regulatory oversight) substantially reduce the likelihood that “hidden” 
leverage leads to systemic fragility. 
 
    1. Limited Leverage Utilization. At Apollo, for example, most private credit funds operate at or near 1.2x

leverage – significantly less than the leverage typically present in the banking sector. Similarly, Athene, USA’s 
adjusted debt-to-capital ratio is comparatively conservative to peers. 
 
    2. Post-2008 Evolution. Modern private credit and structured vehicles, such as CLO 2.0/3.0 structures, have 
over-collateralization, less layering of leverage, and clearer risk tranching than pre -Global Financial Crisis 
securitizations. 
 
    3. Strong Underwriting. Detailed diligence, lender protections, and carefully neg otiated covenants help contain 
risk. Private credit managers often maintain direct borrower relationships and active monitoring, facilitating early 
identification of stress. 
 
    4. Transparent Regulatory Oversight. In many jurisdictions, capital rules (e.g., RBC in the U.S.) oblige insurers 
to stress test their investments, thereby capturing the potential impact of leverage on solvency.  
Athene, USA 
3.5.3 LIQUIDITY RISKS  
Illiquid assets, as part of a properly risk-managed portfolio, support financial stability and retirement security. The 
illiquidity premium is intentionally harvested by insurers whose liability structures naturally align with long -horizon 
investments. In addition, regulatory frameworks ensure liquidity buffers and stress testing that guard against 
short-term volatility. (See also response to Q5.) 
 
    1. Long-Dated, Predictable Liabilities. Life insurers’ stable liability profile (e.g., annuities with surrender 
charges, pensions) fundamentally differs from bank deposit bases. Insurers do not face daily redemptions akin to 
bank runs. Any historical industry lapses have been highly manageable – even the most troubled insurers during 
the Global Financial Crisis did not experience substantial lapses and would need twice the surrenders in the 
worst year for the U.S. industry to need to sell assets. (GAO, Impacts of and Regulatory Response to the 2007 -
2009 Financial Crisis, June 2013). 
 
    2. Stress Testing. Jurisdictions like the U.S. and Bermuda require stress testing of coverage ratios under 
adverse scenarios. Results typically show ample liquidity, even if stress events prompt higher -than-expected 
lapses.

3.5.4 LINKS TO PE FIRMS & CONFLICTS OF INTEREST  
Affiliated relationships between life insurers and asset managers are not limited to U.S. “PE -affiliated” 
companies. They exist across the insurance sector and on a global level. These relationship structures have 
been common in the insurance industry for decades (e.g., Allianz/PIMCO; Athene, USA/Apollo; 
MassMutual/Barings; MetLife, United States/Met Life Investment Management; Prudential/PGIM; 
Noted, and will 
be considered in 
future work eg an 
Application 
Paper.

Equitable/Alliance Bernstein).  (See Appendix, slide 6). Any potential conflicts in the relationship are managed 
via transparent governance, independent committees, and thorough regulatory reviews. Use of “PE affiliation” 
masks a broader trend of increasingly sophisticated insurer asset management arms, which mitigates risk; 
indeed, it can bring substantial expertise and scale in private asset origination.   
 
   1. Strict Investment Guidelines: Most insurer asset management mandates include constraints on c redit 
quality, leverage, and concentration. Independent boards, risk committees, and external auditors oversee 
compliance. 
 
   2. Regulatory Pre-Approvals: In the U.S., affiliated transaction statutes require approval for affiliated 
transactions and provide guidelines for regulator review.  Bermuda’s BMA similarly reviews material related -party 
deals, further reducing conflict potential. 
 
   3. Alignment of Interests: Where affiliates co-invest, the risk appetite is aligned to ensure policyholder and 
shareholder interests are protected.  
Athene, USA 
3.5.5 CREDIT RISK AND RATINGS  
While vigilance is warranted, concerns regarding “higher credit risk” are not uniformly applicable. Adequate 
regulations and best-in-class underwriting practices ensure private credit risk profiles can be as transparent – 
and in some cases more resilient – than certain public debt classes. 
 
    1. Predominance of Investment Grade: Contrary to assumptions that “private” equates to “junk,” most of the 
private credit in insurer portfolios is investment grade or near investment grade. Today, U.S. insurer bond 
portfolios are ~44% private bonds and ABS and ~95% investment grade overall. As insurer allocations to 
“private” bonds and ABS have increased, there has been no material change (and even incremental 
improvement) in the investment grade percentage. (Appendix, slide 7, 8). 
 
    2. Rigorous Underwriting Standards: Detailed diligence, strong borrower relationships, and covenants lower 
default probabilities relative to superficially similar public market high-yield exposures. 
 
    3. Well-Established Supervision: The U.S. and Bermuda, for example, use solvency and RBC regimes that 
continue to evolve, requiring extensive data submission, risk calibrations and stress scenario analysis.  
 
    4. Transparent Reporting and Governance: Private credit holdings are disclosed under statutory filings. 
Insurers also are required to develop an ORSA (Own Risk and Solvency Assessment) to assess their risk 
Noted for future 
work.

management and solvency, which summarizes how credit and liquidity risks are governed at the board and 
committee levels. 
Athene, USA 
3.5.6 REGULAOTRY CAPITAL-RELATED ISSUES Although not fully detailed in the Issues Paper, capital 
treatment can heavily influence insurer participation in private credit. Many jurisdictions already incorporate 
RBC/solvency frameworks (e.g., NAIC in the U.S., BMA in Bermuda) that require insurers to hold capital 
commensurate with asset risk. These frameworks undergo ongoing refinement to reflect actual loss experience 
in private credit, ensuring capital charges align with historical performance and prospecti ve stress tests.A 
cornerstone of sound capital policy is the principle of “equal capital for equal risk”  -- the notion that two assets 
bearing the same economic risk characteristics (credit quality, duration, liquidity, interest rate sensitivity, and 
asset class type or structure) should attract the same capital charge, regardless of whether they sit in public 
markets or private credit funds, or whether they’re labelled “alternative.” Equal capital for equal risk ensures 
regulation does not misdirect or interfere with capital support for long-dated liabilities that address the protection 
gap.The NAIC’s Risk-Based Capital Model Governance (RBC EX) Task Force has formally adopted this principle 
among its core charges, pledging to “further the principle of ‘Equal Capital for Equal Risk’” by calibrating RBC 
factors to observed loss experience and adjusting them only where empirical evidence warrants. (NAIC News 
Release, Feb. 2025).  This demonstrates a true principles-based response to evolving insurer investment 
practices: rather than erecting new capital surcharges for private credit, the RBC EX Task Force commits to an 
ongoing “monitor/collect/assess” cycle – reviewing actual loss data, stress-test outcomes, and market 
developments before proposing any changes.The IAIS may wish to consider a comparable framework in 
reviewing supervisory materials:    1. Classify assets by shared economic risks such as credit quality, duration, 
liquidity, interest rate sensitivity, and asset class type (or structure) – which will give supervisors sharper insight 
than ambiguous legacy labels;    2. Preserve flexibility in Insurance Core Principles and ComFrame to allow 
jurisdictions to reflect local markets, subject to a common “equal capital for equal risk” yardstick;     3. Defer any 
formal revisions until after a targeted evidence-gathering phase, leveraging case studies and jurisdictional 
benchmarks; and    4. Calibrate narrowly tailored enhancements only where genuine divergences between 
capital charges and loss experience emerge.By aligning capital requirements with this principle, the IAIS can 
promote consistent, risk-sensitive treatment of alternative investments globally – supporting sound asset-liability 
management without unduly penalizing insurers that seek to capture illiqu idity premia and diversify funding 
sources.

3.5.7 INCREASED COMPLEXITIES IN MANAGEMENT  
As private credit instruments can be more specialized (e.g., structured finance, infrastructure debt), insurers 
require expertise to manage these exposures effectively. However, the growth of third -party administrators, 
specialized asset managers, and internal risk functions demonstrates that these complexities are being 
addressed through dedicated resources, transparent mandates, and advanced risk analytics.

3.5.8 INFORMATION GAPS 
The premise that private-market assets sit in a “black box” misinterprets the evidence. Documents, regulators, 
and investors already receive a wide lattice of valuation and performance metrics across multiple reporting 
regimes – just not always through the screenshots and call–report templates familiar to banking supervisors.  
(Managed Funds Association, Private Credit Data: Readily Available and Fit for Purpose, Oct. 17, 2024).  
 
    1. Layered regulatory filings. 
       - Insurers: quarterly statutory statements, 7500-plus pages in Athene, USA’s case, give position-level detail 
on every asset, plus RBC, ORSA, and Form F analytics. 
       - Private-fund advisers: Form PF, Form ADV and, where applicable, 10-K/10-Q furnish holdings, leverage, 
liquidity, and counterparty data that the U.S. Financial Stability Oversight Council and the Securities and 
Exchange Commission use for systemic-risk surveillance. 
       - Banks & counterparties: call reports and forthcoming FR Y-14 enhancements map exposures to non-bank 
lenders. 
       - State regimes: lending-license and UCC filings add borrower-level collateral detail. 
 
    2. Valuation and audit discipline. Private credit managers must mark assets under recognized accounting 
standards, subject to third-party audits and, for insurers, annual actuarial opinions. These valuations feed both 
public disclosures and supervisory capital models. 
 
    3. Commercial and academic datasets. Debtwire, PitchBook, Preqin and others aggregate loan -level terms, 
performance, and borrower information that Federal Reserve economists and market participants routinely 
analyze. 
 
In short, transparency does exist – it is simply organized for the economics of long-dated, non-depository 
finance. Before creating new data mandates, the IAIS could add greater value by:  
    1. Mapping and reconciling the significant disclosures already produced under insurance, securities, and state 
regimes – exactly the “consolidate and reconcile” exercise recommended by MFA and industry stakeho lders. 
    2. Focusing on comparability, not quantity will highlight that common risk -taxonomy tags (credit quality, 
duration, collateral seniority) will harmonize cross-border analysis without forcing bank-style templates onto 
fundamentally different balance-sheet models. 
    3. Leveraging existing third-party datasets to triangulate performance, rather than duplicating collection efforts.

By acknowledging the breadth of current reporting and enhancing cross-agency data-sharing, the IAIS can close 
any remaining “information gaps” while preserving the principle-based, proportional supervision it champions.

Balancing Risks, Opportunities, and Regulatory Perceptions of Alternative Assets - Hidden Leverage 
 
As alternative assets continue to play an increasingly significant role in global capital markets, it is critical that 
policymakers and supervisory authorities strike a balanced approach in assessing the associated risks and 
opportunities. While the Draft Issues Paper recognizes that “alternative assets offer benefits such as 
diversification, higher potential returns, and alignment with long-term liabilities,”  it also risks relying on 
Revision done. 
The paper 
discusses the 
strengths and 
limitations of both 
in-house and

misperceptions of alternative assets in arriving at its conclusions and recommendations. We take this opportunity 
to provide an additional perspective on the role of alternative assets, the perceived risks associated with them, 
and how those risks are managed.  
 
Addressing Misperceptions of NAV Loans. The Draft Issues Paper also emphasizes potential drivers for net 
asset value (“NAV”) loans that raise concerns about interconnected risk (see Draft Issues Paper, page 21) while 
overlooking the valuable role they play, including the benefits NAV loans offer to investors. In fact, the drivers for 
NAV loans are varied and are not limited to addressing portfolio underperformance as the Draft Issues Paper 
might suggest. (See NAVigating the Resilience of NAV Loans, KBRA, UNITED STATES Research Report 
(February 2, 2023), available at https://www.KBRA, United States.com/publications/tSFZXXsw/navigating-the-
resilience-of-nav-loans#6e67cc00-7901-4352-bfca-0b1bc2ff89bf). For instance, NAV loans are used to increase 
investment capacity. Buyout managers may use NAV loans to make follow-on investments into existing portfolio 
companies instead of additional capital calls or to the extent capital calls have been exhausted. Similarly, 
secondaries managers may use NAV loans to help finance the purchase of additional LP interests. These 
transactions are also structured with various protections that would deleverage the loans in the event of NAV 
deterioration, asset concentration, and passage of time. (See Private Credit: NAVigating the PE Landscape—
NAV Loans Still Perform, KBRA, UNITED STATES Research Report (October 31, 2024), available at 
https://www.KBRA, United States.com/publications/qHMNTvyP/private-credit-navigating-the-pe-landscape-nav-
loans-still-perform).  For investors, NAV loans can support overall fund performance by enhancing liquidity and 
providing capital flexibility to the asset manager. From the perspective of an insurance company, NAV loans can 
offer an attractive means of diversifying credit risk, while also providing a duration profile that aligns well with 
long-term liabilities. We would encourage the IAIS to take a broader view of NAV loans in its forthcoming work.  
 
Hidden Leverage is Not Unique to Alternative Assets. Disclosure is a critical mechanism to address concerns 
related to hidden leverage and alternative assets, but it is important to note that hidden leverage is not a unique 
feature of alternative assets. The Draft Issues Paper emphasizes that exposure to leverage can be hidden within 
private credit funds or insurers’ investment portfolios in various ways, specifically highlighting that any investment 
funds or ABS with pooled credit for assets (e.g., credit funds, real estate debt funds, and CLOs) may have 
underlying borrowers with leverage and financial conditions that can be difficult for supervisors, policyholders, 
market participants and insurers to fully appreciate. We note this is also true for traditional assets that carry 
embedded leverage (e.g., repos, derivatives).  
 
While ongoing concerns regarding hidden leverage should certainly be addressed, it is also helpful to recognize 
that meaningful measures and tools are already in place to mitigate these risks. For example, regulatory

requirements call for disclosures to be made to investors and supervisory authorities (e.g., disclosure required 
under the Alternative Investment Fund Managers Directive (“AIFMD”) in the European Union and disclosure 
required on Schedule BA of the National Association of Insurance Commissioners in the United States), while 
CRA analytical frameworks account for hidden leverage and evaluate investment management infrastructure and 
oversight. (See e.g., KBRA, UNITED STATES Investment Funds Global Rating Methodology (March 12, 2020), 
available at https://www.KBRA, United States.com/publications/xhzHSVYj/funds-investment-funds-global-rating-
methodology?format=file; KBRA, UNITED STATES Investment Fund Debt Global Rating Methodology (March 
12, 2020), available at https://www.KBRA, United States.com/publications/ynQjbNcN/funds-investment-fund-
debt-global-rating-methodology?format=file; and KBRA, UNITED STATES Financial Institutions: Asset Manager 
Global Rating Methodology (December 28, 2020), available at https://www.KBRA, United 
States.com/publications/RxjnyjbY/financial-institutions-asset-manager-global-rating-methodology?format=file).  
For example, KBRA, UNITED STATES credit ratings of NAV loans incorporate the total net and gross leverage 
within a portfolio of assets that secure the relevant NAV loan. Nevertheless, we agree that enhanced disclosure 
standards could be a useful tool to further mitigate risks.   
 
Life Insurers Focus on Management of Risks. The Draft Issues Paper highlights the potential risks associat ed 
with alternative assets but does not note the range of tools and governance structures applied by insurers to 
mitigate the risks. In particular, the Draft Issues Paper raises concerns with respect to valuation uncertainty, 
illiquidity, hidden leverage, and complexity. However, we have observed that the majority of insurers manage 
these exposures using robust internal credit models, stress testing, and comprehensive enterprise risk 
management frameworks, frequently reflecting existing solvency framework r equirements.  
 
For instance, valuation practices while not 100% uniform, generally benefit from rigorous independent review by 
qualified third-party firms with specialized expertise in less liquid asset classes. With respect to illiquidity, it is 
important to note that life/annuity insurers are by necessity “buy and hold” investors with both the intent and 
ability to hold assets for an extended timeframe. In addition, insurers employ stress testing tools that incorporate 
a broad range of macroeconomic scenarios and policyholder behaviors to guide strategic and tactical 
management of cash and liquidity positions to minimize the likelihood of becoming forced sellers – especially of 
illiquid assets – except in the most extreme scenarios.  
 
As the IAIS continues to assess the role of alternative assets in connection with broader structural shifts within 
the life insurance sector and potential areas for enhancement in supervisory material, we encourage the IAIS to 
consider the existing tools and governance structures applied by insurers to mitigate the risks.

Kroll Bond Rating 
Agency (KBRA, 
UNITED STATES) 
Balancing Risks, Opportunities, and Regulatory Perceptions of Alternative Assets - The Role of Credit Ratings in 
Alternative Assets 
 
Credit ratings play an important role in debt markets by providing investors with independent opinions on 
creditworthiness based on rigorous and transparent qualitative and quantitative analysis. Credit ratings serve to 
reduce information asymmetry in the market and help investors evaluate risk, including with respect to 
investments in alternative assets. As observed in the Draft Issues Paper, credit ratings provide investors in 
alternative assets with insights into the quality of the underlying assets and the structural protections in place.  
 
Investors also benefit from the diversity of opinion that different CRAs provide to the market. The Draft Issues 
Paper seems to suggest that subjectivity and differences in credit ratings outcome between different CRAs could 
be a drawback and indicative of CRA reliance on issuers where information might not be publicly available. We 
would suggest taking an alternate and more positive view. Differences in credit ratings outcomes among CRAs 
reflect analytical judgment and different methodological approaches, and investors benefit from these diverse 
perspectives on credit risk. Further, to the extent there are concerns about herding behavior and concentration 
risk in these portfolios of investments, multiple perspectives on credit risk serve to mitigate those risks.  
 
At KBRA, UNITED STATES, all credit ratings, whether public or private, utilize the same publicly available 
methodologies, rigorous processes, and analytical procedures including credit rating committees, detailed credit 
rating reports, and information disclosure forms. The only distinction between public and private credit ratings is 
how the credit rating and related information are distributed. Reports for private credit ratings are unpublished 
and distributed via controlled channels. These private reports, whether for new issuance or surveillance, provide 
investors and stakeholders with insight into how KBRA, UNITED STATES arrives at its conclusions and 
determines its credit ratings. They allow users to benchmark and compare the subject issuer or transaction 
against publicly rated issuers and instruments. KBRA, UNITED STATES distributes these materials via a virtual 
data room (“VDR”) that includes version control, audit trails, and confidentiality protections, ensuring secure and 
transparent communication with authorized users. In addition, KBRA, UNITED STATES’s annual report of credit 
ratings transition and stability makes no distinction between public or private credit ratings and the data show 
remarkably stable credit ratings across all sectors. 
 
KBRA, UNITED STATES brings transparency to the private credit market, having published more than 35 private 
credit research pieces on a wide range of topics in the last 12 months, including on NAV loans and feeder funds. 
These publications support market understanding of complex and emerging asset classes and demonstrate 
KBRA, UNITED STATES’s commitment to advancing credit risk transparency. A listing of these research reports,

As noted in the Draft Issues Paper, CRAs are subject to comprehensive regulatory oversight in many 
jurisdictions as well as global standards established by the International Organization of Insurance Commissions 
(“IOSCO”). Among other things, these regulations and standards promote quality and integrity in the credit 
ratings process, independence, and avoidance of conflicts of interest, transparency, and protection of 
confidential information. These core principles apply to all credit ratings, regardless of sector or asset class. The 
risk of rating shopping or the potential for conflicts of interest are no greater in alternative assets than in 
traditional asset classes, nor are the regulatory requirements, standards or measures any different to guard 
against them. We discourage suggestions to the contrary.

Bermuda 
International Long 
Term Insurers and 
Reinsurers 
(BILTIR), Bermuda 
We believe the concerns outlined in this section are already appropriately addressed by certain jurisdictions’ 
supervisory regimes, including Bermuda’s.  We expand on this point below: Section 3.5.1 Valuation: While some 
of these concerns have merit, it is possible to address them effectively. For example, private credit and 
securitized assets, while lacking market prices, are typically valued through robust internal discounted cash flow  
models, supplemented by independent pricing services and external auditors. These valuations can sometimes 
benefit from direct access to borrower data, covenants, and management insights. Moreover, the prudent person 
principle, which is mandated in many jurisdictions, including Bermuda, requires insurers to develop a sound  
governance and control framework around their investments, including the valuation of such. One concern 
mentioned in this section is the potential for forced selling and market dislocations. The Oliver Wyman Analysis 
of Systemic Risk in the Bermuda Long-Term Insurance Sector (“OW Analysis”) explored a scenario in which a 
confidence shock leads to mass lapses and potential asset sales. It concluded that Bermudian long -term insurers 
hold sufficient liquid assets to meet unexpected liquidity demands, even in a severe liquidity stress. 
Approximately 30% of insurance reserves in the Bermuda market are composed of products that do not allow for 
withdrawal, while the remaining 70% often have structural features that either discourage surrender or lengthen 
the timing of liquidity demands. The Bermuda Monetary Authority’s (BMA) liquidity stress testing shows that the 
industry’s median post-stress liquidity coverage ratio is 360%, with the stresses exceeding historical experience 
for excess lapses.Section 3.5.2 Hidden leverage: We agree that leverage sometimes exists, and, where it does, 
it should be reflected in regulatory risk charges and in risk management. While certain private strategies employ 
leverage, current industry practices (underwriting, securitisation structure, governance) reduce the likelihood that

acknowledges 
that insurers and 
fund managers 
employ various 
valuation 
approaches 
based on internal 
processes and 
asset type. This 
section aims to 
highlight 
supervisory 
concerns and

gaps in the

“hidden” leverage could have systemic impacts. Section 3.5.3 Liquidity risks: As always, liquidity should be 
assessed within a comprehensive, holistic ALM framework. In addition, retail insurance products have been 
shown to be relatively immune from bank runs. While many insurers are increasing allocat ions to alternative 
assets to take additional illiquidity risk, this concern can be appropriately addressed by effective liquidity risk 
management practices, such as those currently required by the BMA.We support the Issues Paper’s observation 
that the growth of alternative asset investments across the insurance sector has heightened the importance of 
liquidity risk. The OW Analysis points out that the allocations to alternative assets are higher in the Eur opean 
Union than in Bermuda or the United States, increasing from 39% to 51% between 2019 and 2023. We believe 
the Issues Paper should characterize concerns about liquidity as microprudential and not macroprudential 
(financial stability-related).  The possibility that multiple insurers would have near-simultaneous liquidity-based 
failures, leading to real economy impacts, is extremely remote.Section 3.5.4 Links to PE firms and potential 
conflicts of interest: This section seems to be more about PE ownership than alternative investments, so this 
section is oddly positioned in the Issues Paper. Affiliated insurer–asset-manager models are long-standing (e.g., 
PIMCO/Allianz) and operate under strict board-level conflict committees, affiliate-transaction statutes and BMA 
pre-approval. Nevertheless, we believe this section would benefit from a more balanced approach, including 
identification of existing supervisory tools that ameliorate the concerns. For example, the Issues Paper suggests 
that a PE firm might structure assets originated by the firm for a time horizon that is not necessarily optimal for 
the insurer. Even if this dynamic exists, however, it is countered by the BMA’s supervisory approval 
requirements, single fund risk limits, counterparty risk limits, governance around affiliated transactions, and 
regulatory capital charges for mismatched assets and liabilities. Under risk appetite, the Issues Paper states, 
without providing evidence, that “[i]n many cases the investment strategies for PE firms are based around 
optimization of Internal Rate of Return…as opposed to also taking the benefits of longer durations to ma tch 
liabilities into account.” Most insurer asset management mandates include constraints on credit quality, leverage, 
and concentration, with various boards, risk committees and auditors ensuring compliance. We recommend 
incorporating references to these common insurer practices or removing the sentence. This section identifies 
“capital extraction” as a risk. However, insurance supervisors often can restrict dividend distributions and are 
typically able to review fees paid by the insurer to other entities within the group. The BMA requires disclosure of 
fees paid to investment service providers.Section 3.5.5 Credit risk and credit ratings: Although concerns about 
ratings “shopping” are legitimate, they can be addressed by appropriate supervisory oversight of ratings and 
rating agencies. The Issues Paper emphasizes that insurers should perform their own independent 
assessments, not necessarily relying on rating agencies. We believe this is already common practice and the 
concerns stated in the paper are well governed by the BMA’s due diligence requirements within the Prudent 
Person Principle (PPP).Section 3.5.6 Regulatory capital-related issues: This section suggests that regulatory 
frameworks may create incentives that “contribute to trends in alternative asset investments in ways that may not 
valuation 
practices.

benefit policyholders or financial stability.” We agree that these incentives can exist, and we broadly support 
efforts to align regulatory capital requirements with the underlying risk of the investments. To address this, the 
BMA continuously monitors the incentives created by its regulatory framework and undergoes efforts to remedy 
notable deficiencies. We endorse the NAIC’s February 2025 pledge to “equal capital for equal risk” and 
encourage the IAIS to embed a comparable, evidence-driven calibration cycle.3.5.7 Increased complexities 
around the management of alternative assets: We agree that these investments require insurers to develop or 
employ expertise and specialized skills to appropriately manage risks. Sufficient infrastructure, expertise and 
governance is required under the BMA’s prudent person principle.3.5.8 Information gaps: This section 
recommends supervisors “ensure that insurers invest in enhanced due diligence processes to better understand 
the nature and risks of alternative assets if these assets constitute a material exposure.” This is another area 
sufficiently addressed by the mechanisms within the BMA’s PPP. 
Athora, Bermuda 
• On the areas highlighted in this section, it is important that the areas where we can see regulators pay more 
attention in respect of alternative assets is done in comparison with traditional/public markets and not in absolute 
terms (which would lead to the exact wrong conclusions). 
• There are internal inconsistencies across insurance regulators with examples being (i) criticise insurers for 
investing in unrated assets (under S2), being concerned that it is done to achieve lower SCR; (ii) express 
continued concern that insurers rely too much on (external) ratings or (iii) both, in a review on the same asset 
class. Clarification of the priority (rating or insurer-run PPP without reliance on external rating) and further light 
shed on this dilemma, would help. 
• It is important to contextualise the analysis of the ECB.  This was limited to a stylised model of insurers’ 
investment behaviour as it might apply to government bonds, particularly those in jurisdictions other than the 
domicile of the insurer. This analysis makes reference to Biljsma and Vermeulen 2017 which considers the 
implication of a market based regime for valuation on procyclicality.  - ‘Our finding that insurers bought domestic 
sovereign bonds during the sovereign debt crisis, even if their risk premia and credit quality deteriorated, can 
provide some indication as to where to look for the potential buyers in a crisis scenario’. Highlighting that 
valuation can create procyclicality which is contrary to the counter-cycle role of life insurers given their liability 
profile. 
• Section 3.5.6 overlaps with the Asset Intensive Reinsurance section and if this section is retained, duplication 
should be avoided by including constructive consideration of how different underlying philosophies towards 
regulation create differences that do not equate to arbitrage but jurisdictional diversity that can support risk -
matching long term liabilities as considered in Sigma 2/2024.  
Conflicts of interest vs. alignment of interest 
• The argument presented relates to conflict of interest, which may exist in a number of cases. However, it is 
important to also note the potential for alignment of interest created by private markets: while underwriters and

some alternative 
assets there may 
be some 
improvements in 
the availability of 
information.

information gaps 
remain 
meaningful 
enough to be 
addressed by the 
requirement of 
enhanced due 
diligence by the 
insurers investing 
in alternative 
assets compared 
to traditional
