Additional 
insights will be

managers of public assets are almost always pure agents, originators and managers of private assets are often 
principal in addition to agent.  
• The nature of the alignment of interest of the alternative manager is critical: performance fees only (this can 
create both alignment and conflict), or co-investment (mostly alignment) 
Alignment of interest Conflict of interest 
Asset manager as a pure agent Alignment of interest - No incentive to take or reduce risk; no incentive to 
influence valuation Conflict of interest - Asset manager is volume-driven, without ‘skin in the game’ in the 
performance of the assets 
Performance fees Alignment of interest - Manager is incentivised, through the fee structure, to the performance 
of the assets Conflict of interest - The manager’s exposure to asset performance is asymmetric, with alignment 
to the upside but limited downside. In situations where the manager has control over valuations, it can have an 
incentive to influence them to inflate performance fees (p. 20) 
Co-investment of the manager in the assets Alignment of interest - Manager has a symmetric exposure to 
downside and upside, aligned with the insurer 
Work-out 
• Low recovery / poor performance upon default in public credit markets can partly be attributed to the very high 
fragmentation of the investor base, which challenges lender coordination and restructuring ahead of, and during, 
bankruptcy.  
• Private credit, when originated / managed by one or a small number of specialists, facilitates early resolution 
and coordination, supporting additional optionality that can support higher recovery rates upon default, pre -
emption of costly default through orderly restructuring or orderly deferral in times of stress.  
 
Links to PE firms 
• Under 3.5.4, a very clear distinction must be made between insurers under private permanent capital ownership 
and insurers under PE fund ownership.  
• Private permanent capital ownership of an insurer supports the execution of long -term business strategy and 
capital management.  
• Private equity fund ownership of an insurer can give rise to some of the issues identified in the document, 
including (i) misalignment between the financial time horizon of the equity ownership (related to the fund’s life) 
and the long-term nature of the insurance business; (ii) potential pressure on capital distributions; (iii) limited 
incentive or capacity to support the insurance company if capital is needed  
• The experience of the Italian insurance market in the past years provides an illustration of this differentia tion, 
with the case of a life insurance company facing capital and liquidity strains, and a PE fund as owner not 
providing the necessary capital support to ensure the going concern viability of the entity through the stress 
considered in 
future work eg 
Application Paper

period. 
• The consultation paper’s comment that “The differing investment horizons could create conflicts of interest in 
the timing of returns and, by extension, the investment strategies. Life insurance companies are primarily 
concerned with paying policyholders’ claims over a longer time horizon, typically between 10 and 20 years. 
Meanwhile, in certain jurisdictions such as the US and Canada, PE firms have typically managed to a shorter 
time horizon, with the average holding period of each investment just above seven years at the end of 2023, up 
from below six years during the period 2010-2022” applies to PE fund ownership only and this should be made 
explicit. 
Hidden leverage 
• The consultation paper correctly highlights the importance of leverage in the credit risk of assets invested into 
by insurers. However, ‘hidden leverage’ can be better addressed 
Hidden leverage in traditional assets 
• The potential for ‘hidden leverage’ to amplify business and credit risk is not unique to alternative assets. For 
instance, public bonds issued by public corporates are also subject to similar risk: according to recent research 
(“Bank Lines of Credit in Corporate Finance: An Empirical Analysis” - link) drawdowns on lines of credit represent 
30 percent of aggregate debt outstanding for public firms, and unused lines of credit on corporate balance sheets 
represent 10 percent of total assets. 
• The discussion of ‘hidden leverage’ within alternative assets should include a comparison between different 
types of alternative assets and traditional assets such as corporate bonds, in order to ascertain where this risk is 
more, or less, prevalent. 
• It is important to distinguish between different types of interconnectedness: while in the majority of cases the 
asset manager originating private credit is not involved in the (private) equity of the borrowers, there are cases 
where there is direct involvement at two levels of the capital structure which can present greater 
interconnectedness. 
We summarise below three possible types of interconnectedness between private debt and private equity 
a. Direct interconnectedness Private market managers often manage both equity and debt portfolios  
While most managers enforce appropriate conflicts of interest policies with respect to investments made in 
different parts of the capital structure of a company, debt/equity linkages can arise if a manager originates / 
manages both debt and equity 
A given insurer or private markets manager investing debt and equity of the same corporate entity represents  
direct interconnectedness 
b. Indirect interconnectedness When no direct interconnectedness as (a) applies, cross -relationships between 
corporates and sponsors can give rise to indirect interconnectedness 
To illustrate this, consider the case of two private market managers (A and B) financing two corporates (1 and 2).

If: 
- Manager A is debt sponsor of 1 and equity sponsor of 2 
- Manager B is equity sponsor of 1 and debt sponsor of 2 
Then indirect interconnected can arise: While no conflict of interest is present and no direct exposure overlap 
exists between debt and equity within each manager, the interrelationships above have the potential to create 
contagion effects between corporates, and between managers in the even of underperformance or failure of the 
corporates being financed through debt and equity 
c. Macroeconomic links In the case where no material cross-connections (b) are present, there is limited overlap 
between the sponsor base of equity and debt across borrowers. 
Residual interconnected is macro-economic in nature: there is a degree of contagion risk between debt sponsors 
and equity sponsors, in the same way as banks’ exposure to debt or equity of corporates may, in a downturn, 
reduce their lending to other corporates 
• Regarding NAV lending, the statement made in the consultation paper that “The originatio n of a NAV loan 
traditionally indicates that a portfolio company or companies are underperforming” appears incorrect, as NAV 
lending is, for certain private equity / venture capital funds and some private credit funds a structural component 
of their strategy regardless of performance. 
Contagion and macro prudential aspects 
• The section refers to certain potential effects that are macro-economic in nature: they have limited direct effect 
on each individual insurance company, but have the potential, when occurring simultaneously across the 
financial industry, to affect financial stability. For example, “increased leverage and complexity associated with 
issuing additional debt can amplify financial results, escalate valuation volatility” is a macro -economic effect. 
• When considering the macro-economic impact and risks associated with the growth of alternative assets, it is 
important to view these in conjunction with traditional public assets, rather than in isolation. Doing so would 
provide a sounder basis to assess the marginal impact of alternative assets: if one assumes that an increased 
allocation to alternative assets by insurers is funded by a decreased allocation to traditional assets, the net, 
rather than gross, impact should be assessed. 
• Traditional assets do present ‘hidden leverage’ and contagion risk (see ‘Hidden leverage in traditional asset’ 
above). The lending capacity and appetite of banks is a potential factor of contagion and amplification of 
economic and financial shocks within traditional assets. According to recent research (“Liquidity Insurance vs. 
Credit Provision: Evidence from the COVID-19 Crisis”, link). During COVID, banks saw their existing credit lines 
drawn, and at the same time tightened their lending standards, thereby curtailing  lending to small and medium 
corporates and supply of large syndicated loans 
PPP – prudent person principle 
• The identification, reporting, and management of risks associated with ‘hidden leverage’ should be viewed as

part of the PPP of an insurance company. Whether an investment is made in a traditional asset or alternative 
asset, the identification and monitoring of different types of leverage above or below the point in the capital 
structure in which the insurer invests is a critical component of sound asset underwriting. For insurers operating 
with appropriate PPP, hidden leverage should not constitute a distinct risk factor.  
Investing in the real economy  
• “Investing in the real economy” is addressed in very general terms (3.4.7: “Alternative asset investments can 
provide long-term funding for the real economy, supporting economic growth and development. Additionally, 
investments in renewable energy and climate-related assets may help foster innovation and support 
sustainability initiatives, contributing to broader economic stability and growth”) .  
• As public corporate bonds and equity also finance the real economy, a more specific description of the 
respective roles of alternative assets and traditional assets is needed. For example: (i) financing of corporates 
with insufficient size to justify the costs of access to public markets (as noted in the first sections); (ii) customised 
financing structures required for certain projects that cannot conform to standard bu llet bond format of public 
bonds; (iii) medium- to long-term financing that does not fit the banks’ appetite for liquidity transformation risk.  
• One may view all types of financings as investing in the real economy: public bonds and equities, alternative  
assets, bank financing. The structure and investor base of alternative assets complements the role of banks and 
public markets in the financing of the economy. 
Semi-liquid funds 
• The consultation paper highlights the risks specific to semi-liquid alternative funds (3.5.3 box 2). While the 
statement “In the context of private credit funds, liquidity risk can be a significant concern for funds which offer 
early redemption to investors” is logical, it is important to quantify the exposure of insurance compan ies to semi-
liquid funds:  size of this market and share of insurers (general account and unit-linked/VA) invested. We do not 
have quantitative reports on the materiality of insurers’ exposure to semi-liquid credit funds, but understand from 
past experience that it is immaterial today, at least when the insurance industry is taken in aggregate.  
• The European Union introduced the ELTIF structure to facilitate access by retail investors to new types of 
assets. While the growth rate of the ELTIF market has been significant, it remains small relative to total fund 
investments and insurance liabilities, with EUR 13.3bn as of 2023 (link).  
• Real estate represents the largest semi-liquid fund market in Europe. As documented in the ECB’s financial 
stability publication (link) real estate investment funds had approximately EUR 1trn of assets at the end of 2022, 
the majority of which is either open-ended or semi-liquid.  “REIFs with a mismatch between asset liquidity and 
redemption terms accounted for as much as 31% of the total open-ended REIFs market as at the third quarter of 
2021”. A material share of the European real estate investment funds are wrapped in unit -linked insurance 
policies or held by the insurance general account (estimate for France: 17% of SCPI hel d by insurers; 12% in 
unit-linked;  link). The behaviour of open-ended and semi-liquid funds in periods of crisis has been studied (link),

highlighting differences in approach and risk profile. This market segment is hardly new: its size was EUR 323bn 
AUM in 2012. 
• The focus on semi-liquid funds is relevant. However, placing this focus under “private credit” is inappropriate, as 
the largest semi-liquid funds with the potential for liquidity transformation risk, are with real estate funds. On this 
basis, semi-liquid funds should be presented as a standalone topic rather than be included in the private credit 
commentary. 
Liquidity risk 
• The Issues Paper notes (3.5.3) “The timing of capital calls, depending on contractual terms, can transfer 
liquidity stress either directly to fund investors or initially to the banking sector before reaching investors. As 
investors, insurers may have limited control over the timing of capital calls”.  
• It is important to nuance this conclusion by highlighting the differences in the types of structures through which 
insurers invest in private credit funds: 
o Traditional commitments to commingled private credit funds exhibit the feature described in the Issues Paper: 
an insurance investor committed to such a fund does not control the timing of capital calls.  
o Insurers also employ separately managed accounts (SMAs) and bespoke fund structures that give the insurer 
the right to control the flows into and from the private credit portfolio. In such structure, the potential liquidity risk 
factor described in the Issues Paper doesn’t apply. 
• These liquidity risks are reduced through the process of asset liability manage ment as well as incentives to 
maintain a diverse range of assets. 
Credit risk and credit ratings 
The following specific points should be considered for adjustment: 
• “For alternative assets, where information might not be publicly available, more reliance is placed on 
engagements with the issuer, which could introduce subjectivity and may lead to differences in the ratings 
outcome between different rating agencies”.  It is not clear why ‘engagements with the issuer’ are unique to 
alternative assets (they form part of the rating process for traditional, public bonds) or why such engagements 
would reduce the quality or consistency of ratings 
• It may be noted that regulators, e.g. EIOPA, have strongly encouraged insurers to reduce their reliance on 
(external) credit ratings. 
Information gaps 
Section 3.5.8 notes risks associated with potential information gaps when insurers invest in alternative assets. Its 
emphasis on the importance of information of appropriate quality and timeliness supporting investment deci sion 
making is recognised. However, the following should be considered for adjustment  
• Availability of information to private credit and private equity investors 
o The Issues Paper states “One major challenge is the lack of transparency, often providing limited information

on underlying assets, valuation methods and performance metrics. However, this is not always the case. 
Investors in a private loan portfolio might receive detailed analyses such as forward -looking projections, monthly 
performance against budgets and quarterly compliance certificates, enabling more informed decisions and close 
monitoring”.  
o The level of information available on (potential and realized) private equity and private credit investments can 
be significantly greater than in the case of traditional, public, assets, given the direct access to the company and 
the pre-conditions made to investing 
o While information is often not publicly available, private equity and private credit investments are based on in -
depth data and engagement with investee companies, as a pre-requisite for the consideration of a debt or equity 
investments. Post-investment monitoring is also based on regular financial data and business updates.  
o This view is supported by academic research on the topic (private equity: review o f financial studies 2011 (link); 
private credit: University of Chicago (link), Oxford Review of Economic Policy (link and link)) and industry guides 
(USPEC (link)) 
• Broad vs. narrow investor base 
o The Issue Paper states “There is also a risk in cases where the investor base is very limited. This 
concentration risk can exacerbate the challenges associated with information gaps, as a limited number of 
market participants may lead to less competitive pricing and a narrower range of available data”.  
o The statement is misleading. The limited number of the ultimate investor base of a private asset, be it equity or 
credit, provides strong incentive on the investor to conduct and incur the cost and resources for deep due 
diligence on an asset to ensure they understand the risks associated.  
o In broadly held public markets, the cost of research / diligence on a single asset tends to dominate the benefit 
for an investor holding a small shareholding or debt holding. Research (Journal of Finance (link)) tends to 
corroborate this view. 
o In our direct investing experience, information is in fact deeper and richer when a very small number of lenders 
or equity investors are bidding for / originating an asset. 
• Information and use of information 
o The Issue Paper correctly notes that “Investors in public equities and bonds typically receive regulatory 
required information, with publicly listed companies required to publish financial statements and accompanying 
disclosures on a regular, periodic basis”.  
o However, the soundness of the insurance company’s investment process and application of PPP does not 
depend solely on the availability of information, but also its use.  
o Investors in public corporate bonds may for instance apply a passive style (index tracking) without considering 
the financial data available about issuers; or may apply a ‘light active’ management style focused on ratings, 
sector exclusions, and spread levels that does not make substantial use of the financial data published by the

issuers.  
o The availability of information to investors is a necessary, but not sufficient condition for sound investments.  
o Alternative investments are made, in well-managed asset managers and insurance companies, based on an 
analysis of the borrower’s/issuer’s financials and business structure that is more focused and targeted to the 
analysis typically done for an investment in a traditional (public) bond or equity. 3.5.8 should be complemented 
by a discussion/review of the use of issuers’ information in traditional (public) assets by insurance companies. 
• Complexity 
o Complexity is not limited to alternative assets. For instance, bank-issued bonds may be seen as highly 
complex (presence or absence of bail-in; different types of subordinated debt / hybrid capital;  heterogeneity of 
issuing entity within a banking group), while being a traditional, public, asset. 
o In the 3.5.7, the potential complexity of certain alternative assets should be put in the perspective of the 
complexity of traditional assets, in order to provide perspective on the ‘net’ change complexity associated with an 
insurer increasing its investment in alternative assets while decreasing its allocation to traditional assets.

[The following is an excerpt from our General Comment.]  
 
The Issues Paper also makes inferences and draws conclusions that are wholly unsupported by quantitative or 
historical data. A particularly concerning example pertains to the “indicative” alternative asset classes set forth on 
Table 1 that appear to be intended to serve as the de facto alternative asset classes under the IAIS principles -
based definition of the same. Section 3.3.1 proposes an open-ended definition of “alternative assets” such that 
alternative assets are assets “which display a high degree of either valuation uncertainty, illiquidity or complexity, 
or a combination of these” (“Principles”). An “indicative” alternative asset class as set forth on Table 1 is an asset 
that meets “two or three” of the Principles.  
 
Although Table 1 classifies a total of 12 alternative asset classes as “indicative” alternative asset classes, the 
Issues Paper fails to provide a basis for how any one or more of the indicative alternative assets were 
determined to display a high degree of the valuation uncertainty, illiquidity or complexity, and no quantitative data 
was provided to support such a conclusion. Although a qualitative survey of insurance supervisors was 
discussed in the same section, the data – a 2024 survey of supervisors reflecting their views with respect to (a) 
valuation uncertainty, (b) illiquidity, and (c) complexity of alternative assets (see Figure 2) – actually supports a

conclusion that no alternative asset class exhibits a high degree of valuation uncertainty, illiquidity, or complexity. 
For example, with respect to collateralized loan obligations (“CLO”), only a very small minority of supervisors 
took the position that CLO investments are “very illiquid” with respect to the liquidity poll, “very hard to value” with 
respect to the valuation poll, or “very complex” with respect to the complexity poll (see Figure 2).  Nevertheless, 
“structured securities” (which broadly includes CLO) is included in Table 1 as an indicative alternative asset that 
“meets two or three” of the Principles.  
 
The foregoing example also raises important structural considerations that permeate throughout the Issues 
Paper: (1) the need to distinguish and define each alternative asset class and discuss each on its own merits, (2) 
the need to incorporate additional data points to assess the captioned asset classes and instruments, and (3) 
whether the proposed principles-based definition of “alternative asset” is fit for purpose.  
 
(1) The need to distinguish and define each alternative asset class and discuss each on its own merits  
 
The current version of the Issues Paper conflates an extremely diverse set of products whose only similarity is 
that they are not corporate bonds or publicly-traded equities. The Issues Paper should endeavor to describe 
each of the various types of asset managers, alternative asset classes, and financial instruments referenced 
throughout the report, and how they differ from one another. The Issues Paper should also discuss each 
alternative asset class and instrument on its own merits (based on quantitative and historical data), rather than 
grouping everything into one discussion and suggesting that potential drawbacks of one in strument apply to all of 
the captioned alternative assets. Along the same lines, the Issues Paper should seek to use consistent 
terminology throughout the report, particularly with respect to asset classes and instruments. These 
recommendations align with the stated objectives of the Issues Paper.  
 
(2) The need for additional data points to assess the captioned asset classes and instruments  
 
The Issues Paper concludes that there is a need for additional data on alternative asset investments and AIR, 
and that “certain data items are only available in a limited number of jurisdictions.” See Section 3.3.3. Such a 
concession should be sufficient to conclude that additional data is necessary to produce a report that is fit for 
purpose. Although it does appear that the IAIS has collected a limited amount of relevant data (e.g., via the 
GIMAR), which was utilized to conclude that alternative assets “currently do not pose a global financial stability 
concern” (see Section 3.2), there are also abundant objective studies and analyses by scholars, industry groups, 
and others that can be leveraged to develop a more objective report and provide clarification on some of the 
misconceptions referenced therein. For example:   
depending on 
internal 
processes and 
type of alternative 
asset".

• Macroeconomic Considerations: The Federal Reserve Bank of Boston recently concluded that, in comparison 
to traditional banks, private credit funds are less vulnerable to runs and that overall financial stability risk would 
decrease to the extent that (as the Issues Paper suggests) private credit lending represents a credit substitution 
(i.e., private credit firms are lending to borrowers that might otherwise seek loans from a traditional bank). See 
Federal Reserve Bank of Boston, Current Policy Perspectives, Could the Growth of Private Credit Pose a Risk to 
Financial System Stability?, May 2025. Indeed, objective studies indicate that alternative assets like CLOs “do 
not pose a systemic risk to the U.S. financial system.” See LSTA, Assessing Whether Lever aged Loans and 
CLOs Pose a Systemic Risk., December 2023. To the contrary, alternative asset investments like CLOs are 
unlikely to present a global systemic risk in the future because they are stable, there exists a strong internal 
governance framework with respect to the investments, they will represent a relatively small proportion of insurer 
investment portfolios, and insurers remain obligated to demonstrate adequate capital and liquidity buffers, among 
others.  
 
• Valuation Uncertainty: There are a number of methods to value alternative assets which are broadly accepted 
and have been used for many years. The International Private Equity and Venture Capital Valuation Guidelines, 
for example, contemplate a wide array of alternative asset valuations.  In addition, insurers and their third-party 
investment managers have long track records of being able to accurately identify and value appropriate 
investments. See e.g., Young Soo Jang and Ginha Kim, Valuation Discipline in Private Credit, March 2025.  
 
• Illiquidity: Although the Issues Paper takes issue with illiquid assets, such assets actually reduce risk for a given 
level of return. See e.g., National Association of State Retirement Administrators, NASRA Issue Brief: Public 
Pension Plan Investment Return Assumptions, updated February 2017; Hamilton Lane data via Morgan Stanley 
Research, How Long Will the Golden Age of Private Markets Last?, February 2021.  
 
See also, LSTA, Private Credit Survey Report, April2 2025, available at: https://www.lsta.org/content/lsta -private-
credit-survey-report/. A 2022 Aon Inpoint white paper titled Review of Alternative Asset Managers’ Involvement 
with Life & Annuity Insurers, which was commissioned by the AIC, may also be instructive and is available at: 
https://www.investmentcouncil.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/09/9.09.2022-Aon-White-Paper_Final.pdf.  
 
(3) Whether the principles-based definition of “alternative asset” is fit for purpose 
 
It is unclear how the proposed principles-based definition of an alternative asset – i.e., an asset with a high 
degree of valuational uncertainty, illiquidity, and/or complexity – achieves the Issues Paper’s objective of

“establishing a clear definition of alternative assets and criteria to classify these investments.” To the contrary, 
such a definition is likely to result in further confusion amongst supervisors and lead to incomplete or misleading 
data points, particularly given the diverse set of supervisor practices, regulatory frameworks, and jurisdiction -
specific markets. The definition also fails to account for various product features and options that can vary from 
product to product and can materially alter the risk profile of the investment. For these reasons, the IAIS should 
reconsider the appropriateness of establishing an “alternative asset” definition (of any kind), at least until 
supervisors have the opportunity to conduct further analysis and information gath ering efforts.

The IAIS notes supervisory concerns related to valuation uncertainty, illiquidity, and complexity. However, the 
risk that supervisors should focus on is the net risk after mitigants that truly matters.  
 
The risk of a stress scenarios involving elevated surrenders on a life insurance company varies globally. The 
regulatory framework and product features in some countries, like the US, greatly reduce the risk for these 
scenarios from insurers.  In 2020, the United States adopted a Liquidity Stress Testing (LST) Framework for 
large life insurers that annually evaluates for the potential for mass lapse. The LST results indicated that U.S. life 
insurers would retain sufficient liquidity, even in the event of a mass lapse. Regulators also have other tools that 
evaluate liquidity. In the U.S., regulators require all life insurers to regularly test the adequacy of their assets to 
meet their obligations, including under stressed scenarios. Cash flow risk (lapse) and interest rate risk are 
included in the mandatory asset-adequacy testing.

Comments on Section 3.5.5 Credit risk and credit ratings 
 
The Issues Paper states that “[c]redit rating agencies aim to remain independent and objective in their 
assessments.” It is important to note that credit rating agencies are in fact required by regulation in many 
jurisdictions to be independent and objective in their assessments of credit risk. 
 
The Issues Paper notes that credit ratings are less prominent in the private credit market and attributes their 
absence to the fact that the issuer typically bears the cost of ratings which limits their appeal primarily to large, 
well-capitalized firms. While additional cost may sometimes be a factor discouraging issuers from obtaining credit 
ratings, private issuers and investors frequently do obtain (private) credit ratings. In fact, private ratings on 
alternative assets in insurers’ portfolios constitute one of the fastest growing segments for credit ratings. The 
primary ratings-related distinction between private and public debt markets is not in the relati ve demand for 
ratings, per se, but rather a preference in the private market for private over public ratings.  
 
In the private market, where borrowers and lenders typically negotiate terms directly and the lender often intends 
to hold the debt to maturity, there may not be a direct demand for a credit rating from one or both parties. 
Revisions done.

Nonetheless, credit ratings for alternative investments are frequently sought, particularly when the investors’ 
financial regulators use such ratings for calculating credit risk capital requirements. Regulatory frameworks 
exhibit notable variation globally, which may increase the appeal of certain types of credit ratings in certain 
jurisdictions. For instance, in jurisdictions such as the EU and the UK, private ratings cannot be used for 
regulatory purposes, and only public ratings or ratings distributed by subscription are generally permissible for 
regulatory use. In contrast, the U.S. insurance regulatory framework does not distinguish between public and 
private ratings, and notably, the expansion of alternative asset investments within the U.S. insurance sector has 
coincided with an increased demand for private ratings.  
 
The Issues Paper also states that “[i]n some jurisdictions, regulations require all credit rating agencies to be held 
to the same standards, and credit rating shopping may be mitigated.” While credit rating agencies, such as those 
registered with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission as nationally recognized statistical rating 
organizations, or NRSROs, are subject to common regulatory requirements concerning their business conduct 
and processes, this statement could be misconstrued. There is no regulatory mandate or basis for assuming that 
all credit rating agencies utilize rating scales that carry the same meaning with respect to default risk or that they 
will necessarily reach similar rating conclusions, on individual securities. As we have discussed elsewhere, [FN8] 
the primary mechanisms that could foster greater comparability in ratings among different credit rating agencies 
– to the extent such comparability exists in certain market segments – are market forces. These forces, including 
investor scrutiny and issuer feedback, are most effective when ratings, detailed rating rationales, the  
corresponding rating methodologies, the identity of the rating provider, and critical underlying financial 
information are publicly disclosed.

Comments on Section 3.5.8 Information Gaps 
 
This section focuses on the challenges individual insurers may face in their sourcing, underwriting and risk 
management of alternative assets, and it argues that supervisors should ensure that insurers make the 
necessary resource investments to overcome these challenges. This section does not, however, discuss the 
broader issue suggested by its title; that is, the information gaps experienced by third parties – potential 
policyholders, equity analysts, credit analysts, and for the regulators, themselves – arising from the lack of 
financial information or public ratings on alternative assets.  Moody’s discusses its view on these information 
gaps more broadly throughout our other responses.

Comments on section 3.6 Macroeconomic considerations 
 
As noted above, there is very little historical evidence of runs on insurance companies, even during the 2008 
financial crisis or the Great Depression in the 1930s. In addition to this relative stability on the liability side of 
insurers' balance sheets, the asset side is also relatively stable, given the longer duration of private credit. The 
extended lead time required to establish alternative investments should be taken into account, as it tends to 
dampen procyclical behavior typically observed in traditional bond and equity markets. For instance, initiating a 
new investment in a commingled alternative fund, a single-investor fund, or a separately managed account 
typically takes between six and eighteen months, with capital deployment occurring over several years. This 
structure inherently limits rapid shifts in response to market cycles, making it less prone to classical procyclical 
dynamics. 
 
A recent ACC report, "Reassessing Systemic Risk in Nonbank Financial Institutions," highlights several ways in 
which private credit—and, by extension, many other NBFIs—contribute to financial stability through anticyclical 
behavior that can make banks safer. 
 
Private credit funds are typically structured with long-term, closed-end capital commitments, unlike banks that 
rely on short-term deposits and face run risk. This stable funding structure allows private credit funds to deploy 
capital steadily and avoid forced sales during periods of market volatility. The report also references empirical 
studies, including one by the European Systemic Risk Board, showing that insurers and similar NBFIs acted as 
countercyclical investors during crises such as the 2008 financial crisis and the European sovereign debt crisis. 
While banks were forced into procyclical asset sales, insurance investors were able to acquire depressed assets, 
helping to stabilize markets. 
 
In addition, private credit funds avoid the maturity transformation common in banking by aligning the duration of 
their assets and liabilities, thereby mitigating liquidity risk. The report further explains how banks' exposure is 
reduced when they lend to private credit funds in a senior, secured position. In this model, equity investors in the 
fund absorb losses first, providing a buffer that enhances the safety of the bank's position. 
Noted. The 
benefits section 
has been 
enhanced to 
make the paper 
more balanced.

Application Paper

Finally, because private credit funds use little or no leverage and hold bespoke, non -traded loans, they are less 
exposed to the kind of fire-sale risk that can exacerbate systemic crises. These features show how private credit 
can dampen systemic volatility and procyclically complement the banking system's stability.

The IAIS’s concerns regarding potential procyclicality and systemic instability stemming from insurers’ growing 
exposures to “alternative” assets should be balanced against the structural and regulatory safeguards intrinsic to 
the insurance business model: 
    1. Long-Term, Stable Liabilities. Long-term obligations reduce the likelihood of forced asset sales. 
    2. High-Quality Risk Management. ALM frameworks and third-party valuations ensure effective governance. 
    3. Liquidity Testing. U.S. (NAIC) and Bermuda (BMA) annual liquidity stress tests confirm insurers’ resilience 
in extreme scenarios, including high-rate or stagflation environments. 
    4. Transparent Oversight. Existing solvency and disclosure rules require insurers to regularly demonstrate that 
capital and liquidity buffers are sufficient, even under hypothetical market shocks.  
 
By recognizing these factors – along with capital, audit, and regulatory requirements – observers can be 
confident that the insurance sector’s participation in private credit and other “alternative” assets enhances market 
stability rather than worsening systemic vulnerabilities. See more detail below.  
 
LONG TERM, STABLE LIABILITIES PROVIDE SYSTEMIC STABILITY  
Noted. Existing 
regulatory 
safeguards, as 
well as ALM and 
governance 
frameworks, 
already help to 
mitigate 
macroeconomic 
concerns. Further 
data and analysis 
would enhance 
the section’s 
usefulness. 
Overall,

1. No Maturity Transformation.  Unlike banks that rely on overnight or short-term deposits, life insurers 
typically hold stable, long-dated liabilities such as annuities or pension obligations, without maturity 
transformation. This funding profile reduces the risk of a “run” and lessens the likelihood of forced asset fire sales 
during stress events.  As an example, Athene, USA’s insurance policies have an eight-year weighted-average 
life (WAL) overall, with some policies in the portfolio being very long term (Appendix, slide 9, 10). 
 
    2. No Federal Backstop / Government Guarantee. Insurers operate under conservative asset -liability 
management (ALM) frameworks to ensure they can meet obligations, as they do not have direct access to bank 
liquidity windows (like the U.S. Federal Reserve discount window) or rely on government guarantees. 
 
    3. Complement, Not Substitute, for Banking Sector. Because insurer liabilities are stable and predictably long -
dated, insurers are well-placed to hold illiquid or “alternative” assets for extended periods, thus absorbing 
volatility when markets tighten. This can reduce systemic fragility by dispersing credit exposure across nonbank 
balance sheets. 
 
VALUATION AND REPORTING STANDARDS  
    1. Adherence to Accounting and Audit Requirements. In major jurisdictions, private credit fund managers must 
follow recognized accounting frameworks. Valuations typically undergo rigorous internal modeling (e.g., 
discounted cash flow) and third-party pricing assessments. External auditors regularly review these valuations, 
minimizing “information gaps” that could lead to hidden risk. (Appendix, slide 11).  
 
    2. Publicly Filed Stress Testing Results. U.S. insurers follow annual NAIC Liquidity Stress Testing (LST) 
procedures in collaboration with their lead state regulators. Bermuda Monetary Authority (BMA) imposes stress -
test mandates, requiring long-term insurers to demonstrate adequate liquidity buffers under simulated adverse 
market scenarios (e.g., sudden policyholder surrenders, interest rate shocks).  (BMA, Dec. 18, 2023, Supervision 
and Regulation of PE Insurers in Bermuda, pp. 10-12, 16, 21), 
 
MITIGATION OF PROCYCLICALITY RISKS  
       1. Countercyclical Lending. In times of market stress, banks often retrench, whereas life insurers with long -
dated liabilities can maintain or even increase lending (i.e., “private credit”), helping to stabilize credit markets. 
This was observed after the Global Financial Crisis, where insurers and other nonbank lenders continued to 
deploy capital while banks reduced exposures. 
 
An inherent difference between the bank and insurance business models is that insurers receive significant cash 
examining 
insurers’ potential 
investment 
behaviours under 
different 
macroeconomic 
conditions 
remains an 
important and 
valuable

inflows on a recurring basis from insureds’ premium payments, which are invested on a long -term basis to meet 
long-term liabilities. As a result, life insurers generally can hold their investments to maturity. In practice, this 
enables them to be buyers during short-term market dislocations, a reality that has been empirically established 
in various studies, including a government-led study by the European Systemic Risk Board. That paper analyzed 
market data from 2005 to 2014 and found that insurance companies were countercyclical investors during the 
2008 Global Financial Crisis and the European sovereign debt crisis. While banks were forced to procyclically 
sell assets, insurers were able to buy assets with depressed prices during market dips and enhance t heir long-
term investment gains.  (Alternative Credit Council, 2024, Reassessing Systemic Risk in Nonbank Financial 
Institutions, pp. 6-7).  (Appendix, slide 12). 
 
    2. Interest Rate Scenario Analysis. Whether rates are rising, falling, or stagnant, insu rers employ scenario-
based modeling for their assets and liabilities. These analyses capture how changes in rates might affect 
portfolio valuations, cash flows, surrender behavior, and capital ratios. Stagflation or high -rate environments are 
accounted for by applying severely adverse assumptions to both default rates and interest rate movements. 
(Appendix, slide 13). 
 
FOCUSED REGULATORY OVERSIGHT OF LIQUIDITY AND ALM  
    1. Annual Liquidity Stress Testing 
       - United States: The NAIC LST Framework (adopted in 2020) mandates large life insurers to file annual 
stress test results with lead regulators, evaluating coverage ratios across moderate -to-severe economic 
scenarios. 
       - Bermuda: The BMA similarly requires annual stress testing to ensure insurers can withstand unexpectedly 
high lapses or dislocations, with a Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) that must remain above prescribed thresholds. 
(BMA, Supervision and Reg. of PE Ins., pp. 16, 21). 
 
    2. ALM Governance and Board Oversight. In both the U.S. and Bermuda, insurer boards (and committees like 
Risk, Audit, ALM, and Investment) actively monitor liquidity positions, credit exposures, and interest rate 
sensitivities. This ensures capital planning incorporates potential market disruptions, limiting p rocyclical 
pressures.  (Appendix, slide 14). 
 
INTEREST RATE RISK AND SCENARIO ANALYSIS  
    1. Match Funding Advantage. Because insurers’ assets are intentionally matched to predictable liabilities (e.g., 
annuities), they can accept longer duration instruments without incurring the maturity mismatch typical of bank 
balance sheets. This characteristic inherently reduces the risk that an insurer must liquidate assets when rates

spike or markets become illiquid. 
 
    2. Dynamic Hedging Programs. Many life insurers maintain interest rate hedging programs using derivatives, 
within regulatory constraints, to protect capital from rate volatility. These risk controls are tested extensively in 
insurer ORSAs (Own Risk and Solvency Assessments), which supervisors review. 
 
Athene, USA embeds disciplined risk management at the core of its balance sheet strategy. We maintain a 
clearly articulated risk appetite framework, calibrated by regular, bilateral stress tests of both assets and 
liabilities. Since 2020, Athene, USA has publicly published its annual asset stress-testing results, illustrating 
projected impacts under a range of rate-shock and liquidity-strain scenarios. This practice not only demonstrates 
the efficacy of our match-funding and hedging programs but also addresses supervisory concerns about opacity 
in stress-scenario analysis – providing a replicable model for transparent disclosure and enhancing confidence in 
insurers’ interest rate risk management. (Appendix, slide 14).

We believe this section inappropriately views alternative assets in isolation. The section should instead be 
reframed to view alternative assets of as part of an overall ALM and investment strategy with regulatory 
safeguards in place such as liquidity stress test requirements (to confirm insurer resilience under extreme 
scenarios) and solvency and disclosure requirements (to ensure sufficient capital and liquidity).  
 
In the second sentence, the Issues Paper suggests that the investment behaviour of insurers  is “significantly 
influenced by economic cycles and market sentiment.”  However, the next paragraph notes that alternative 
assets have appeal in both periods of economic expansion and during economic downturns. This might suggest 
that the appeal of alternative assets is not necessarily a function of macroeconomic conditions. For accuracy and 
simplicity, we recommend deleting the second sentence.  
 
The Issues Paper also states "in times of stress, withdrawal from these investments (alt assets), could 
exacerbate market downturns and liquidity shortages." The point seems to be that such a “withdrawal” in a time 
of stress would be akin to a forced sale. However, a decision to withdraw would be informed by market 
conditions, and depressed valuations may create a market incentive not to sell.

• As noted above, there is very little historical evidence of runs on insurance companies, even during the 2008 
financial crisis (save for specific exceptions where companies had particular concentrations (AIG) or products 
with features that mirrored bank deposits) or the Great Depression in the 1930s.  
• The concern about an insurer being procyclical needs to be considered in the context of situations where a 
failure of an insurance company has led to wider systemic impacts. There is no clear link connecti ng investment 
in private assets with this issue. The two topics have become entangled at certain regulators, and it will help to 
distinguish them, potentially with ‘good practise’ / ‘bad practise’ examples – example is the holdings by Japanese 
Life Companies in the 1980s to 1990s or revisiting the analysis done by the ‘Report on Prudential Supervision of 
Insurance Undertakings’ by the Conference of Insurance Supervisory Services of Members States of the 
European Union in 2002 that informed the development of Solvency II. UK232gbrev6.PDF  In this context, the 
Issues Paper should consider the role of profitability of life insurers in balancing product pricing, surrender risk 
and asset liability management.  
• In addition to this relative stability on the liability side of insurers’ balance sheets, the asset side is also relatively  
stable, given the longer duration of private credit. The extended lead time required to establish alternative 
investments should be taken into account, as it tends to dampen procyclical behavior typically observed in 
traditional bond and equity markets.

• In order for the cautionary signals included in the document to fit with the current dynamics of insurers, the 
document should be complemented by an assessment of whether procyclical behaviour has actually been 
observed in alternative assets and whether it is more pronounced or less pronounced than in traditional assets.  
• The lead time for the set-up of alternative investments should be considered as this makes procyclical 
behaviour (with respect to credit spreads and equity prices) less pronounced than  in traditional bond and equity 
markets. As an example, the set-up of a new investment in a commingled alternative fund, single investor fund, 
or separately managed account typically takes six to eighteen months and the deployment period covers several 
years, which does not naturally fit a classical procyclical behaviour, even though it may arise in a ‘low rate for 
long’ scenario 
• It is important to distinguish between types of insurer behaviour – some may be countercyclical (set up 
funds/mandates built to deploy in times of market stress) while others can be procyclical (portfolio allocation by 
insurer based on spread target) as well as the basis on which an insurer is evaluating their allocation to 
alternative assets. This needs to be dynamic and adaptable to events bearing in mind the liability profile of the 
insurer. This is to avoid inadvertently creating procyclical behaviour when ‘public’ asset values sank and 
‘necessitated’ insurers who had exceeded their alternative asset allocation to sell private assets to meet their 
target asset allocations rather than reflecting on the appropriateness for their liability profile.  
Relationship to interest rates 
• For insurers with appropriate expertise to manage an interest rate derivatives portfolio and sufficient liquidity to 
support associated collateral movements, floating-rate assets combined with hedge overlays can better support 
asset/liability management than certain types of fixed rate assets (traditional callable bonds including capital 
securities; whole residential mortgages).

Characteristics and Market Evolution 
Asset-intensive reinsurance (AIR) is a risk management technique that benefits the insurance market and 
ultimately policyholders. The Paper, while recognizing many of the benefits of AIR strategies, focuses mainly on 
potential supervisory concerns without properly recognizing the benefits of AIR as an appropriate risk-transfer 
mechanism. We believe additional discussion of AIR in the context of the risk management benefits these 
arrangements provide—including enhanced capital efficiency, improved portfolio diversification, and specialized 
risk management expertise—would allow supervisors to take a more balanced perspective.  
While the Paper identifies several concerns around AIR (including questions about motivation, increasing 
complexity, market concentration, and recapture risk), it presents a somewhat narrow perspective that 
Revisions done. 
Additional 
insights can be 
considered for 
future work. Eg 
Application 
Paper. Societal 
protection gaps

emphasizes regulatory concerns over its broader economic and strategic functions. As noted, the term "asset -
intensive reinsurance" itself is potentially misleading, as the reinsurance process itself does not alter the degree 
to which a business is asset-intensive. AIR is fundamentally a matter of counterparty exposure management and 
collectability, not an issue of reserve adequacy for insurance risks. While the level of reserves determined under 
cashflow testing or reserve attribution approaches can provide insight into a cedant's exposure to collectability 
risk, such efforts do not accurately assess reinsurers’ capacity because they fail to evaluate their overall financial 
strength or solvency. Approaches that comprehensively assess a reinsurer's financial soundness would be more 
effective at addressing the fundamental concerns outlined in the Paper. 
It is important to address directly the specific concerns regarding life insurance and asset -intensive reinsurance 
in the private equity-owned context. Following cession of assets and liabilities, investments may be directed 
toward instruments such as private credit that could be characterized as risky assets, potentially due to different 
investment requirements in the assuming entity or jurisdiction. This specific concern relates to potential risks to 
policyholders in the ceding jurisdiction rather than to global financial stability. We believe this issue should be 
addressed through bilateral engagement between relevant jurisdictions, with ICPs providing a suffic iently robust 
baseline. Rather than modifying reinsurance frameworks, the focus should be on more consistent 
implementation of appropriate investment management requirements across jurisdictions. We note that several 
jurisdictions have already taken steps to address some of these considerations through enhanced regulatory 
frameworks and supervisory approaches.  
The growth of AIR has been influenced by multiple factors, including the low-for-long interest rate environment 
following the global financial crisis, demographic shifts affecting liability structures, and evolving business 
strategies focused on capital efficiency. These developments represent rational responses to market conditions 
rather than regulatory arbitrage. The Paper's treatment of affiliated transactions in Section 4.1.2 reflects an 
underlying presumption of regulatory arbitrage as the primary motivation while downplaying legitimate group risk 
management and capital efficiency objectives. While appropriate analysis is needed when regulatory f rameworks 
differ significantly between affiliated entities, consolidated supervision at the group level already captures many 
of the risks laid out in the Paper. 
The connection between new capital sources (such as private equity firms) and cross -border reinsurance 
provides valuable market capacity and diversification that helps address concentration risk concerns. These new 
market participants offer specialized asset management expertise that can be valuable in managing long-term 
liabilities. We note that, while the Paper expresses concern with respect to concentration risk, it simultaneously 
takes a negative view of the new capacity providers that help address this very issue.  
Section 5.1 identifies "financial market disruption from a mass recapture of AIR by one or many insurers" as a 
key financial stability risk. While this concern warrants attention, insurers already implement comprehensive 
safeguards including transaction-specific collateral arrangements, contractual terms and conditions, investment 
are out of scope 
in this paper.

guidelines, early warning triggers, and trust fund rules. Companies regularly conduct stress testing to assess 
potential impacts of recapture events on capital and asset positions, with these risk management practices 
documented and disclosed within regulatory filings such as the ORSA. This holistic approach to managing 
recapture and collectability risks represents standard industry practice that merits proper recognition in the 
Paper. 
Section 4.1.5 on complexity of retrocessions overemphasizes complexity while undervaluing the risk distribution 
benefits. Retrocession and sidecars provide important risk management functions. The Paper would benefit from 
more detailed exploration of these market practices to provide a more comprehensive educational resource f or 
supervisors. 
With respect to collateral arrangements (Sections 4.1.6-4.1.7), the Paper should highlight the maturity and 
sophistication of collateral management practices and the rarity of collateral inadequacy in practice.  
By enabling the provision of retirement savings, investment vehicles, and pension solutions at competitive 
pricing, AIR can serve as a mechanism for reducing protection gaps. Through strategic reinsurance 
arrangements, insurers can optimize capital allocation, access specialized inves tment expertise, and capture the 
illiquidity premium inherent in long-duration liabilities—all while maintaining robust safeguards for both cedants 
and policyholders. 
Section 4.1.1 on the economics of AIR transactions would benefit from greater acknowledgm ent of how such 
arrangements can facilitate better risk distribution across the financial system and enable greater market 
capacity. Economic benefits can extend beyond reinsurers and cedants to broader market capacity and 
consumer outcomes. The significant contribution these arrangements make to expanding insurance market 
capacity, improving product affordability, and ultimately reducing societal protection gaps merits greater 
emphasis in the Paper. 
Jurisdictional Approaches  
The use of AIR varies significantly across markets, with substantial differences in transaction structures, 
motivations, and characteristics. While we appreciate the Paper's acknowledgment of this diversity, at this stage 
standardized guidance may not be appropriate given these jurisdictional differences. For instance, different 
jurisdictional approaches to reserving, capital frameworks, and investment flexibility —as noted in Section 4.2—
can be equally effective in protecting policyholder interests. While we appreciate the acknowledgmen t of the 
diversity of insurers’ approaches, standardized guidance may be inappropriate given these jurisdictional 
differences.  
Rather than imposing uniform standards, supervisors would better serve market participants by engaging directly 
with insurers to understand the specifics of material transactions and developing proportionate, targeted 
responses to identified risks. This tailored approach is particularly important considering that AIR, though 
significant in certain jurisdictions and market segments, constitutes a relatively small portion of total industry

exposures, as noted in Section 5.2. The substantial differences across markets make standardized supervisory 
guidance impractical and potentially harmful to market development. A more nuanced regulatory framework 
would recognize effective jurisdictional practices while addressing material risks. 
The IAIS needs to articulate clearer objectives regarding insurance company solvency assessment frameworks. 
The Total Asset Requirement (TAR) concept, while acknowledged in the Paper as part of the regulatory and 
market tendency toward consolidated metrics, requires substantial further development to enable meaningful 
cross-jurisdictional comparisons. 
Both the IAIS and individual jurisdictional regulators should establish more precise objectives regarding group 
solvency standards. At its core, a target TAR for an insurance policy portfolio fundamentally expresses a 
regulatory preference for positioning the risk of those policies at a specific "probability of ruin" threshold—the 
statistical likelihood that assets will be insufficient to meet obligations. 
This probability-based approach to solvency assessment provides a more transparent framework for evaluating 
capital adequacy across different regulatory systems and business models. By clearly defining acceptable “ruin” 
probabilities, regulators can better align solvency requirements with policyholder protection goals while allowing 
for appropriate comparison of diverse solvency frameworks. 
The reference to investment flexibility in Section 4.2.6 should be updated to reflect that insurer investment 
frameworks are governed by both regulatory frameworks and internal risk management processes. These 
model-based constraints work in conjunction with formal investment guidelines to establish parameters for 
portfolio composition and risk exposure. This integrated framework balances investment flexibility with prudent 
risk management, ensuring that investment decisions remain aligned with the insurer's liability profile and defined 
risk tolerances. 
Supervisory Concerns  
While understandable from a supervisory perspective, the concerns raised in Section 4.3 should be 
substantiated by additional empirical evidence of material risk and more thorough acknowledgement of industry's 
robust risk management practices. Similarly, the Paper often fails to recognize the substantial enhancements to 
supervisory frameworks, the extent of existing cross-border supervisory coordination, and increased discussions 
between cedant and reinsurance supervisors, as noted in Section 4.3.6 and in the BMA's p ublication, The 
Evolution of Asset Intensive Insurance (December 2024).  Additionally, many of the supervisory enhancements 
proposed in Section 4.3.6 are sufficiently addressed within the ICPs. Rather than further expanding the ICPs, we 
believe a more proportionate approach would be to ensure their consistent implementation as they apply to AIR.  
Insurers implement sophisticated approaches to manage their reinsurance arrangements, protecting both 
themselves and policyholders while optimizing capital efficiency and risk diversification. The Paper inadequately 
acknowledges the active management of recapture risk through transaction-specific collateral arrangements, 
contractual terms, and monitoring mechanisms. Highlighting the sophisticated techniques insurers  employ to

actively manage these arrangements would provide a more accurate representation of how insurers effectively 
mitigate potential vulnerabilities in their reinsurance structures, and demonstrate the industry's commitment to 
robust risk management. 
The Geneva 
Association, 
International 
We acknowledge the supervisory interest in the increasing use of asset-intensive reinsurance (AIR) within the life 
insurance sector. The life insurance industry faces challenges from aging populations and evolving consumer 
needs, requiring efficient capital management. Third-party capital, expertise, and diversification benefits from AIR 
offer valuable solutions. 
 
Reinsurance is inherently a cross-border activity, enabling reinsurers to pool and spread complex risks globally. 
This approach prevents over-exposure, stabilizes local insurance markets, and leverages economies of scale to 
provide more affordable insurance. Global diversification is central to cross-border reinsurance and the role of 
reinsurance hubs. 
 
Global reinsurance groups utilize intragroup retrocessions for capital management and risk diversification. The 
ability to transfer risks and capital through affiliated transactions is vital for reinsurance operations. A global 
reinsurance group effectively moves risks to achieve diversification and allocates capital to support growth and 
claims, underscoring the importance of global diversification and capital fungibility.  
 
Reinsurers engage in sophisticated business-to-business (B2B) transactions with highly skilled clients. These 
arm's-length agreements are tailored to meet cedents' specific needs, fostering long-term partnerships. Cedents 
and brokers possess the expertise to conduct thorough due diligence when selecting financially strong reinsurers 
to support their programs. Reinsurance is a risk mitigating tool, that contributes to financial stability and 
enhances resilience.  
 
AIR is an effective solution for affiliate and nonaffiliate reinsurance transactions, enabling insurers to raise third -
party capital, optimize balance sheets, transfer long-dated liability and asset risks to specialized reinsurers, and 
mitigate credit or investment risks by shifting them to reinsurers who have comparative advantages in sourcing 
and managing alternative assets. 
 
Contrary to some perceptions, jurisdictions hosting the majority of AIR business already apply rigorous, front-
loaded oversight. For instance, life AIR treaties in Bermuda and the United States typically require supervisory 
pre-approval, employ structures like funds withheld or modified coinsurance for  collateral visibility, and operate 
under robust solvency regimes with capital coverage well above international minimums. These safeguards 
demonstrate that AIR is a mature mechanism for transferring long-term guarantees, releasing capital, and 
Noted. Revisions 
done. Many of 
the clarifications 
are done in 
various sections 
of the paper. 
Societal benefits 
of AIR are out of 
scope in this 
paper.

addressing the global retirement income gap, rather than a novel or "light touch" approach.  
 
The presence of a reinsurer in a jurisdiction using an economic prudential regime does not automatically indicate 
regulatory arbitrage. The IAIS itself acknowledged that both economic and non-economic capital standards can 
yield comparable outcomes. Therefore, arguments of regulatory arbitrage in this context are not supported. We 
advocate for a global level playing field and view regulatory fragmentation as a signi ficant concern. 
 
The IAIS has also highlighted the importance of supervisory authorities enhancing resources and capabilities to 
keep pace with market developments without hindering innovation in reinsurance solutions. It is crucial that 
supervisors have the financial means and expertise to match the sophistication of reinsurance transactions. 
International standards should prioritize strengthening Pillar 2, focusing on robust, continuous dialogue between 
insurers and supervisors regarding risk appetite, stress-testing, and governance, rather than solely on Pillar 1 
capital charges. This approach would be more proportionate and less distortive than additional blunt capital add -
ons, fostering innovations that benefit the market, competition, and consumers.  
 
Given the highly individualized nature of alternative assets and AIR transactions, which are shaped by local 
market dynamics, regulatory frameworks, and bespoke strategies, supervisory assessments must remain 
grounded in the specific practices of each jurisdiction and company. A standardized approach risks overlooking 
important safeguards already embedded in local regulations or firm-specific governance. 
 
Supervisory cooperation is another key consideration raised by the IAIS. Sustained dialogue between cedent 
and reinsurer supervisors can alleviate concerns about regulatory arbitrage in external reinsurance. The IAIS, 
along with regional accords and bilateral memoranda of understanding, provides platforms for this dialogue. For 
affiliated transactions, group supervisors and colleges of supervisors offer fora for joint assessment of group 
functioning. It is essential that the supervisory community utilizes these cooperative mechanisms to address 
specific issues rather than imposing blanket regulatory restrictions. 
 
We advocate for further removing barriers to supervisory cooperation. The global diversification of risks through 
cross-border reinsurance enhances societal resilience and financial system stability, supporting the provision of 
essential long-term living benefits through capital efficiency. Barriers such as mandatory collateral, asset 
localization, or pre-approval for third-country reinsurance lead to market fragmentation and inefficiencies. The 
IAIS should promote international convergence by eliminating these barriers, enabling the life insurance sector to 
help close the protection gap in aging societies.

We recommend emphasizing the broader context of AIR within the financial system. The draft issues paper 
appears to overemphasize supervisory concerns, while the policyholder and societal benefits of AIR, such as 
capital-efficient risk transfer, broader consumer reach, and a deeper market that addresses old-age protection 
gaps, deserve equal prominence. It would also be beneficial to contextualize current AIR volumes against the 
size of the global life reinsurance market and life insurers' aggregate general account assets to demonstrate that 
any potential risk to global financial stability is relatively small, as already noted in the paper. Simple ratios, such 
as AIR liabilities versus total life liabilities, would substantiate this point.  
 
Furthermore, elements like cross-border transactions, affiliate transactions, retrocessions, and collateral are not 
exclusive to asset-intensive reinsurance; they are common to all forms of reinsurance. The paper should avoid 
implying that reinsurance in general is a concern or that long-standing transactional elements have suddenly 
become problematic. Instead, it should focus on providing evidence of specific activities or practices that could 
be problematic under plausible scenarios. 
 
Finally, it is important to highlight existing risk mitigants, including diversification between the reinsurer's and 
insurer's business models and risk profiles, thorough due diligence on counterparty risk, contractual protections 
such as assets held in trust or funds-withheld structures, investment guidelines, and cedent recapture rights, as 
well as risk limits and Own Risk and Solvency Assessment (ORSA) analysis.  
 
In summary, we acknowledge that certain supervisors have legitimate reasons to monitor AIR transactions in 
their jurisdictions. However, it would be helpful for the paper to clarify that the prevalence of such transactions is 
currently limited to specific markets and is not a worldwide phenomenon. The issues highlighted are primarily 
microprudential in nature and can be addressed through established channels of supervisory cooperation. 
Relevant and plausible counterparty and concentration risk concerns are already covered within existing 
Insurance Core Principles (ICPs), indicating no material gaps that would necessitate a new interna tional 
standard-setting process. Both AIR and conventional reinsurance add valuable capacity to the global risk transfer 
system. Well-calibrated, consistent rules foster healthy competition and market resilience, whereas 
uncoordinated barriers to cross-border reinsurance risk fragmenting the market and ultimately limiting 
policyholder choice and cover quality. 
American Council 
of Life Insurers, 
United States

demand for insurance products that are designed for retirement saving”. The impact of this demographic shift 
isn’t limited to “alternative” assets, it is also closely linked with the increasing use of AIR, which can be an 
increasingly important mechanism to access different sources of capital for these asset -intensive lines of 
business.

The growing demand for asset-intensive products is driving a need for capital and specialized asset-liability 
matching experience. Over the last decade, AIR has served as a prime mechanism for insurers to support the 
growth of capital-intensive products that are a critical source of retirement security for many consumers. 
 
ACLI encourages the IAIS to update the Executive Summary, Introduction and Conclusion to include language 
similar to language used in Section 4, which acknowledges that there is growing societal need for long-term 
products designed for retirement savings and that AIR can be a critical source of capital for companies offering 
asset intensive insurance products.  
 
In addition, we encourage the IAIS to recognize that the provision of these products is consistent with the IAIS 
strategic goal of supporting insurance’s societal role of building resilience by helping supervisors’ address 
protection gaps. Including this context is not to imply that supervisors should abandon their supervisory duties or 
provide lesser oversight of AIR (or “alternative” assets) just because AIR is helping insurers serve an important 
societal need for retirement savings products.

As discussed in more detail below, we believe the paper does not adequately consider the drivers behind AIR, 
specifically the ways in which the life insurance industry is leveraging AIR to derisk their portfolios and increase

Finally, the paper seems to imply, with no substantive evidence, that the size of the reinsurance market presents 
a threat to the financial stability of the insurance industry, when that is simply not the case. As noted in the rece nt 
IAIS Global Insurance Market Reports, only 5.3% of global life transactions involve AIR, with Bermuda's making

Athene, USA 
The IAIS’s efforts to understand AIR now converge with a larger global challenge: mobilizing long -dated capital 
to close the retirement-savings gap, especially in the United States where guaranteed income needs are most 
acute. AIR should therefore be assessed as a capital-generation mechanism that strengthens solvency and

consumer outcomes - not as a vehicle for regulatory arbitrage. Policy conversations in the EU (e.g., Draghi, Sept. 
2024, “The Future of European Competitiveness” two-part report) and across Asia (Japan FSA, MAS, HKIA 
white-papers on private-capital mobilization) echo this imperative, signaling rising international interest in 
frameworks that safely channel private capital into retirement guarantees. 
 
Jurisdictional Regulatory Enhancements. The Bermuda Monetary Authority’s March 2025 paper, Insights and 
Reflections on Asset-Intensive Reinsurance, confirms that AIR can be executed safely when the right guardrails 
are in place: 
 
    1. Pre-transaction approval. Every closed-block life reinsurance deal requires BMA sign-off and consultation 
with the ceding regulator; transactions may be amended or denied. 
    2. Collateral as first-line defense. About 80% of treaties are fully collateralized through funds -withheld, 
modified coinsurance or trust accounts, ensuring ring-fenced assets remain visible and enforceable. 
    3. Capital and liquidity. At year-end 2023, Bermuda long-term reinsurers reported a median solvency ratio of 
259% and post-stress liquidity coverage of 418% – well above minimum thresholds. 
    4. High-quality portfolios. Roughly 77% of assets held by these reinsurers are investment grade or higher, 
diversified across corporates, mortgages, infrastructure loans and investment grade structured credit.  
    5. Cross-border cooperation. Bermuda’s Solvency II equivalence and NAIC reciprocal-jurisdiction status 
facilitate data exchange, joint inspections and supervisory colleges. 
 
These safeguards underscore that prudential outcomes depend on rules and oversight, not domicile.  
 
OBJECTIVE PRACTICAL SYSTEMIC CONSIDERATIONS 
    1. AIR covers only about five percent of global life-insurance provisions, with Bermuda’s share under two 
percent. 
    2. Funding is long-term equity and surplus notes-so there is no reliance on short-term wholesale markets. 
    3. During recent stress episodes (the 2020 liquidity shock and the 2022-23 rate spike), Bermuda reinsurers 
met all collateral calls and maintained solvency well above 200%, demonstrating that AIR absorbs rather than 
transmits market shocks. 
 
 
RECCOMENDATIONS TO THE IAIAS.  We suggest that the Issues Paper be amended to:  
    1. Recognize AIR as a legitimate, prudentially sound capital-formation tool that helps address the global 
retirement-security gap.

2. Acknowledge best-practice safeguards, including regulatory pre-approval, collateralization, rigorous ALM 
stress testing, transparent supervisory colleges, instead of imposing label-based restrictions. 
    3. Facilitate knowledge transfer so jurisdictions in Europe and Asia that are seeking new capital -generation 
mechanisms can adapt proven AIR frameworks to their own markets. 
Insurance Europe, 
Europe 
The life insurance sector contends with the challenges posed by ageing societies while endeavouring to satisfy 
the needs of consumers. Addressing these challenges requires making continuous progress in ways to manage 
a finite amount of capital efficiently. Third-party capital, expertise and diversification benefits brought by AIR is 
one of the solutions.   
 
The cross-border nature of reinsurance brings benefits including geographic diversification, access to specialised 
risk capacity, and global capital efficiency, which in turn helps mitigate concentration and systemic risks.  
 
Global reinsurers rely on intragroup retrocessions to achieve global diversification. The transferability of risks and 
capital through affiliated transactions is indispensable for the business of reinsurance. A global reinsurance 
group functions by moving risks where they diversify within the group and allocating capital where it’s needed to 
support the growth of the activity and pay claims. Here again, affiliated retrocessions have ev erything to do with 
global diversification and fungibility of capital.     
 
Reinsurers have highly skilled and knowledgeable risk professionals as clients. These B2B transactions are 
tailored at arm’s length to respond to the needs of the cedents, which become long -term partners. Cedents and 
brokers have the expertise and processes in place to make the necessary due diligence when selecting the pool 
of reinsurers with the financial strength to safely support their reinsurance programmes.  
 
Contrary to the impression that asset intensive reinsurance is only now attracting supervisory scrutiny, the 
jurisdictions that host the bulk of this business already apply stringent, front-loaded oversight. In both Bermuda 
and the United States, life AIR treaties generally require supervisory preapproval, are written through funds 
withheld or modified coinsurance structures that give ceding insurers full collateral visibility, and sit under 
solvency regimes whose capital coverage is well above IAIS minima (Bermuda Class E reinsurers, for example, 
reported a median Bermuda Solvency Capital Requirement (BSCR) ratio of roughly 259 per cent in 2023).  
 
In addition to the examples cited from the US and Bermuda, the UK has also taken a clear and structured 
approach to AIR. The Prudential Regulation Authority’s (PRA's) publication of policy statement PS13/24 and 
supervisory statement SS5/24, for example, set out the regulatory expectations in respect of insurers entering 
into or holding funded reinsurance arrangements as cedants.
