sections of the 
Paper.

AIR has developed into a well-established market tool, subject to structured oversight in key jurisdictions. 
Insurance Europe, Europe support continued supervisory engagement to ensure that oversight keeps pace with 
market evolution, for transferring long dated guarantees, releasing capital and helping to close the global 
retirement income gap. 
 
The fact that a reinsurer is based in a jurisdiction using an economic prudential regime is not indicative of 
regulatory arbitrage. The IAIS itself adopted an economic International Capital Standard and determined that a 
non-economic US Aggregation Method provided a basis for a comparable outcome. This context does not 
support the argument of regulatory arbitrage.  
 
However, the IAIS also raised important considerations relating to the need for supervisory authorities to have 
appropriate capabilities to keep abreast of market developments without stifling innovations in reinsurance 
solutions.  
 
Another important consideration raised by the IAIS is about supervisory cooperation. Perception of regulatory 
arbitrage through external reinsurance can be dismissed through sustained dialogue between superv isors of the 
cedents and supervisors of the reinsurers. The IAIS provides a global platform for this dialogue, in addition to 
regional accords and bilateral Memorandum of Understanding. Regarding affiliated transactions, the group 
supervisor has a holistic view, and colleges of supervisors are allowing joint assessment of the functioning of the 
group. It is paramount that the supervisory community uses the levers of cooperation to address any specific 
issues instead of resorting to blanket regulatory restrictions.    
 
The global diversification of risks through cross-border reinsurance benefits the resilience of societies and the 
stability of financial systems. It supports the provision of much needed long-term living benefits through capital 
efficiency. Barriers to cross-border reinsurance, such as mandatory collaterals, localisation of assets or pre-
approval for third-country reinsurance, lead to market fragmentation and inefficiencies. It is essential that the IAIS 
promotes international convergence through the elimination of trade barriers, and in such a way that the life 
insurance sector can play its part in closing the protection gap in ageing societies.  
 
As a result, Insurance Europe, Europe considers that the current ICPs are adequate to address issues arising 
from reinsurance arrangements in general and AIR in particular. An element of the strength of the ICPs should 
be that they are stable over time.

Insurance Europe, Europe acknowledges that targeted clarifications could still add value without reopening core 
principles. For example, Insurance Europe, Europe considers that the IAIS should tighten its definition of AIR to 
be more targeted, and proportionate, in its analysis.  
 
A transfer of reserves should be considered asset-intensive when it gives rise to a significant transfer of assets 
(eg >5bn) and the allocation towards illiquid asset classes is very high compared to the allocation of assets 
backing a traditional block of business transfer. AIR should not include reinsurance structures that only transfer 
tail asset risk exposure of life blocks. These considerations should be a starting point for regulatory discussions, 
rather than a standard or ICP revision.  
 
Application of proportionality and risk-based regulatory oversight is key. The definition of AIR proposed above 
would not be applicable in all cases. For example, any potential regulatory classification of AIR would also need 
to consider the context of the relevant regulatory regime, the materiality of the transaction on the reinsurer’s 
balance sheet  and asset mix.

We believe this section, and the Issues Paper more broadly, does not adequately discuss the market forces that 
are contributing to the growth in AIR, including the overall globalization of reinsurance that regulators have 
overseen.  For the recent growth in AIR, the Issues Paper adeptly highlights that demographic trends and 
consumer demand for asset intensive products are among the drivers.  Given the protection gaps societies are 
facing, these factors are expected to grow and drive greater need for the so lutions life insurers can provide, 
which should be acknowledged and discussed in the paper.  We also believe is important to provide context on 
the scale of the AIR market relative to the overall life insurance market.  For example, recent IAIS Global 
Insurance Market Reports estimate that only ~5.3% of global life provisions involve AIR, with Bermuda’s share at 
around ~1.8%. While the AIR market is growing, it remains modest.  
 
Combined with the fact that insurance is generally not systemic, this indicates  that Bermuda does not create or 
exacerbate systemic risk. Indeed, reinsurers act as risk absorbers rather than risk transmitters. Bermuda AIR 
reinsurers have demonstrated resilience through various stress events, including the COVID 19 pandemic, 
Noted. The text 
has been revised 
for clarification

market volatility and the unprecedented rise in interest rates, which led to spikes in policy lapses. Bermuda AIR 
reinsurers predominantly operate on a collateralized basis, maintain strong liquidity, and hold diversified, high 
quality investment portfolios. 
 
We note that AIR is enabling insurers to achieve the following objectives, among others, that should be 
acknowledged in the opening framing of Section 4: 
1. AIR provides an effective mechanism for accessing third-party capital that insurers need to address protection 
gaps, particularly as public equity has retreated from certain lines of business;  
2. AIR enhances insurer’s ability to manage risks related to certain liabilities (e.g., align valuation of assets and 
liabilities under a market-based solvency framework); 
3. AIR frees up capital that can be used to support other strategic priorities; 
4. AIR allows cedents to exit certain markets that no longer align with their strategic priorities; and  
5. AIR allows access to greater investment flexibility and/or expertise enabling insurers to capture illiquidity 
premiums that can both enhance returns and pricing of coverage for consumers.  
 
Although some of the above objectives are among the traditional benefits of reinsurance, AIR has become an 
important resource for the life insurance sector as it seeks to grow and deliver affordable solutions against a 
backdrop of demographics and trends that are making them increasingly necessary for society (the estimated 
global retirement gap exceeds $50 trillion). 
Athora, Bermuda 
Overarching comment on the framing of the paper 
• Throughout the paper, references to complexity and recent market developments are often framed with a 
degree of scepticism. But what is being labelled as “complexity” within the paper, could just as easily be viewed 
as “progress”. The developments can be seen as part of the insurance industry’s natural, and perhaps 
necessary, evolution in response to not only the plain desire to transfer risks and share the capital burden but 
also a competitive financial landscape as noted in Sigma 2/2024.  
• If insurance solutions don’t offer attractive returns, and policyholders aren’t compelled to buy insurance, they 
may simply take their money elsewhere, as seen in the UK following pension freedoms in 2015, when annuity 
sales dropped precipitously, leaving the retirement savings gaps problem unaddressed.  
• The policy question is therefore whether the complexity should be embraced while being responsibly regulated 
to allow the industry to adapt and create appropriate sharing of risk and support for policyholders.. 
2. Origins of complexity 
• Elsewhere in the paper, complexity is framed as a negative aspect of reinsurance arrangements. Complexity in 
retrocessions should not be viewed as inherently problematic or self-serving.  
• The complexity often arises as a necessary response to the underlying complexity of the risks being transferred 
Noted. Revision 
done.

and the structure of the insurance and reinsurance markets themselves.  
• Simple solutions are preferrable but, in many cases, effective risk transfer requires sophisticated, bespoke 
arrangements. The challenge is to ensure it is undertaken and managed in a well-controlled manner.  
3. Differences in approaches to reserving 
• As outlined in Section 4.2, there can be variations in reserves, capital requirements, and investment flexibility 
across different jurisdictions, particularly where the business is being ceded from and to – for example between 
Europe and Bermuda. 
• As acknowledged in Section 4.2.1.1, actuarial judgment regarding assumptions can vary, which, however, is 
not systematic and, in particular, not dependent on which of the two solvency systems applies. This typically 
involves prudent adjustments on top of best estimate assumptions.  
• In terms of the valuation of policyholder liabilities (or the reinsurance treaty liabilities), the Bermuda EBS 
employs two valuation methods: the Standard Approach and the Scenario Based Approach (SBA), as detailed in 
Table 2. 
• The Standard Approach for European business ceded to a Bermuda reinsurer has recently been updated to 
use the same discount rate as Solvency II EIOPA+VA. Consequently, the Standard Approach in Bermuda will 
materially result in the same BEL as under Solvency II. 
• The SBA, however, is similar to the Matching Adjustment in Solvency II in some respects and to the valuation of 
liabilities in other jurisdictions such as Canada. It allows the reinsurer to take credit for the illiquidity premium 
specific to its actual assets and liabilities, rather than the VA, which is based on broader market spreads and 
would be more volatile over time. 
• The SBA is a sophisticated balance sheet projection approach to valuing liabilities. It encourages well -thought-
out Asset and Liability matching, as the (re)insurer is able to take credit for the illiquidity premium on assets 
backing liabilities and full credit is only given where assets match the liabilities very well. If the business is not 
duration matched (and this includes key-rate matching), then the credit taken for the illiquidity premium is 
reduced via the use of 8 different interest rate scenarios, similar to 1-standard deviation sensitivities, as indicated 
in Table 2 (ALM mismatch haircut). The SBA also allows for further prudence if lapse -sensitive products are 
included in the valuation, particularly where those products have shown volatile lapse rates that are difficult to 
predict. 
• The SBA is subject to a rigorous approval process, with final approval by the BMA required before use in 
liability valuation (the default valuation methodology being the Standard Approach). The SBA model is owned 
and signed off by the Board of the relevant company, and multiple annual attestations are required regarding risk 
management, investment strategy, and appropriateness of assumptions, signed off by the CEO, CRO, CIO, and 
Chief Actuary. 
• The thorough governance required for the initial use of the SBA and ongoing mainte nance and review of the

SBA and its assumptions and modelled investment strategy mean that the use of the SBA in the valuation of the 
relevant liabilities should be expected to be appropriate, sufficiently prudent, and well -managed. 
• In conclusion, the SBA valuation approach can result in differences in BEL versus other jurisdictions. However, 
as discussed above and in Section 4.2.1.2, there is a comprehensive set of regulatory controls to ensure the 
valuation under the SBA is appropriate and the liabilities are not being undervalued.  
4. Differences in approaches to setting capital requirements 
• The Bermuda Solvency Capital Requirement (BSCR), as noted in Table 2, is a mix of factor -based and 
prescribed shock-based approaches. For example, lapse risk is shock-based, long-term interest rate risk can be 
shock or factor-based, and Fixed Income investment risk is factor-based. 
• Other jurisdictions feature various shock or factor-based approaches as designated in Table 2.  No single 
calculation is perfect; however, the BSCR appropriately reflects the asset risk on the balance sheet, the lapse 
risk capital, and other risks. 
• The Bermuda regulator, similar to Solvency II, also has the ability to impose capital add -ons where required and 
is known to have thorough discussions with various European regulators when new transactions are being 
discussed. 
• Any block deal is also now subject to discussions, including a comparison of cedant capital requirements and 
reinsurer capital requirements. 
• It should be recognized that no single regime is a perfect fit for all insurers. Therefore, differences between 
different jurisdictions should be expected. If a market standard insurer in one jurisdiction, such as Bermuda, is 
not subject to similar risks as those faced by a market standard insurer under a different regime, such as 
Solvency II, then there may be differences in capital requirements (i.e. the SII Standard Formula is based off the 
risks of a market standard insurer across Europe, whereas the Bermuda BSCR will be calibrated off the risks 
seen by a standard insurer within Bermuda).  
5. Concerns regarding capitalisation upon recapture 
• Section 4.1.3 identifies a potential risk associated with “re-establishing required capital” upon recapture. The 
framing of the risk appears to presume that the ceding insurer had sufficient capital prior to the establishment of 
the reinsurance treaty.  
• Whilst it may be true that certain reinsurance treaties are established to transfer unwanted risk, thereby 
allowing the cedant to release capital, reinsurance may often result as a necessity due to capital constraints on 
the part of the cedant. If recapture occurs, the cedant will face a capital challenge, but this is not a new risk – it is 
a reversion to the risk position that existed pre-reinsurance.  
• As such, the risk should not be viewed as arising from the reinsurance arrangement, rather the reinsurance 
arrangement helped mitigate a pre-existing risk.  
• For instance, it is a well-established practice for direct insurers in the UK bulk purchase annuity spa ce to write

transactions with back-to-back reinsurance arrangements already in place, particularly for large transactions. 
This opens up capacity and allows the direct writer to service the needs of the market (pension schemes in this 
example) that may otherwise not be met.  
• The risk of reinsurer default and recapture remains, but this should not be overstated relative to the important 
role the reinsurer’s capacity plays in the overall system. Importantly, the reinsurers themselves are well -
capitalised and highly-regulated entities – as such, any capital relief provided to cedants is met by the capital of 
the reinsurer; recapture (in many cases) would only happen once the reinsurers own capital stock has been 
critically diminished – leaving the cedant in the same position as if it had retained and capitalised the risk itself. 
6. Framing of the use of “alternative” assets 
• There is an assertion that AIR is leading to increased exposure to “alternative” assets. However, this is not just 
a trend amongst asset-intensive reinsurers. Large direct writers in certain jurisdictions (e.g. UK) have well -
established teams dedicated to the sourcing of illiquid, private assets to support their business strategy, including 
infrastructure debt and direct lending. Equity Release Mortgages are also a common feature in UK insurer 
investment portfolios, which require complex modelling and structuring to meet Matching Adjustment 
requirements. As such, complexity in investment portfolios does not arise only from cross -border AIR 
arrangements. 
7. Drivers of affiliate reinsurance 
• Section 4.1.2 of the document appears to suggest that, if there is no risk transfer outside of an insurance group 
or other “discernible economic benefits”, then affiliated reinsurance is likely to be motivated by jurisdictional 
differences in regulation.  
• This overlooks important other reasons why reinsurance may be established between affiliated parties, this 
includes: 
o Fungibility of capital: the paper focuses on possible differences in required capital across ju risdictions, but the 
availability of capital across various entities within an insurance group often is a major driver of affiliate 
reinsurance. There may be a variety of constraints that mean the surplus regulatory capital in one entity cannot 
be remitted to the parent and redeployed across the group, including IFRS/GAAP earnings -related constraints. 
Affiliate reinsurance may allow the insurance group to more effectively deploy the surplus, albeit non -fungible, 
regulatory capital that exists across the various balance sheets of the subsidiary entities. 
o Expertise: the reinsurance may allow different parties within the wider insurance group to specialise in their 
areas of expertise. For example, the reinsurer may  be established to focus on business that is legacy to the 
cedant, or multiple cedants within the group, but core to the (affiliated) reinsurer 
o Management of constraints: the reinsurer may have a simpler set of constraints against which is has to 
optimise or manage its balance sheet. For example, a reinsurer in Bermuda whose primary constraint is its EBS 
balance sheet, may be able to focus solely on the SBA and thereby improve cash flow matching – and protection

for policyholders, ultimately – in comparison to cedant insurers who may be trying to balance different regulatory 
and GAAP requirements 
o Access to 3rd party capital: this is discussed elsewhere in the paper, but the availability of capital flows may be 
greater in certain jurisdictions than in others and may be accessed through, for example, sidecars.  
8. Sourcing of capital 
• AIR plays an important role in expanding market capacity by attracting new sources of capital – particularly 
investors who may be interested in targeted exposure to specific lines of insurance risk (e.g. long -term savings, 
pension annuities). By contrast to large insurance conglomerates, where capital is exposed to a broad range of 
underwriting / operational risks, AIR providers can offer a more focussed and transparent investment proposition, 
with the possibility for greater leverage of specific skills and expertise and the prospect of stable returns. This 
clarity appeals to specialist investors who may not otherwise participate in more traditional insurance markets, 
thereby unlocking capacity that would not otherwise exist within the overall system.  
9. Acknowledgement of systemic benefits 
• We note the regulatory concerns highlighted in section 4.3.1, but it may also be informative to consider the 
counterfactual – i.e. what concerns might be expressed in a market where the reinsurance sector had not 
developed to the extent that it has in other markets. In such a scenario, concerns would likely centre around the 
growing concentrations of risk among a relatively small number of direct writers, limited access to external 
capital, unmet demand for derisking solutions and unmet demand by the society for secure and, yet attractive, 
savings solutions. The evolution of the reinsurance market has brought more capital into the overall system, from 
a wider pool of market participants, and transferred risk more broadly across the industry. In the process of 
acknowledging the potential risks introduced by market developments, a balanced view should be  struck which 
weighs these risks against the systemic benefits brought about by those same developments  
Global 
Communications 
Group, United 
States 
From an industry perspective, GCG would like to emphasize that reinsurance – including cross-border AIR – is 
an essential risk management tool for life insurers. It can improve policyholder protection by diversifying risk and 
by moving liabilities to specialized entities that are sometimes better equipped to manage certain risks (for 
example, a reinsurer focusing on longevity risk might manage a pension annuity block more efficiently). It also 
allows for capital to be freed and redeployed, which can support new business growth or innovation in products. 
We therefore caution against approaches that might unnecessarily restrict or stigmatize the use of reinsurance.  
 
Instead, the goal should be to ensure reinsurance is done in a sound way. In that regard, GCG supports the 
IAIS’s view that robust supervisory cooperation and information sharing among jurisdictions is critical. When 
liabilities move from one jurisdiction to another, the supervisors in both the ceding and assuming jurisdictions 
need to coordinate closely. The IAIS’s Multilateral Memorandum of Understanding (MMoU) is a useful tool that 
already facilitates information exchange among many insurance supervisors. Some jurisdictions have also 
Noted. The text 
has been revised 
for clarification.

developed useful tools and frameworks to promote cross-border communication and collaboration. The Issues 
Paper mentions the need to improve information sharing and cross-border supervisory oversight in light of these 
trends– GCG strongly agrees.  
 
Another aspect we urge the IAIS to consider is the risk management and governance at insurers engaging in 
AIR. The Issues Paper notes the importance of ensuring appropriate expertise and risk management when 
dealing with complex reinsurance structures. Indeed, from the industry side, companies that enter into these 
arrangements typically conduct extensive due diligence on counterparties, set up trust accounts or collateral 
arrangements to secure obligations, and plan for contingency actions (like recapture or rest ructure of the deal) if 
conditions change.  
 
We believe the Paper could give more attention to such mitigating measures. For instance, in many cross -border 
reinsurance transactions for life and annuity products, the reinsurer provides collateral to the ced ant (often in the 
form of high-quality assets held in trust) to guarantee the payment of reinsured liabilities. This significantly 
reduces counterparty credit risk – effectively, the cedant holds security that can be used to fulfil policyholder 
claims if needed. Likewise, deals often include recapture clauses that allow the ceding insurer to take back the 
business under certain conditions (which can be a safety valve if the reinsurer’s creditworthiness deteriorates). 
By highlighting these practices, the IAIS can acknowledge that the industry has not been complacent in the face 
of these new structures; rather, firms and supervisors have been adapting contractual and regulatory tools to 
keep risks in check. The analysis should reflect a full spectrum of risk mitigants already in play, to better identify 
what additional measures might be necessary.  
 
We also echo GCG stakeholders’ queries about whether sufficient supervisory resources and expertise are 
allocated to monitor these often complex arrangements. IAIS could consider providing training or best practice 
workshops for supervisors on assessing AIR transactions, drawing on lessons from those jurisdictions that have 
dealt heavily in these deals (for example, the U.S., Bermuda, Cayman, and European jurisdictio ns that have 
seen notable activity). This kind of capacity building, championed by the IAIS, would be a prudent next step to 
ensure effective oversight worldwide. 
Pinpoint Policy 
Institute, United 
States 
Section 4 – Rising adoption of AIR in the life insurance sector 
 
The IAIS Issues Paper also examines the rise of asset-intensive or funded reinsurance (AIR) transactions, which 
involve the transfer of significant investment risk associated with life insurance liabilities to reinsurers. These 
transactions are especially common for products like fixed annuities and universal life insurance, where insurers 
cede liabilities (along with supporting assets or funds) to a reinsurer in exchange for capital relief an d risk 
Noted. The text 
has been revised 
for clarification.

diversification. The IAIS notes that such arrangements can bring benefits: they free up capital for the ceding 
insurer and provide access to a wider range of investments through the reinsurer but introduce additional 
considerations.  But the U.S. insurance sector has been a frontrunner in utilizing forms of AIR, notably through 
captive reinsurance affiliates and cross-border reinsurance deals. 
 
It is essential to place AIR in the proper context of U.S. regulation. The growth of affiliated reinsurance in the  
U.S. life market during the 2000s and 2010s was largely a response to reserve requirements that were perceived 
as excessively conservative for certain life products (for example, Regulation XXX and AXXX for term life and 
universal life).  U.S. regulators responded decisively to this evolution. Rather than banning such reinsurance 
(which could have had unintended consequences), rigorous standards were established to govern it. Over the 
past decade, oversight of AIR has been strengthened through new frameworks for credit for reinsurance, 
collateralization standards, Form D and Form E disclosures, and the development of the Group Capital 
Calculation. Reflecting the state-based regulatory model, some U.S. states have more rigorous approaches still. 
There is no evidence that AIR, in the U.S. context, has created financial instability.  
 
Life insurers can still utilize AIR structures to manage their risks and capital, but under increased scrutiny. The 
IAIS paper raises the concern that differences in regulatory frameworks across jurisdictions drive the rise in AIR. 
Indeed, the IAIS’s survey of supervisors indicates a common emphasis on robust risk management of AIR, even 
if implementation varies.  IAIS should highlight best practices, such as the U.S. approach of r equiring collateral 
and spreading reinsurance among well-capitalized counterparties. Any IAIS recommendation that limits the use 
of AIR globally or imposes uniform capital charges on such reinsurance without regard to local reserve credit 
rules, however, would be misguided. The U.S. experience shows that it is possible to allow insurers to obtain 
legitimate capital relief from reinsurance while maintaining policyholder protection, given proper regulatory 
oversight.  
 
AIR can serve as a valuable tool that, when supervised correctly, can enhance insurers’ risk distribution and 
financial flexibility. A rigid one-size-fits-all approach is unworkable, and the IAIS should be careful not to brand 
these practices as inherently problematic but rather work with jurisdictions to ensure that adequate safeguards. 
The U.S. model can serve as a guide in this respect. 
Global Atlantic 
Financial Group, 
United States 
The IAIS should avoid implying that AIR poses systemic risks. Current AIR volumes are limited and concentrated 
in well-regulated jurisdictions. Like alternative assets, AIR should be evaluated based on specific transaction 
characteristics and supervisory safeguards. The benefits of AIR—such as capital formation, risk management, 
and enhanced product offerings—deserve equal emphasis.

has been revised 
for clarification in 
section 5.

The Geneva

We do not support the introduction of “asset-intensive reinsurance” (AIR) as a new, stand-alone supervisory 
label. The existing ICPs already capture the underlying risks, and a new label risks duplicating, and potentially 
conflicting with, those standards.  
 
If the IAIS nevertheless chooses to adopt the term, we strongly urge the IAIS to ensure the approach it pursues 
adheres to the principle of proportionality and only capture those transactions that are material.  
 
We reiterate that reinsurance structures transferring only remote asset-risk exposure of life and annuity blocks do 
not constitute AIR.  
 
Life insurers provide long-term guarantees, necessitating corresponding long-term assets. However, the 
imbalance created by valuing liabilities at book value and assets at market value in various jurisdictions 
introduces non-economic balance sheet volatility. This often leads to business decisions favoring reinsurance.  
 
We find the assumptions surrounding affiliated transactions to be overly simplistic. These transactions are 
frequently undertaken to maintain control over risk management and asset allocation for the associated business 
blocks. Furthermore, they offer economic benefits beyond what might be perceived as capital arbitrage, for

example to efficiently raise third-party capital. Moreover, there is a clear need for a framework that better 
accommodates the fundamental business requirements for matching assets and liabilities in such transactions.  
 
The current paper does not differentiate between AIR for in-force blocks and for flow business, which refers to 
new policies issued after an agreement's effective date. This distinction is crucial as the drivers and risks can 
differ significantly between the two. For flow business, the collective goal of all participants—direct-writing 
companies, AIR reinsurers, and their investment managers—is to empower the direct-writing company to 
compete effectively, offering attractively priced insurance products that help bridge the societal protection  gap. In 
contrast, for in-force business, it is a normal for companies to adjust their asset portfolios as investment 
strategies evolve, and an active reinsurance market for in-force liabilities helps direct writers to manage their 
strategic direction. Given the enduring customer relationships in the life sector, mergers and acquisitions are less 
common, making reinsurance an important mechanism for restructuring business models while preserving 
customer relationships.

United States 
AIR may enhance macroeconomic and societal resilience by expanding capacity which ultimately results in more 
innovative products available to consumers, and allowing insurers to enter or exit lines of business as needed.  
 
AIR unlocks capital and mitigates risk in several ways. It enhances capital efficiency and strengthens balance 
sheets by releasing "trapped" capital. Insurers can free up capital by ceding mature or less -core blocks to 
reinsurers that excel in matching illiquid, long-dated liabilities with appropriate assets. Private capital-backed 
reinsurers often maintain strong asset underwriting and ALM capabilities, capturing illiquidity premiums that 
enhance returns while respecting solvency requirements. AIR can also reduce investment and credit risk s by 
shifting them to reinsurers who may have comparative advantages in sourcing and managing “alternative” 
assets. Large transactions can also allow insurers to reconfigure leverage ratios, reduce asset –liability 
mismatches, and reinforce solvency coverage. 
 
As noted in Section 4.1.8., jurisdictional differences are one potential driver in the use of AIR, but they are not the 
only driver. AIR deals are bespoke and can be complex, and there can be more than one driver for each 
transaction. Jurisdictional supervisory reviews can assess all transactions individually, with a focus towards 
understanding the objectives of each transaction, while recognizing that global standard setters can have a valid 
interest in understanding consistent themes. 
 
I. Comments on Section 4.1.3 Investment Flexibility, Guidelines and Recapture risk  
 
ACLI appreciates this section’s explanation of how cedents can use investment guidelines to mitigate risk in the

event of recapture by limiting the reinsurers’ investment flexibility in accordance with the risk tolerance of the 
ceding company’s reinsurance program. ACLI agrees that recapture risk is highly dependent on the nature of the 
AIR contract and collateral structure. The bespoke nature of AIR treaties underlines the importance for 
supervisors to review each transaction on its merits. ACLI recommends adding a sentence noting that collateral 
levels in AIR transactions are often over-collateralized, which is another mitigant in the event of a stress

***Recommended edit to Section 4.1.3 (para. 4): 
 ACLI recommends adding this language to the end of paragraph 4 (p. 36): <<"Collateral levels may be 
established above statutory levels, to provide additional safeguards against potential shortfalls in stress 
scenarios.”>> 
 
II. Comments on Section 4.1.6.4 Coinsurance with Funds Withheld and Section 4.1.6.5 Modified Coinsurance  
 
ACLI appreciates that the Paper highlights the collateralized nature of many AIR tr ansactions . ModCo or funds-
withheld structures prevent "asset flight" and typically deploy stable, ring-fenced collateral. Asset retention, with 
appropriate transparency and disclosure requirements, can provide cedents and regulators with visibility into the 
quality of the assets backing the transaction. ModCo and funds-withheld treaties also structurally mitigate against 
fault-based recapture risks. 
In the U.S. cedents must obtain regulatory approvals for affiliate transactions under the Insurance Holding  
Company Systems Act. Supervisory colleges and cross-border collaboration, allow involved jurisdictions to 
appropriately regulate ceding companies and reinsurers, safeguarding policyholders globally.  
 
III. Comments on Section 4.1.8 Drivers of AIR  
 
Asset-intensive reinsurance (AIR) transactions are deeply influenced by the diverse market and regulatory 
environments across countries and the specific business models of individual firms.  
AIR can help address both the retirement protection gap and the capital gap. The rising demand for long-term 
insurance products, driven by global demographic shifts and economic factors such as higher interest rates, has 
spurred rapid growth in asset-intensive insurance. These guaranteed or savings-type products have a need for 
capital and specialized asset liability management (ALM) expertise. Concurrently, many publicly listed insurers 
have scaled back participation in guaranteed products, returning billions in capital to shareholders. As public 
capital retreated from supporting certain lines of business, AIR has become a prime mechanism for insurers to 
access private sources of capital.

ACLI has expanded on several benefits of AIR, below: 
 
The use of AIR aligns with the IAIS's understanding of the societal role of insurance. As written in the 2025 -2029 
IAIS Strategic Plan, “At its essence, insurance exists to build societal resilience by offering risk management, 
pooling and diversification of risk, and mitigation of adversities.” AIR helps build societal resilience and diversifies 
risk. The paper describes the benefits cedents derive from AIR, including risk reduction, immediate profit 
conversion, indirect access to broader investable assets, and ease of investment management. For reinsurers, 
the benefits include growth of assets under management, profit potential, and the ability to leverage investment 
expertise. 
 
Other benefits include enhancing macroeconomic resilience by drawing on more resources to respond to a loss, 
expanding capacity which ultimately results in more innovative products available to consumers, and allowing 
insurers to enter or exit lines of business as needed. As noted in Section 4.1.8., jurisdictional differences are one 
potential driver in the use of AIR, but they are not the only driver. AIR deals are bespoke and can be complex, so 
supervisors should assess all transactions individually, with a focus towards unders tanding the objectives of 
each transaction. ACLI agrees with the Paper’s findings that there may be more than one driver for each 
transaction. 
Everlake Life, US 
AIR is a vehicle important for capital formation critical to meeting the growing demographic changes and 
consumer demands.  It also mitigates risk in several ways. 
 
It enhances capital efficiency and strengthens balance sheets by releasing "trapped" capital.  
 
Insurers can free up capital by ceding mature or less-core blocks to reinsurers that excel in matching illiquid, 
long-dated liabilities with appropriate assets. Reinsurers maintain strong asset underwriting and ALM 
capabilities, often capturing illiquidity premiums that enhance returns while protecting consumers. AIR can also 
reduce investment and credit risks by shifting them to reinsurers who may have comparative advantages in 
sourcing and managing “alternative” assets. 
Noted.  
Athene, USA 
AIR, especially in Bermuda, exemplifies how sound prudential frameworks, appropriate tax treatment and 
transparent structures can attract fresh institutional capital into the U.S. insurance market -capital crucial for 
meeting growing retirement liabilities.  
 
Bermuda’s recognized solvency regime, equivalences with both U.S. and EU standards, and extensive 
collateralization requirements collectively ensure that AIR transactions strike an optimal balance between capital 
Noted. Points are 
Bermuda specific 
but may not apply 
to all jurisdictions 
party to a 
reinsurance

accessibility and policyholder protection. While certain illustrations of recapture risk can appear in theoretical 
discussions, day-to-day practice is governed by rigorous local standards, ensuring that AIR remains an efficient 
and secure channel for global capital inflows. 
 
HOW AIR ATTRACTS NEW CAPITAL 
    1. Well-Regulated Jurisdiction. Bermuda adheres to international regulatory standards, having achieved both 
U.S. NAIC qualified/reciprocal jurisdiction status and EU Solvency II equivalence. These recogn itions assure 
investors and ceding insurers that AIR transactions remain subject to high prudential oversight and demonstrate 
that Bermuda’s supervisory practices for AIR meet, or exceed, standards in major onshore markets.  
 
    2. Attractive for Foreign Investors. Bermuda’s regulatory strength and clarity and specialized insurance 
industry infrastructure reduce frictional costs, simplifying the process of raising capital from foreign sources to 
support US insurers. 
 
    3. Facilitating Global Investment. Bermuda-licensed reinsurers can channel third-party foreign capital into US 
insurance markets through tax-efficient structures, without incurring dividend withholding taxes that apply to 
direct investment into U.S. insurance companies.   
       - The U.S. insurance industry has struggled to raise capital domestically. Many U.S. insurers, including 
Athene, USA, have established Bermuda-based reinsurers to raise foreign capital to support domestic 
annuitants. Reinsurance in Bermuda allows for capital formation and attractive investment to foreign third parties, 
with tax rates on returns comparable to U.S. debt (e.g., bonds).  
       - From an investor perspective, reinsurance is a capital investing activity that is akin to investing in stocks, 
bonds, or other investments.  As is the case with traditional equity and debt investing, investors are looking to 
earn passive returns.  In the case of reinsurance, the primary insurer will only get the desired capital risk transfer 
if the underlying risks are reinsured to a regulated insurance entity.  State-based regulation appropriately 
requires strong capitalization to support promises to policyholders. As a result, investors must invest in 
reinsurance transactions through equity ownership in a reinsurance company.  
       - U.S. tax rules encourage global investment in the U.S. economy via fixed income capital markets at low 
cost of capital.  Under current law, foreign investors are not subject to U.S. withholding tax on substantially all 
debt investments in U.S. companies.  These rules have helped spur the availability of credit to U.S. businesses.  
To attract loss absorbing capital from foreign investors with similar tax treatment to debt investment, reinsurance 
companies must be found outside of the U.S. in jurisdictions that do not impose withholding tax on dividend 
payments. 
       - Capital raised in offshore jurisdictions achieves two important outcomes – two features that are critical to 
transaction. 
Proportionately 
applies.

capital formation to support the U.S. retirement gap – (1) allowing capital to be raised at slightly lower corporate 
income rate (15% in Bermuda versus 21% in the U.S. versus 0% in Cayman) and (2) removing the punitive 30% 
equity dividend withholding tax that applies to capital returned to equity investors around the world in jurisdictions 
that do not have a tax treaty with the U.S. It is important to note that, with respect to (2), our estimates are that as 
much as 75% of the capital raised offshore are from these countries. 
       - This is why such capital raised to support the (re)insurance industry globally is raised offshore. Given 
significant growth in retirees over the next decade, insurers will require more capital in the years ahead.  
       - Allowing safe, regulated avenues to raise capital effectively, as the U.S. has done, is vital for the health and 
growth of this industry. 
 
TRANSPARENCY VIA MODCO AND FUNDS-WITHHELD 
1. Legal Asset Retention by Cedent. In most Bermuda-based AIR transactions (approximately 80% 
collateralized), the ceding insurer retains legal possession of the assets in a funds -withheld or modified 
coinsurance arrangement, ring-fencing them for the reinsured block. When Athene, USA uses a Bermuda-based 
reinsurer, the underlying assets remain in the U.S. and on Athene, USA’s U.S. balance sheets in separate 
accounts. These separate accounts are then mandatorily backstopped by the reinsurance treaties that require 
quarterly funding of any separate account deficits to the extent of the reinsured reserves.   
 
2. Full Visibility on U.S. Statutory Balance Sheet. Since the assets remain in the ceding insurer’s statutory 
account or a dedicated collateral account, ceding insurers and their regulators maintain direct oversight, 
bolstering investor and stakeholder confidence. 
 
Addressing the Retirement Protection Gap.  By combining advanced ALM expertise in Bermuda with the tax and 
regulatory efficiencies mentioned above, AIR provides a practical solution to expand underwriting capacity and 
maintain competitive product pricing.  (See more detailed response in Q11.) 
Insurance Europe, 
Europe 
As mentioned in the previous question, Insurance Europe, Europe considers that the IAIS should tighten its 
definition of AIR to be more targeted, and proportionate, in its analysis.  
 
Transfer of life blocks of business is a traditional life reinsurance activity. It is performed routinely and contribute 
to the well-functioning of the life and health insurance market. Supervisors are well accustomed to this market 
practice and well equipped to oversee it.  
 
However, Insurance Europe, Europe sees a risk that AIR could be loosely defined as a catch-all category that 
captures any long-term reserves cession.   
Noted.

A transfer of reserves should only be considered asset-intensive when it gives rise to a significant transfer of 
assets (e.g. >5bn) and the allocation towards illiquid asset classes is very high compared to the allocation of 
assets backing a traditional block of business transfer. AIR should not include reinsurance structures that only 
transfer very remote (tail) asset risk exposure of life blocks.

Section 4.1: We believe a sentence should be added to note that “Many AIR transactions do not entail significant 
immediate revisions to the existing asset portfolio that is transferred (or withheld) by the cedent, particularly for 
transactions with affiliates.” 
 
At the top of page 34, we believe the following sentence should be redrafted to add “subject to investment 
guidelines negotiated with the cedent”; the sentence as amended would read – “The AIR reinsurer takes 
responsibility for deploying the premium (i.e. buying or originating assets), subject to investment guidelines 
negotiated with the cedant, to achieve the target returns and is required to provide any additional top -up if claims 
exceed asset cash flows.” 
 
Section 4.1.1 Economics of the Transaction: We believe there are broader potential benefits from transactions 
that should be acknowledged, including: 
- Those the IAIS identifies in section 4.2.6; 
- Accessing additional sources of capital; 
- Potential diversification benefits the counterparty may obtain; 
- Attractiveness for investors (e.g., Bermuda-licensed reinsurers have enabled foreign capital to invest in US 
insurance markets in a tax efficient manner); and  
- Benefits for consumers such as improved choice and availability of products, pricing, and protection a second 
balance sheet offers (i.e., i.e., that of the cedant and the reinsurer). 
 
This section, and the Issues Paper more broadly, appears to focus narrowly on the use of AIR for in -force block 
transactions.  For completeness, we recommend providing context on the use of AIR for flow business (i.e., 
Revisions made.

reinsurance of a portion of new sales after the effective date of a reinsurance treaty), which is enabling insurers 
to share benefits AIR provides to consumers in real time through improved pricing.  
 
Section 4.1.2 Affiliated transactions: As currently drafted, the structure and tone of this section suggest affiliated 
AIR is done solely to exploit differences in regulatory regimes to the detriment of policyholder protection.  To 
provide a more balanced perspective of the practice, the section should highlight that affiliated reinsurance – 
including for asset intensive business – is not novel for the life sector and acknowledge that insurers can realize 
economic benefits through these transactions, including enabling better measurement and hedging of risks. In  
addition, we believe the BMA sufficiently addresses the paper’s “additional considerations” – several components 
of its framework require and rely upon comprehensive and frequent collaboration with other supervisors.  
 
Section 4.1.3 Investment flexibility, guidelines and recapture risk: Understanding and managing risks effectively 
is fundamental to the business of insurance. In this spirit, AIR transactions are subject to thorough review and 
negotiation between counterparties to ensure adequate protections are in place, including the ability to effectively 
recapture business from an operational and regulatory compliance perspective if it were necessary.  Investment 
guidelines, related reporting requirements, and the use of trusts/collateralization requiremen ts (e.g., ~80% of AIR 
to Bermuda is collateralized through funds withheld (FWH) or modified coinsurance (ModCo) structures) are key 
elements of these negotiations and serve as meaningful levers for reducing risk to the cedant. It is common for 
FWH or ModCo accounts to be over-collateralised, meaning the assets held in these accounts exceed the 
reserves in the ceding jurisdiction. 
 
From a structural/flow perspective, we believe it is important for the Issues Paper to provide context on these 
measures and how AIR is conducted in practice before a discussion on recapture risk.  To this end, we believe 
the discussion on recapture risk should be covered in Section 4.3 (supervisory concerns and responses) or, at a 
minimum, after section 4.1.7.   
 
As noted elsewhere in our response, the ability to better measure and hedge risks can be a driver of AIR.  This 
can include the ability to improve cash flow matching by managing the business in a market consistent regime or 
through employing a wider range of asset classes to back the liability. 
 
Figure 5-Illustration of the cedent’s trade-off between pricing and recapture risk: We believe the IAIS should 
explain what it is classifying as “risk-free assets” (column 2) and “risky assets” (column 3).  In addition, we note 
that local reserving and regulatory standards typically determine how recapture provisions apply, meaning ceding 
insurers seldom rely on recapture triggered purely by a shift in “the box.” Leading with such a figure can give the

misleading impression that this is the default approach, whereas it is more of a nuanced, less common scenario.  
 
Section 4.1.4 Termination events: This section should more specifically identify that termination/recapture 
options are only exercised at the option of the cedent and cannot be forced upon the cedent.  
 
Section 4.1.5 Complexity of retrocessions: This section should acknowledge that retrocessions can play a 
valuable role within the insurance ecosystem, providing an additional ‘layer’ of counterpart y involvement and 
protection in the event of adverse future outcomes.  This provides valuable diversification and pooling of risk 
across the industry.   
 
We recommend clarifying these sentences in the last paragraph – “Furthermore, these agreements should also 
incorporate the impact of retrocession on the termination event and early-warning triggers. For instance, 
retrocession is likely to impact the solvency ratio of the reinsurer due to capital relief. If termination events or 
early warning triggers in the agreement are based on solvency ratios, then these thresholds should be adapted 
to reflect that.”   
 
From a structural/flow perspective, we believe we believe this topic should follow section 4.1.7 and include 
discussion on how the structural protections negotiated under the treaty between the original cedant and 
reinsurer will persist (e.g., use of a trust, investment guidelines, etc.). 
 
Section 4.1.6 Common structure of collateral arrangements in AIR: It is important for the Issues Paper to 
acknowledge that even without a trust or other collateral mechanism, the reinsurance treaty can still codify the 
requirement for investment reporting, risk management, and adherence to investment guidelines. We 
recommend adding language to capture this point. 
 
Section 4.1.7 Collateral level and other collateral features: We recommend noting that cedants always have the 
ability to require collateral, provided such requirements are appropriately codified within the reinsurance treaty, 
and typically do so. 
 
Section 4.1.8 Drivers of AIR: We believe it is important for the Issues Paper to note that sidecar structuring is 
typically done from the perspective of what is most efficient from an investor perspective, rather than trying to 
manage the cedant’s tax position. In Bermuda, sidecars are subject to the same regulations and requirements as 
all other commercial (re)insurers. Further, we note that there are well-established protocols and guardrails which 
can prohibit the use of tax mitigating strategies within an insurance gr oup/through affiliated transactions (e.g.,

Base Erosion and Anti-Abuse Tax (BEAT) law in the U.S., ownership limits, transfer pricing requirements).  
 
More broadly, and as noted earlier in our response, AIR is enabling insurers to access additional sources of 
capital needed for the sector to address the protection gaps society is facing and driving greater diversification, 
competitiveness, and product availability/pricing for policyholders. 
Athora, Bermuda 
Section 4.1 
• Generally, the language could be more precise; for example 
o The characterisation of AIR currently suggests that in certain instances only investment risks are transferred; 
this is not the case as, at a minimum, policyholder behaviour risk is transferred  
o The Issues Paper refers to “claims” etc, whereas, in respect of AIR, it would be clearer to use, say, “reinsured 
claims” etc 
o The Issues Paper says that an additional top-up is required “if claims exceed asset cash flows”; actually, top-
ups are required if the value of the collateral assets falls below the collateral requirements as set forth by the 
collateral framework 
o Figure 4 misses typical other positions that are exchanged between cedant and reinsurer, such as ongoing 
reinsurance premiums and ceding commissions   
Section 4.1.1 
• The insurer to be a counterparty in the sentence “…transfers … risks … to a counterparty (insurer or reinsurer)” 
can actually only occur with PRT buy-ins; this could be clarified 
• The second bullet under cedant benefits may also mention that AIR can turn a stream of future uncertain profits 
into a stream of future certain profits (i.e. it doesn’t have to always be the translation into an immediate profit)  
• The second bullet says “sacrifice of additional future profits”, which is vague; it rather is a “reduction of 
expected future profits” 
• It is not clear why the growth of AUM (mentioned in the first bullet under counterparty benefits) is a benefit, and 
the bullet should be clearer on the underlying reasons: (i) economies of scale/operational leverage, (ii), leverage 
of inv expertise (the third bullet), (iii) better deployment of chunky investment opportunities  
Section 4.1.2 
• The section (rightfully) says that the economic impacts can be different between Group and individual entities. It 
is important to note here that the individual entities will always have their own governance frameworks and 
profitability targets, upon which they will assess a transaction. In addition, the parties will have to adhere to the  
arms-length principle.  
Section 4.1.3 
• “higher-yielding assets” alone are unlikely to suffice to enable the reinsurer to offer attractive pricing; it’s fair to 
say that it takes at least “assets with higher risk-adjusted yields”  
Revisions done. 
Additional 
insights can be 
considered in 
future work.

• The objective of AIR is to transfer the relevant economic risks from the cedant to the reinsurer over long time 
horizon (usually until run-off of the underlying liabilities). AIR does, therefore, typically not envisage an ordinary 
early termination/recapture at the discretion of one party alone. However, as Section 4.1.4 outlines, early 
termination rights need to emerge in certain extra-ordinary circumstances, or the parties may mutually agree to 
terminate the agreement. In our view, the only type of termination/recapture that matters here and that the paper 
should focus more on is the termination/recapture as a consequence of a reinsurer fault (due to e.g. continued 
non-payment of claims, non-cured collateral breach, default,…). In a reinsurer fault event, the continuance of the 
agreements is unlikely to be beneficial for the cedant, and the cedant will therefore effectively be forced to make 
use of its termination right and possibly end up with the collateral assets. Other termination avenues are either 
under the cedant’s control (cedant fault events) or require its consent (mutually -agreed termination), which the 
cedant would simply not provide if the termination had a too-detrimental effect on its financial position.  
• The section suggest that the recapture consideration would always consist of the collateral asset. The setup, 
however, is usually more nuanced. The recapture consideration and the form of its settlement are negotiated at 
outset and then contractually enshrined in the reinsurance agreement. Usually, the amount depends on the 
termination event (with reinsurance-fault termination often carrying the largest recapture amount). For example, 
the consideration in a certain termination event may amount to the economic value of the liabilities, which  the 
reinsurer may then settle by transferring assets and cash with a fair value equal to this amount. The collateral 
assets hereby “merely” serve as a security, i.e. if the reinsurer does not settle, the cedant can seize the collateral 
assets. Hence, only in this worst case, the cedant would end up with the collateral assets. That should be made 
clearer.  
• Section needs to be clearer about what “losses” it is referring. Is it GAAP losses? Losses in own funds/available 
capital? (The context suggests the latter.) 
• Figure 5 – the dashed line is incorrect, should be up to the top of the column 
• Figure 5 suggests that the value of the collateral assets (on Day 1) is equal to the initial reinsurance premium. 
That is not necessarily the case. There, however, is indeed the positive relationship between collateral 
requirements and initial reinsurance premium: the more onerous those requirements, the higher, directionally, 
the reinsurance premium. 
• “Collateral structures where the assets are held on the balance sheet of the cedent and are therefore by default 
compliant with the cedent regulatory regime carry less risk associated with recapturing the supporting assets…”. 
It could be noted that for funds-transferred transactions, the same can be achieved through the investment 
guidelines; i.e. funds-withheld is not necessarily better then collateralised funds-transfer in this respect.   
Section 4.1.4 
• It should be noted that the legal agreements provide for cure periods. This allows the parties first aim to resolve 
the matter potentially avoiding the early termination (as mentioned above, AIR agreements are meant to be

effective for a long time…).      
Section 4.1.5 
• It is not clear whether the draft suggests that any reinsurer solvency triggers referred to in the reinsurance 
agreement “should be adapted” such that the triggers’ assessment is effectively done gross of any retrocession 
(i.e. as if any such retrocession would be terminated). We would see such treatment as too punitive. It is also not 
clear whether this approach should apply to the reinsurer’s entire retro program, i.e. also include retro 
agreements on risks that are not attached to the AIR agreement in question.  
• Figure 6: the dotted arrow from Sidecar to Investors should also show “return of risk capital” (in addition to 
“profits”). 
Section 4.1.6 
• Last sentence in first paragraph should read: “A key mitigant of these transactions against reinsurer 
counterparty risk is the collateral arrangement agreed between the parties.”  
Section 4.1.6.2 
• What exactly is meant by “…the trust is insulated from geopolitical risks”?  
Section 4.1.6.3 
• Periodic settlements working differently than described in the draft: the net settlement amount (reinsurance 
claims, plus ceding commission, less reinsurance premiums) are usually deducted from the funds withheld 
accounts (and transferred to another account of the cedant); this is obviously not a payment between cedant and 
reinsurer but, as the draft rightfully puts, otherwise indeed working analogously to the trust arrangement, just that 
there the net settlement amount is deducted from the reinsurer’s trust accounts and thus a  payment between 
cedant and reinsurer; in addition to the periodic settlements, there is the periodic rebalancing of the funds 
withheld accounts, which will result in payments or asset transfers between cedant and reinsurer  
• A funds-withheld arrangement does not necessarily lead to lower counterparty risk compared to an AIR with 
trust; the charge the cedant has under a trust agreement can be equally powerful and effective to protect its 
interest in the event of a reinsurer (de)fault     
• The reinsurer’s asset manager would usually also be designated as an “advisor for investment management” 
(not only the reinsurer) 
Section 4.1.6.3 
• We would not necessarily see ModCo to be “structurally the same as funds withheld” (because, as the draft 
points out, reserves and assets remain with the cedant); however, the cashflows streams between cedant and 
reinsurer are effectively the same as under funds withheld

Asset-intensive reinsurance, when properly structured and supervised, is match-funded, investment grade, and 
long-term in nature. Regulatory frameworks should reflect the actual risk – the net risk of these transactions, 
which requires a holistic examination of each transaction. 
Noted.

“Along with regulatory oversight associated with AIR due to the potential complexity of the transactions, affiliated 
reinsurance treaties are subject to pre-approval or notification in some jurisdictions to ensure the terms of the 
contract are arms-length in nature amongst other key terms.”  In the Cayman Islands, pre-approval and 
notification are not limited to affiliated transactions.We do not see the substantiation for the sentence:  “Collateral 
structures where the assets are held on the balance sheet of the cedent and are therefore by default compliant 
with the cedent regulatory regime carry, less risk associated with recapturing the supporting assets, though the 
risk associated with re-establishing required capital remains.”  There are collateral structures where the assets 
are in a trust and typically those investment guidelines are more stringent than for a funds withheld structure. 
These would also be associated with less risk, especially if the collateral is at or above the level of the ceded 
reserve. Additionally, most large cedants would assess a counterparty risk capital within their capital framework 
or internal economic capital.We would appreciate understanding the basis for the statement: “The collateral 
valuation is recalculated regularly and compared to an updated required collateral amount. This frequency may 
be monthly, quarterly or annually.”   We are not aware of a transaction with annual collateral valuation 
requirements.  They are usually more frequent than this.If the statement is made that “For instance, larger 
reinsurers established in non-US jurisdictions, such as Bermuda and the Cayman Islands, were able to gain 
market share in the US annuities market by partially passing tax savings into product pricing”, we suggest that 
tax elections be considered here. Most internal reinsurers of U.S. annuities are already U.S. taxpayers. 
Additionally, there are tax implications even if third-party, for e.g. FET, and repatriation tax considerations.

Comments on section 4.1 Understanding AIR 
 
AIR plays a constructive role in managing legacy liabilities and long-duration risks. We recommend: 
§ Deleting the quantitative analysis in Section 4.2.5 due to limited sample size and simplistic assumptions  
§ Emphasizing how AIR facilitates capital raising and ALM flexibility 
§ Recognizing existing jurisdictional supervision that mitigates identified risks  
§ Highlighting the strength and effectiveness of U.S. and Bermuda regulatory systems where AIR is most 
prevalent 
§ Incorporating quantitative assessments, supported by industry and supervisory data, to strengthen policy 
conclusions and reflect jurisdictional differences. 
Revisions made.

Section 4.2.5 includes an attempt at a quantitative analysis to help identify key economic drivers. The NAIC 
appreciates the effort to achieve this goal but recognizes that the limited sample size and required simplifications 
significantly diminish the value that could come from such an exercise in this paper. The value of supervisory

- On 4.2.1.1 It might be worth highlighting that the level of policy data granularity available to cedants and 
reinsurers for valuation purposes often differs significantly. While cedants typically have access to detailed 
policyholder data and can perform policy-level cash flow modelling, reinsurers may work with more aggregated 
data or grouped model points. This disparity in modelling approaches can lead to valuation differences, 
particularly AIR transactions. 
 
- On the points (4.2.6)  made in relation to the quantitative analysis, IAIS had noted that ‘it appears that one of 
the drivers of the growth in the use of AIR is the leveraging of jurisdictional differences in reserving approaches, 
capital requirements or investment flexibility. … Thematically, the most significant differences are attributed to 
the degree to which assumptions are prescribed by supervisors compared to jurisdictions that allow more insurer 
discretion in setting assumptions, subject to oversight and limitations by supervisors.’ However,  these 
jurisdictional differences would technically be true for traditional reinsurance arrangements as well. As such, it 
would appear that the key reason that these jurisdictional differences are playing such a huge role in AIR is the 
investment/asset risks transfer associated with AIR, which resulted in the consequential differences highlighted.  
Noted.

We believe that greater clarity is needed from the IAIS and individual jurisdictions regarding their objectives for 
insurance company solvency. The paper accurately identifies a trend toward combining solvency concepts into 
"Total Asset Requirements" or "TAR," which ultimately reflects a desired probability of ruin for a portfolio of 
insurance policies. It is crucial for insurance companies, regulators, investors, and rating agencies to clearly 
articulate their risk preferences, as the insurance business cannot be fully insulated from failure. Rating agencies 
appear to grasp this concept more readily than many regulatory bodies. 
 
Effective regulation demands resources and knowledge, which may not be universally available. A jurisdiction's 
insurance ecosystem, encompassing actuaries, accountants, lawyers, and investment managers, significantly 
influences its regulatory effectiveness. Bermuda and the BMA exemplify strong leadership in fostering this 
ecosystem and allocating necessary resources for sound regulation. 
 
The conceptual coherence of IAIS standards is important. The draft paper's apparent questioning of economic 
valuation, Prudent Person Principle (PPP) investment rules, and group-wide consolidation appears inconsistent 
with a decade of IAIS policymaking. The final issues paper should clarify that the mere presence of an economic 
regime, PPP freedom, or consolidated supervision does not inherently indicate weakness or regulatory arbitrage.  
 
The IAIS should avoid portraying transfers of insurance risk to "economic" regimes as problematic. Table 2 
suggests that most accepted insurance risk (AIR) flows to economic frameworks, which may reflect insurers' 
pursuit of valuations that better capture market and credit risk, rather than an attempt to manipulate c apital.

refer to 
jurisdictional 
weaknesses or 
regulatory 
arbitrage. The 
main point of this 
comment have

paper and it is 
not the IAIS's role  
to qualify any 
difference as 
legitimate or not.

Section 4.2 should acknowledge this reality and refrain from judgmental assessments of certain regulatory 
approaches as mere loopholes. 
 
It is important to respect legitimate jurisdictional diversity. Solvency methods evolve to suit local market needs 
and realities. Therefore, the IAIS should describe differences rather than rank them, recognizing that protection 
goals can be achieved through various mixes of tools. We recommend clearly separating micro- from 
macroprudential concerns. Several passages conflate firm-level solvency with systemic risk. We suggest 
inserting an explicit statement that, based on current evidence, AIR exposures do not constitute a 
macroprudential threat. Microprudential techniques, such as fit-and-proper assessments, ORSA, stress-testing, 
collateral haircuts, and recapture triggers, are already available in well-regulated jurisdictions and should remain 
the primary line of defence. 
 
The narrative surrounding "regulatory arbitrage" needs reframing. While we acknowledge the IAIS's con cern that 
certain AIR flows may exploit regulatory differences, many of these differences reflect legitimate jurisdictional 
choices. Clarifying how solvency rules, collateral requirements, and enterprise risk management expectations 
already curb arbitrage would help supervisors focus on substance rather than labels.  
 
Finally, paper should acknowledge the diversification benefit of reinsurance. This and other sections in the issues 
paper sometimes read as a critique of reinsurance itself. Instead, the paper should highlight that reinsurance 
provides diversification, lowers the cost of guarantees, and ultimately offers more affordable coverage for 
policyholders, which are central aims of insurance in closing protection gaps and should align with IAIS prioriti es.

Besides comparing reserve, capital, and investment flexibility among jurisdictions, we suggest adding a 
subsection to highlight other work that has been done to enhance the supervisory role. For example, the U.S. 
has taken the following steps: 
 
The NAIC and states thoroughly review and approve the qualification of each qualifying reciprocal jurisdiction 
annually, with clear standards and criteria for designation as a reciprocal jurisdiction. If a jurisdiction lacks the 
reciprocal designation, assets equal to full statutory reserve are required to back the transaction. In either case, 
AIR transactions are generally fully collateralized or overcollateralized, which increases the security of the AIR 
transaction. The NAIC is also in the process of designing enhanced asset-adequacy testing and disclosure for 
reinsurance transactions.

valuation, capital requirements and investment flexibility.  
 
I. Comments on Table 2 Summary of Jurisdictional Approaches to Reserve Valuation and Capital Requirements  
 
While Table 2 is an interesting exercise, it should not be relied upon as a comprehensive comparison of 
jurisdictions. The table does not differentiate between the types of capital resources permitted, which may vary 
by jurisdiction. The table is also a relatively limited selection of jurisdictions; it is not clear why certain large 
jurisdictions, like Canada (LICAT), are not included. 
 
II.  Comment on Section 4.2.5 Quantitative Analysis  
 
The quantitative analysis in Section 4.2.5 is based on a sample of a simplified, hypothetical transaction that was 
performed by a very limited sample of insurers. Considering the material limitations of the exercise, ACLI 
recommends deleting this section and focusing on the analysis of actual transactions. 
 
III. Comments on Section 4.2.6 Impact of Differences in Reserving, Capital Requirements and Investment  
Flexibility 
 
ACLI agrees with the observation in Section 4.2.6 that there are many important drivers that drive cedents’ 
growing appetite for AIR, not least of which is access to capital that allows insurers to continue to meet the 
growing need for long-term insurance products with retirement savings features. AIR deals are bespoke and can 
be complex, with multiple drivers influencing the decision to use AIR. Supervisory review should assess each 
transaction individually, with an eye towards understanding the driver(s) for each transaction.

We disagree with the assertion that "Use of amortised cost does not produce a clear picture of asset-liability

backing short-term liabilities, but only if the liabilities are also marked to market. A robust impairment process

that factors in the fair value of an asset is an important component of amortized cost accounting. Additionally, 
cash." Cash flow testing is a robust risk management tool that complements accounting approaches, whether fair

strategy. As noted above, jurisdictional approaches to reserving and capital requirements should take into

consistently and match well with their corresponding longer-duration liabilities. For instance, infrastructure bond 
investments are not and should not be measured on a daily-mark-to-market basis in the same way that it would 
not make sense to mark-to-market very long-duration liabilities that are not affected by temporary market 
fluctuations.

Athene, USA 
The Issues Paper focuses largely on structural shifts – namely increased allocations to private credit and AIR – 
that remain concentrated in only a few jurisdictions, chiefly the U.S. and Bermuda. Both have adapted and 
continue to adapt their frameworks – through revised RBC factors, scenario-based testing, comprehensive 
transaction approvals, and supervisory colleges – to ensure that insurers remain adequately capitalized, 
transparent, and well-managed.  
 
BERMUDA REGULATORY FRAMEWORK 
Bermuda’s regime, long recognized by the NAIC (Qualified and Reciprocal Jurisdiction status) and by the EU 
(Solvency II equivalence), has undergone a comprehensive modernization: 
 
    1. Economic Balance Sheet (EBS). Principles-based valuation with a rigorous Scenario-Based Approach 
Already included

(SBA) requiring alignment of assets and liabilities and strong liquidity risk management practices.  
 
    2. High Capital Quality. The long-term sector maintains a median solvency ratio of 259%.  
 
    3. Capital reforms. Policy holder behavior risk was refined to explicitly capture lapse risk.  Expense risk is now 
explicitly modeled. 
 
    4. Collateral Management. 77% of liabilities are collateralized using funds withheld or trust -based structures, 
with reinsurers required to top-up under mark-to-market shortfalls. 
 
    5. Regulatory Enhancements. 2023 and 2024 reforms include guidance on prudent person principle (PPP) 
application, enhanced investment disclosure, liquidity stress testing, increased SBA governance requirements, 
and board accountability. With respect to PPP, enhanced standards for insurers’ management of investments, 
including more stringent requirements for capital, risk management, board oversight, and disclosures.  
 
    6. Governance 
       - Utilization of the SBA for reserve calculation is limited at the point of adoption and formally monitored on an 
ongoing basis. 
       - Requirements around liquidity risk management, given that the SBA is a dynamic cashflow matching 
approach that works in the context of a liquidity risk management framework.  
       - Review and approval of affiliate assets, derivatives, and below investment grade assets.  
       - Model risk management requirements, management attestation and board approval.  
       - Supervisory Colleges and Bilateral Coordination: Bermuda hosted or participated in 46 supervisory 
colleges in 2023 alone.  
 
    7. Reserving 
       - Explicit default and downgrade charge assessed in Scenario Based Approach reserve  
       - Additional lapse conservatism explicitly modeled in reserves 
       - Clarified guidance on reinvestment/disinvestment  
 
The Bermuda regime includes conservativism through conservative default costs, the worst of nine interest rate 
scenarios, and a provision for adverse lapses. These are in addition to the explicit risk margin  and best estimate 
liability that form technical provisions (reserves). Rather than trying to compare only reserves, it is more 
appropriate to compare the total asset requirement (TAR) where BSCR is at a higher calibration than RBC. This

is why BSCR ratios are commonly lower than RBC ratios at the equivalent rating point.  
 
US REGULATORY FRAMEWORK 
The U.S. regulatory framework, led by the NAIC and U.S. state insurance commissioners, has not been idle. 
Over the past decade, the U.S. has implemented targeted reforms and continues to evolve supervisory oversight 
in key areas: 
 
    1. Revised Risk-Based Capital (RBC) Treatment. Updates to RBC methodology now better align capital 
charges with the actual risks posed by reinsurance and structured products.  The NAIC, through its RBC Model 
Governance (EX) Task Force, has undertaken in 2025 to develop guiding principles for future RBC adjustments 
and identify gaps. The Task Force will be positioned to modernize the U.S. capital framework and advance RBC 
integrity.  Those goals the NAIC had already committed to through its Holistic Framework for Regulation of 
Insurer Investments 
 
    2. Enhanced Reserve Credit Rules (XXX/AXXX). Revisions tighten credit for reinsurance, including rigorous 
requirements on collateral and capital sufficiency.  
 
    3. Asset Adequacy Testing (AAT). AAT proposals now incorporate enhanced modeling requirements and 
liquidity sensitivity to support state-level review of reinsured liabilities. 
 
    4. CLO Treatment. Residual tranches are subject to heightened capital charges, while other tranches have 
been reassessed using granular modeling of tranche-level credit risk. 
 
    5. Liquidity Stress Testing Framework. Adopted in 2023 to evaluate insurer resilience under market stress. 
U.S. regulators now require stress-tested liquidity coverage ratios. 
 
    6. Macroprudential Monitoring Tools. The Group Capital Calculation and Systemic Risk Assessment 
Framework continue to monitor insurers at the group level for potential systemic exposure.  
 
These regulatory enhancements are not merely cosmetic. They are targeted, risk -aligned, and designed to 
strengthen supervision while preserving the ability of insurers to support long-term economic needs.
