From an overarching perspective, we believe it is important to highlight that the content of the section is based 
on a point in time snapshot of jurisdictional frameworks.  As we noted earlier in our response, supervisors have 
and continue to update their frameworks to ensure they remain fit for purpose in light of the structural shifts 
discussed in the Issues Paper, or for other important market developments such as the use of artificial 
intelligence.  To provide a more comprehensive perspective on the jurisdictional landscape, we believe Section 
4.3.6 (Supervisory enhancements) should be moved to the end of Section 4.2.   
 
We believe Section 4.2 is narrowly scoped and implicitly suggests reserving, capital, and investment 
requirements are the main drivers of AIR.  While regulatory requirements certainly play a role, AIR transactions 
are bespoke and, as we have outlined elsewhere in our response, the universe of factors that inform insurer 
business decisions are broader.  In addition, supervisors employ a wider universe of tools to ensure risks are 
identified and understood that can inform AIR activity.  We believe a discussion on approaches to assessing 
liquidity risk in particular would be appropriate in light of views the IAIS has outlined throughout the p aper 
regarding the use of alternative assets to back insurance liabilities.  
 
We also believe that the discussion of the total asset requirement (TAR) should be enhanced to acknowledge the 
limitations of a point-in-time estimate. Definitionally, the Issues Paper describes TAR as comprising “required 
liabilities, capital requirements, and buffer,” but is not clear what is meant by “buffer.” (This appears again in 
Section 4.2.5.) Conceptually, while a comparison of the sum of required liabilities and capital requirements is 
preferrable to comparisons of the standalone elements, using TAR to compare jurisdictional frameworks can be 
misleading, as a point in time comparison can fail to capture the fact that market consistent regimes react 
dynamically to emerging experience. 
 
Finally, we believe this section should acknowledge that a target TAR reflects a preference in terms of where on 
the risk curve supervisory authorities (and other stakeholders) want to be positioned, which can appropriately 
vary.  The IAIS has implicitly acknowledged this dynamic by developing the ICS as a minimum standard, with 
jurisdictions free to impose additional prudence.  
 
Section 4.2, Table 2: For Bermuda, the table should identify the Lapse Cost (“LapC”) component of technical 
provisions within the “Other” category (in addition to the Risk Margin). 
 
Revision not 
done. Refer to 
Box 3.

Section 4.2.1 Reserve valuation: We believe this section warrants further framing, including acknowledgement 
that market-based approaches are designed to react to changing liability performance and assumptions much 
more quickly than prescribed approaches allowing for early recognition of potential issues and intervention if 
necessary. 
 
Section 4.2.1.2 Discounting and adjustments: The first sentence of the last paragraph states “[w]here the 
discount rate is higher in one jurisdiction versus another for the same liability, the insurer or reinsurer may be 
able to hold a lower liability valuation.” As was done in Section 4.2.1.1, we believe the following statement in this 
section should be accompanied by an acknowledgement that while the value of liabilities may  differ across 
regimes, this does not mean that this results in a reduction in TAR or that TAR is not set at a prudent level.  
 
Section 4.2.1.3 Additional liabilities: The second paragraph should note that Bermuda also requires a provision 
for Lapse Cost as part of the technical provisions. 
 
Section 4.2.2 Capital frameworks: The second paragraph should note that Bermuda also requires a provision for 
Lapse Cost as part of the technical provisions. 
 
Section 4.2.3 Investment flexibility: We believe this section requires additional context around the interaction of 
investment flexibility and investment guidelines that are negotiated as part of the reinsurance treaty, which may 
be informed by requirements or limitations applicable to the cedant.   
 
In addition, the Issues Paper suggests recapture risk is heightened for cross border reinsurance as a result of 
different investment requirements/limitations across jurisdictions.  We respectfully disagree with this assessment 
and request the IAIS provide a more comprehensive assessment of the issue in the final paragraph of the 
section that recognizes the various mitigants that reduce potential recapture risk including:  
- Investment guidelines negotiated between the parties that restrict the type of assets the reinsurer  can invest in; 
- Collateral requirements, which are typically maintained in the jurisdiction of the cedant; and  
- Investments the reinsurer makes to back the liabilities are generally made in the same market / jurisdiction as 
the cedant.  For example, AIR from U.S. insurers to counterparts in Bermuda is comprised of USD denominated 
liabilities.  Bermuda based reinsurers predominantly back these liabilities with USD denominated investments 
within the U.S. (i.e., the breadth and depth of the asset market the cedant and reinsurer use to conduct ALM are 
consistent).   
 
Given the fundamental nature of ALM to the business of insurance, we were taken aback by the final sentence’s

suggestion that assets may be recaptured into a jurisdiction that does not follow rules regarding ALM.  In such an 
instance, AIR to a reinsurer operating in a risk conscious manner, in a well-regulated jurisdiction, would enhance 
protection for the policyholder. 
 
Section 4.2.4 Valuation basis for solvency purposes: We believe the differences between amortised cost -value 
based regimes and market-consistent regimes are fundamental, as they impact the measurement and hedging of 
risks and, as a result, can serve as a driver of AIR, particularly in the case of transactions between affiliates. The 
discussion of these differences should be more prominent and potentially integrated into Section 4.2.1.  
 
Section 4.2.5 Quantitative analysis: We recommend removing this section from the Issues Paper unless the IAIS 
broadens the sample, publishes key asset-mix assumptions, and pairs the TAR comparison with parallel stress-
tested solvency ratios, mirroring Bermuda’s scenario-based framework and the NAIC’s “equal capital for equal 
risk” initiative. Given the highly simplified nature of the study, limited sample size, lack of comparison between 
cedent and reinsurer TAR by jurisdiction, lack of auditability, and assumptions employed (through both its design 
and the participants), we believe it is inappropriate to deliver the conclusion that the “quantitative exercise should 
be viewed as demonstrating that differences in jurisdictional approaches can lead to material quantitative 
differences.” As noted in our earlier comments, TAR reflects a preference in terms of where on the risk curve 
insurers, regulators, investors, and other stakeholders want to be positioned and can appropriately vary.  The 
IAIS acknowledges this in Section 4.2.1.1. by stating “[w]hile this difference can re sult in a reduction in the value 
of liabilities, all other variables held constant, this does not mean that this results in a reduction in TAR or that 
TAR is not set at a prudent level.”  If this section is retained in the Issues Paper despite the shortcom ings of the 
study, we believe the observations drawn from it should be revisited and accompanied with context similar to that 
referenced above from Section 4.2.1.1. 
 
Section 4.2.6 Impact of differences in reserving, capital requirements and investment flexibility: We appreciate 
the IAIS acknowledging some of potential drivers of AIR.  We believe the list should be included in Section 4.1.1 
and expanded upon to include:  
- Accessing additional capital / capacity for business growth through transactions with non-affiliates; 
- Potential diversification benefits; 
- Attractiveness for investors (e.g., Bermuda-licensed reinsurers have enabled foreign capital to invest in US 
insurance markets in a tax efficient manner); and  
- The ability to enhance product offerings for consumers.

The Geneva 
Association, 
International 
We acknowledge the supervisory concerns discussed in section 4.3. Our primary recommendation is to reframe 
the discussion of "motivation" to emphasize supervisory cooperation rather than focusing solely on alleged 
arbitrage. While the paper suggests that affiliated intra-group reinsurance (AIR) is largely a means of leveraging 
regulatory differences, we believe this overstates the reality of regulatory arbitrage. A more fundamental risk is 
the potential for insufficient day-to-day cooperation between home and host-state supervisors. Effective 
supervisory colleges, bilateral memoranda of understanding, and real-time data-sharing can ensure that cross-
border AIR is supervised on its substance, not on unsubstantiated assumptions about arbitrage. Therefore, we 
propose adjusting the language in section 4.3 to emphasize strengthened cross -jurisdictional coordination and 
information exchange. 
 
We also believe the paper should acknowledge the diversification benefits of reinsurance, including AIR. 
Reinsurance is fundamentally a mechanism for diversifying risk across geographies, currencies, and lines of 
business. Well-structured AIR transactions place risk with entities that possess both the appetite and the asset -
management capability to support long-dated guarantees. A balanced narrative should weigh the supervisory 
prudential concerns described in this section against the public-policy benefits of broader, more affordable 
retirement solutions. 
 
Regarding recapture risk, we observe that the paper focuses heavily on its severity without adequately 
addressing its frequency. It is important to remember that AIR involves at least two counterparties, each with its 
own regulator. Regulatory interaction is crucial for promoting transparency and addressing concerns related to  
AIR. Jurisdictions like Bermuda, which primarily regulate reinsurers, have established linkages and offer 
transparency to ceding company regulators in the context of AIR. We contend that recapture risk is significantly 
reduced when reinsurers are strong and remain solvent over the long term. To better contextualize recapture 
risk, we recommend analyzing both historical statistics and the balance sheet strength of reinsurers. We 
generally support efforts to promote greater transparency, such as disclosure o f assets held by reinsurers, to 
help market observers and direct-writing companies evaluate the true risk standing of reinsurers.  
 
For ceding company regulators, it is also important to understand the aggregate recapture risk companies may 
hold against specific counterparties. Existing supervisory tools—including stress-testing in the Own Risk and 
Solvency Assessment (ORSA), collateral haircuts and top-ups, early-warning triggers, and supervisory reviews 
of trigger clauses—can already manage recapture risks. Section 4.3 should encourage supervisors to refine

these controls.  
 
We agree with section 4.3.1 that AIR arrangements should not be used to circumvent local prudential rules from 
a ceding supervisor's perspective, a point supported by Table 3's survey results. However, the fact that ceding 
jurisdictions approve AIR transactions suggests an awareness of local prudential shortcomings and a willingness 
to work with insurers to mitigate their effects. We suggest that the International Association of Insurance 
Supervisors (IAIS) establish an expectation for its members to continuously monitor their local solvency regimes 
for shortcomings that incentivize reinsurance solutions. 
 
In section 4.3.2, the paper recommends that supervisors judge whether affiliated and cross -border AIR are used 
to optimize legal entities' positions or to move risks to subsidiaries where undervaluation may occur. The paper 
also emphasizes that a better assessment is based on the "total asset requirement" (TAR) encompassing 
valuation/reserves and required capital. While TAR represents an improvement over a standalone comparison of 
reserves or technical provisions, TAR may also be compromised because differences in valuation can arise 
solely from discrepancies between market value and book value accounting, among other factors.  
 
Finally, while section 4.3.6 describes various jurisdictional initiatives that include an assessment of "recapture 
risk," we note that the term "recapture" is not currently found in the Insurance Core Principles (ICPs) or 
ComFrame. In the event of recapture, even under stressed conditions, all stakeholders, including supervisors, 
would have an incentive to facilitate the transition of risk either back to the original cedent or to a different 
reinsurer. Typical collateral structures are designed to robustly facilitate this transition, making it very unlikely that 
recapture would lead to either microprudential or macroprudential effects.  
American Council 
of Life Insurers, 
United States 
I. Comments on Section 4.3.3 Group-Wide Supervision Overview and Tools for AIR 
 
ACLI agrees that the inter-jurisdictional cooperation on approaches to asset-intensive insurers is critical to 
comprehensively evaluate the risks associated with AIR transactions. The section’s description of supervisory 
tools to address potential risks associated with AIR provide a helpful resource for supervisors who may have 
varying degrees of familiarity with AIR. 
 
ACLI agrees that a TAR comparison is a superior approach to simply considering AIR’s impact on capital or 
reserves alone, however we recommend that TAR be used as one component in a comprehensive evaluation of 
asset-intensive transactions. 
 
II. Comments on Section 4.3.4 Data Availability 
Revisions done.

The U.S. collects a significant amount of detailed data related to reinsurance transactions. The report refers to 
the ability to identify retrocessions and differentiate between reinsurers and multiline insurers. It is not clear how 
this statement is germane to AIR. Recommend deleting the comment about the U.S.’ ability to identify 
retrocessions and distinguish between reinsurers and multiline insurers. 
 
III. Comment on Section 4.3.6 Supervisory Enhancements 
 
ACLI appreciates the Paper’s effort to catalogue regulatory initiatives from multiple jurisdictions that will promote 
the robust supervision of AIR. 
Athene, USA 
The IAIS’s stated concerns about AIR – valuation complexity, credit risk, recapture, conflicts, information gaps, 
and potential systemic concentration –- are being effectively addressed within Bermuda’s supervisory 
environment and by Athene, USA and Apollo’s internal controls. 
       - Our reinsurance structures rely heavily on collateralized ModCo or funds-withheld, preventing “asset flight” 
and aligning recapture with stable, ring-fenced collateral. 
       - Our credit investments are predominantly investment grade, subject to rigorous Apollo underwriting, and 
built around ALM strategies at Athene, USA to neutralize maturity or liquidity mismatch. 
       - Bermuda’s regulatory regime includes thorough transaction-level approvals, data-driven capital and 
reserve requirements, and advanced stress testing, helping ensure both micro - and macro-level stability. 
 
In short, the alignment of Athene, USA’s capital management practices with Apollo’s disciplined credit approach, 
alongside Bermuda’s well-developed regulatory frameworks, demonstrates that AIR can be conducted 
responsibly, providing genuine risk transfer and stable capacity without adding unwarranted systemic risk. See 
more detail below. 
 
VALUATION, CREDIT RISK, AND RECAPTURE CONCERNS 
The IAIS’s critique centers on accurately valuing reinsurance recoverables, potential credit risks from reinsurers, 
and recapture risk (where the cedent may assume assets back if the reinsurer falters). In practice, Athene, USA, 
Apollo, and Bermuda’s regime collectively address these risks: 
 
    1. Aligned Reserving Standards 
       - Athene, USA’s and other Bermuda reinsurers’ reserving standards are at least as conservative as those of 
U.S.-domiciled life insurers. Bermuda has strengthened its valuation and solvency framework, requiring scenario -
based reserve approaches, third-party actuarial reviews, and expense and lapse capital charges. (See BMA, 
Noted. This 
comment 
provides both 
jurisdictional and 
company best 
practices

Insights and Reflections on AIR, pp. 4-5). 
       - At Athene, USA, we employ a rigorous asset-liability management (ALM) framework that ensures liability 
reserving fully aligns with the actual economic risks. We conduct annual reviews, validated by both internal 
experts and external actuaries, to confirm credit risk and recapture exposures are adequately managed. 
 
    2. Credit Risk 
       - Apollo has developed a consistent, in-depth underwriting process for the private credit and structured 
investments that often back annuity blocks reinsured to Athene, USA. By focusing predominantly on investment 
grade assets (97% of Athene, USA’s assets are investment grade), we manage credit risk at origination and 
throughout the asset’s life. 
       - A ceding insurer’s Actuarial Opinion captures any residual credit risk. Moreover, any perceived credit risk is 
further mitigated by collateralization, an approach that Athene, USA endorses for its reinsurance structures. 
 
    3. Recapture Risk 
       - Recapture concerns are greatly reduced by widely used “funds withheld” or “modified coinsurance” 
(ModCo) structures, ensuring that assets remain ring-fenced – often in the cedent’s home jurisdiction. (See BMA, 
Insights and Reflections on AIR, pp. 8-9). The ceding insurer retains direct control of our visibility into those 
assets, addressing collateral adequacy if recapture is triggered. 
       - In Athene, USA’s transactions, recapture protocols are clearly spelled out; collateral levels are often 
established above statutory reserves, preempting a potential shortfall under stress scenarios. 
 
COMPLEXITY AND SUPERVISORY CHALLENGES  
The IAIS notes AIR transactions can be highly bespoke, demanding extensive regulatory resources. Yet Athene, 
USA, Apollo, and the BMA’s transaction-level oversight demonstrate these complexities are managed: 
 
    1. Dedicated Transaction Approval 
       - Since 2023, the BMA has required closed-block life reinsurance transactions to secure pre-approval, with 
input from both the ceding and assuming jurisdictions.  (BMA, Insights and Reflections on AIR, pp. 7-8)). Apollo 
and Athene, USA fully comply with these processes, providing scenario analyses, capital modeling, and risk -
transfer documentation to ensure supervisory clarity. 
       - In practice, Apollo’s structuring teams engage directly with the BMA early in the process, discussing 
proposed transactions’ collateral mechanics, asset composition, and fallback triggers, ensuring full transparency 
before the block is written.

2. Cross-Border Coordination. Bermuda’s equivalences (Solvency II equivalent, NAIC qualified/reciprocal) 
facilitate deeper alignment with major jurisdictions’ supervisors. Athene, USA holds capital and uses reserving 
standards based on the most prescriptive of the NAIC regulatory environment, the Bermuda regulatory 
environment, and rating agency standards. Athene, USA’s reserving standards and capital ratios are 
substantially similar in Bermuda and the U.S. Reinsured blocks in Bermuda remain subject to the BMA’s 
continuing scrutiny. There is no regulatory arbitrage. This synergy reduces cross -border gaps, a major focus of 
IAIS ICP 13.  
 
CONFLICTS OF INTEREST AND INTERCONNECTEDNESS 
The IAIS highlights potential conflicts when an asset manager and reinsurer are part of the same group (e.g., 
Apollo and Athene, USA). It also cites systemic contagion if private credit funds become large or too 
interconnected. Our governance, transparent disclosures, and capital oversight measures mitigate these 
concerns: 
 
    1. Enterprise Risk Function 
       - Athene, USA and Apollo maintain dedicated enterprise risk committees. Athene, USA’s Risk Committee, 
supplemented by business-line risk teams, continuously reviews exposures and affiliated transactions. Any 
transaction with Apollo’s asset management affiliates (e.g., co-investments or direct originations) undergoes 
comprehensive conflict of interest checks documented in internal conflict of interest memoranda or formal conflict 
committees.  (Appendix, slide 14). 
       - The BMA’s supervisory approach further subjects affiliated transactions to a high bar of transparency 
(BMA, Insights and Reflections on AIR, pp. 7-9). 
 
     2. Systemic Interconnectedness 
       - Athene, USA invests predominantly in investment grade credit (~97% rated investment grade). 
Additionally, Apollo’s underwriting is anchored in rigorous internal committees that review the credit quality and 
structure of each asset. 
       - The BMA’s new public disclosure proposals (pp. 9-10) ensure that key data-like asset-level detail, ALM 
positions, and group exposures-are available to regulators and stakeholders, reducing potential blind spots in 
large-scale credit exposures. 
 
INFORMATION GAPS AND CONCENTRATION RISKS 
The IAIS raises concerns about data gaps that could impede effective supervision and concentration risk from a 
limited set of reinsurers.

Bermuda’s regime, supplemented by Apollo and Athene, USA’s own disclosures, addresses such gaps: 
 
    1. High-Resolution Reporting 
       - All affiliated or large reinsurance transactions must pass BMA review. Athene, USA provides the BMA with 
detailed risk data, including scenario-based analyses that help the BMA gauge potential concentration or 
correlated exposures. We keep the ceding regulator apprised of any changes to collateral or ALM profiles to 
ensure consistent cross-border oversight. 
       - Public disclosures of assets, set to expand under proposed BMA disclosure enhancements (for assets and 
liabilities), will reach “CUSIP-level” or position-level detail, shining further light on potential concentration pockets.  
(BMA, Insights and Reflections on AIR, p. 10). 
 
    2. Relatively Small Global Footprint.  IAIS’s GIMAR data place total AIR at approximately 5.3% of global life 
provisions, with Bermuda’s share around 1.8%.  (BMA, Insights and Reflections on AIR, pp. 10 -11). Though 
Athene, USA’s assets in Bermuda have grown in absolute terms, they remain a modest part of overall global life 
liabilities, calling into question the systemic nature of the risks. More directly, this phenomenon appears to be 
quite narrow, given the predominance of activity between the U.S. and offshore jurisdictions, and therefore a 
practice that should be managed by U.S. supervisors. 
 
BERMUDA'S REGULATORY AND SUPERVISORY STRENGHTS 
Finally, Bermuda’s environment – featuring collateralized transactions and rigorous solvency and liquidity 
assessments – effectively addresses IAIS concerns on financial stability. 
 
    1. Collateralization of AIR 
       - Nearly 80% of Bermuda’s long-term reinsurance uses funds-withheld or ModCo, so the cedent retains legal 
control over assets.  (BMA, Insights and Reflections on AIR, p. 10).  Athene, USA frequently employs ModCo to 
ensure ceding insurers and regulators maintain direct oversight of underlying investments.  
       - Apollo’s investment teams coordinate with cedent regulators on permissible investment guidelines. 
       - This structure significantly mitigates counterparty risk and fosters transparency.  
 
    2. Strong Solvency.  Bermuda long-term (re)insurers’ median solvency ratio is approximately 259% (YE2023), 
exceeding the 100% regulatory minimum.  (BMA, Insights and Reflections on AIR, pp. 9 -10). Athene, USA’s 
capital position likewise remains strong.  Athene, USA publishes its solvency metrics annually in compliance with 
Bermuda and U.S. RBC regimes and drives considerable conservatism over stress scenarios in internal tests.

3. Rigorous Stress Testing 
       - The BMA requires forward-looking scenario testing of capital and liquidity.  (BMA, Insights and Reflections 
on AIR, pp. 14-15).  
       - Our ORSA (Own Risk and Solvency Assessment), which assesses the insurer’s risk and solvency position, 
incorporates these stress outcomes, ensuring that even under severely adverse economic conditions, Athene, 
USA’s obligations remain securely funded. 
 
    4. Active International Cooperation. Athene, USA, as part of a larger group supervised by the BMA, engages 
in cross-border colleges where Apollo, Athene, USA, and BMA representatives exchange data with U.S. state 
and federal regulators. This approach satisfies ICP 13’s call for frequent, transparent supervisory cooperation.

We appreciate that supervisors need to understand the structural shifts in the life sector.  BILTIR looks forward to 
continuing to partner with the IAIS and jurisdictional supervisors to gain a thorough understanding of them as 
they relate to Bermuda, discuss the various measures our members and the Bermuda Monetary Authority (BMA) 
regulatory framework have in place to address them, and partner on ways for addressing gaps that may be 
identified. 
 
With respect to the Issues Paper, we believe there is a need for revisions to present a more comprehensive and 
balanced view of AIR, the risks and opportunities it may present (especially given the IAIS strategic theme of 
supporting insurance to serve its societal purpose in building resilience), and measures the in dustry and 
supervisors are using to mitigate risks. For example, Box 3 provides a jurisdiction specific and simplified example

Societal

are out of scope 
in this paper

that does not account for risk management measures typically present in AIR deals, such as collateral, 
investment guidelines, and counterparty diversification. We believe the IAIS should include an additional 
example of another jurisdiction, or examples, (in addition to the current UK example) that consider these 
measures or to incorporate them into the current analysis included in Box 3. As Section 4.3.6 notes, supervisors 
have and continue to update their frameworks to ensure they remain fit for purpose i n light of the structural shifts.  
We believe the BMA has been a leader in this respect. 
 
While we don’t believe it to be a macroprudential or systemic risk, we recognize that supervisors have concerns 
around the potential impact of a cedent needing to recapture assets and liabilities if the reinsurer falters.  In the 
context of Bermuda, a number of features serve to mitigate this potential risk, many of which have been 
referenced in other sections of our response: 
- Nearly 80% of Bermuda’s long-term reinsurance uses “funds withheld” or “modified coinsurance” (ModCo) 
structures, which ensure that assets remain ring-fenced – often in the cedent’s home jurisdiction – with the 
cedant retaining direct control of or visibility into those assets. 
- As noted in Oliver Wyman’s Analysis of Systemic Risk in the Bermuda Long-term Insurance Sector (“OW 
Analysis”), Bermuda long-term insurers invest predominantly in investment-grade credit, with comparable 
distribution of ratings to those of U.S. insurers (see Section 5.1.1 of the Oliver Wyman report). Bermuda long-
term insurers also perform robust management of credit risk at origination and throughout the asset’s life.  
- Bermuda long-term (re)insurers’ median solvency ratio was ~259% as of year-end 2023, which is significantly 
above the 100% regulatory minimum, and typical Bermuda (re)insurers target solvency ratio approximating 
200%, which demonstrates that Bermuda (re)insurers are not only well-capitalized but also do not seek to lower 
total asset requirements.  
- The robust supervision of the BMA. This includes measures instituted over the last few years as the market has 
evolved – e.g., strengthened its valuation and solvency framework, drawing from the ICS to enhance its 
approach to capital charges for expense and lapse risk; requiring pre-approval of transactions with input from the 
cedant’s supervisor; and forward-looking stress testing of capital and liquidity. The BMA continues to pursue 
further updates including enhancements to required public disclosure of key asset and liability information.  As 
the Issues Paper notes, Bermuda’s regulatory framework has been recognized as equivalent to Solvency II and 
as a qualified/reciprocal jurisdiction by the NAIC, which demonstrate it is on par in quality with that of other major 
jurisdictions.  
 
The OW Analysis assessed the potential impact of a hypothetical stress scenario where all business reinsured 
from US and UK cedants to third party reinsurers domiciled in Bermuda is simultaneously recaptured (see 
section 5.1.1 of the OW Analysis for details on this portion of their analysis).  As Oliver Wyman notes, “this

approach essentially sets a maximum impact at the market level and should overstate the impact of a more 
plausible ‘mass recapture’ event”.   
 
Based on the in-depth analysis it performed regarding asset intensive business ceded from the U.S. to Bermuda, 
Oliver Wyman concluded that for insurers with little or no reinsurance to Bermuda, there is expected to be 
minimal impact. In a severe scenario, insurers with general account reserves of at least $10 billion might see 
their average RBC ratio decline by about 40 points, but no insurer is expected to fall below regulatory 
intervention levels. Thus, while such an extreme event would significantly affect industry capitalization, it is 
unlikely to threaten the solvency of a substantial portion of the industry. 
 
Based on the in-depth analysis it performed for business ceded from the UK, Oliver Wyman concluded that:  
- “The impact of this scenario on individual insurers with material asset-intensive reinsurance could be material. 
However, it is also important to note that in our example, the firm itself remained well above regulatory 
intervention levels (100% solvency ratio) and quite possibly internal risk appetite thresholds (this varies by firm 
but is typically set around 140-150 % solvency ratio). The firm would likely have also had a range of 
management actions available to further mitigate the impact of the stress and/or restore solvency.”  
- “To explore this further, we perform a reverse stress test to understand the quantum of AIR our illustrative 
insurer would need to cede (as a proportion of overall liabilities), such that a simultaneous credit stress and re -
capture event across all counterparties would lead to its solvency ratio falling to 100%.  Based on the generally 
prudent assumptions above, we estimate this to be ~25% total liabilities. This means that for our illustrative 
insurer ceding a material portion (~25%) of liabilities via AIR and experiencing a severe credit stress and 
simultaneous recapture of all its AIR exposure from all counterparties (and wit hout further management actions) 
will be in a position to just meet regulatory capital requirements (i.e. pay policyholders after a further 1 in 200 
year event).” 
- “It is possible to develop more severe stresses that might threaten the solvency of individual firms (even after 
accounting for management actions), however given the regulatory and firm level safeguards in place and the 
analysis above, it is important not to conflate this with systemic risk (as defined and discussed previously). We 
therefore also investigate the potential impact of recapture on the UK sector as a whole; noting that ~$22 BN 
liabilities are ceded to Bermuda from the UK.” 
- “Extending the analysis to the UK life industry as a whole, the impact on aggregate cedent solvency is 
dampened, reflecting the lower use of AIR across the industry compared to the illustrative individual cedent.”  
- “In summary, we do not believe that the current magnitude of asset-intensive business ceded to Bermuda 
contributes to systemic risk. With use of AIR increasing, we are also generally supportive of the PRA’s evolving 
expectations for firms in managing the risks associated with AIR.”

Section 4.3.1 Supervisory concerns: In the spirit of providing a more comprehensive overview of the subject and 
to help facilitate collaboration between the industry and supervisors on this important topic, we believe the IAIS 
should provide further insight on the highest ranked concern “Motivation for AIR.” This could include an 
accompanying table that ranks the supervisor’s views on the various motivations and table that ranks what 
perceive to be the primary drivers.  We also believe a table that provides insight on how supervisors rank the 
potential benefits of AIR would be helpful and appropriate to include.   
 
Section 4.3.1 Table 3 – Results of supervisory ranking of risks: In reviewing the results included in Table 3, we 
were struck by the modest ranking for “Recapture risk” given the prominence the of the topic throughout the 
Issues Paper relative to other risks covered in the survey.  It would be helpful for the IAIS to provide context on 
this point and why the paper is not more balanced in its analysis of the various supervisory concerns and 
potential benefits of AIR.   
 
We also believe the table should be accompanied by the following finding from the survey that is included in 
Section 4.3.5 (i.e., we suggest moving the following point to Section 4.3.1): “Overall, the survey responses 
indicate that while the use of AIR varies across jurisdictions, there is a common emphasis on robust risk 
management frameworks, effective governance and the importance of collateral arrangements in mitigating 
counterparty risks. Notable differences include the extent of AIR transactions, and the specific regulatory 
approaches adopted by each jurisdiction. The responses underscore the need for continued vigilance and 
collaboration among supervisors to address the evolving risks associated with AIR.”

Financial stability considerations should be addressed primarily at the jurisdictional level. We note that Section 
5.3 of the Paper explicitly acknowledges that the current exposure to alternative assets and asset -intensive 
reinsurance in the global insurance sector is limited in size, resulting in minimal risk to global financial stability at 
this time. While the Paper identifies potential transmission channels for systemic risk, these remain theoretical 
without empirical evidence of actual systemic impacts. 
The attention given to these issues should be proportionate to their materiality. The significant focus on 
alternative assets and asset-intensive reinsurance in the Paper may not reflect the reality across many 
jurisdictions, where alternative asset allocation remains limited to a small number of insurers. Supervisory 
resources, which are already constrained, should be directed toward material risks within their jurisdictions.  
The appropriate tools already exist at the jurisdictional level, including the Global Monitoring Exercise (GME) as 
part of the Holistic Framework. While we appreciate the IAIS seeking to understand the different transmission 
channels and dynamics, there is not a demonstrated need for additional global supervisory action in this spac e at 
this time.

We believe the analysis of systemic risk should be grounded in evidence rather than assumptions. The scenarios 
presented, such as forced liquidation of illiquid assets, rapid pull-back from lending markets, and mass recapture 
of AIR treaties, are largely hypothetical for life insurance and lack historical precedent. We urge the IAIS to 
document the empirical frequency and severity of past liquidity events in life insurers before assigning systemic 
weight. It is crucial to differentiate between institutional products, where run-like behaviour is conceivable, and 
traditional retail policies, for which contract design and surrender penalties make “bank -run” dynamics highly 
unlikely.  
 
We also request a clear explanation of why the cited risks would overwhelm existing sector -specific safeguards 
like asset-liability duration matching, policyholder protection schemes, and contractual surrender limits. We 
distinguish between the liability matching portfolio and the pre-positioned high-quality liquid asset (HQLA) buffer 
that insurers are required to size through ORSA and supervisory liquidity stress testing. Since day -to-day and 
stress period cash outflows are met from this HQLA bucket, insurers typically do not need to liquidate longer -
dated, illiquid assets held against their liabilities, which significantly reduces the risk of “fire sales” compared to 
what the issues paper suggests. 
 
We also recognize the high substitutability within private-credit markets. Insurers are only one type of lender in 
private-credit and infrastructure-debt markets. Should insurers reduce their involvement, other entities like 
pension funds, asset managers, or even banks, if regulations are eased, could step in. Although the paper 
implicitly acknowledges this, it still frames an insurer pull-back as a systemic shock. We recommend reframing 
the “rapid pull-back” scenario as a market-share shift rather than a financial stability threat, unless compelling 
data demonstrates otherwise. 
 
Furthermore, we contend that “mass recapture” should not be treated as a sector-wide tail risk. There is no 
documented precedent for a sector-wide, synchronous recapture of AIR treaties. For funds-with-held and 
modified coinsurance structures, which the paper specifically mentions, assets remain on the cedant’s ba lance 
Some revisions 
done. Additional 
insights will be 
considered in 
future work eg 
Application 
Paper.

sheet, making “recapture” primarily an accounting reversal. In cases where full coinsurance is used, orderly -run-
off provisions, collateralization, and rating-based termination clauses already mitigate tail risk. Therefore, Section 
5 should not position mass recapture as a macroprudential scenario requiring blanket capital or liquidity buffers.  
 
Finally, we believe it is important to balance the concept of “interconnectedness” with the proven benefits of 
global diversification. While reinsurance inherently increases links across firms and jurisdictions, this 
interconnectedness is precisely how shocks are spread and absorbed. Portraying cross -border risk transfer as 
dangerous risks undermining the core social value of reinsurance, which is the efficient pooli ng of long-dated 
biometric and investment risks. It also risks accelerating regulatory fragmentation, leading to increased protection 
gaps and higher capital costs. We suggest the paper reflect the Financial Stability Board’s long -standing view: 
interconnectedness has two sides, and supervisors should map and monitor it, not curtail it.

In light of the findings that the use of “alternative” assets does not pose a risk to financial stability, ACLI strongly 
recommends augmenting this section with a sentence, at the beginning of section 5, stating that “Due to the 
limited size of current exposure to “alternative” assets and AIR in the global insurance sector, the risk to global 
financial stability is limited at present.” 
 
We think this clarification is needed to avoid unintentionally telegraphing mixed signals. To avoid this, the Issues 
Paper should also emphasize in Section 5 the finding that the risk to financial stability is limited. That finding is 
currently buried in the next -to-last paragraph in the Executive Summary and in the last section (on page 62 in 
Section 5.3) addressing Macroprudential Risk.  
 
The finding about the current limited risk to financial stability should be highlighted as a stand -alone statement. In 
the current draft, the finding about limited risk is undercut by a sentence that immediately follows it – which 
claims increases could “increase the risks to global financial stability.”   
 
These potentially conflicting conclusions and extensive discussions of what are primarily theoretical risks - could 
mislead some readers to believe the IAIS is stating that structural shifts pose a current or imminent threat. 
Including a stand-alone statement about the limited risk may help prevent that misunderstanding and promote 
alignment between the conclusions in the Holistic Framework and this Issues Paper.  
 
ACLI recommends the Issues Paper incorporate language from the Holistic Framework to promote consistencies 
in discussions about financial stability risks and to generally remind those outside of the insurance community 
that insurance is typically a volatility reducer.

ACLI appreciates the Paper’s acknowledgement that the “impact of policyholder behaviour may vary depending 
on jurisdictional differences with regard to regulatory frameworks and liquidity in combination with local 
characteristics in terms of overall insurer allocations.” The structural design and features built into U.S. insurance 
products, which have long-term liabilities such as annuities and pension obligations, makes a run scenario 
unlikely and lessens the likelihood of forced asset fire sales during stress events. 
 
With that in mind, ACLI cautions against the uniform use of a “market wide liability -driven event” to analyse 
potential disruption to financial markets – because there are jurisdictional and product differences that may make 
the likelihood of such an event more or less likely in a given jurisdiction. While recent events may have 
demonstrated that mass lapses may occur in certain jurisdictions, (particularly those where product features do 
not discourage disintermediation and products are sought for investment-like features as opposed to long-term 
retirement security), the risk of mass lapse can vary significantly across jurisdictions due to product features, tax 
consequences, and regulatory frameworks. 
 
We recommend the IAIS to re-evaluate the lapse/surrender risk mentioned in this section. ACLI believes that this 
is a jurisdiction specific risk and should not be characterized as a macroprudential risk for several reasons:  
1) Life insurers have a stable liability profile (e.g., annuities with surrender charges, pensions) which 
fundamentally differs from bank deposit bases. Insurers do not face daily redemptions or “runs” like banks do.  
2) Some jurisdictions, like the US, require robust stress testing of coverage ratios for adverse scenarios. Results 
typically show insurers are maintaining ample liquidity, even in the event that stress events prompt greater than 
anticipated lapses.  
3) Life insurers, especially those in the retirement-services space hold liabilities over multi-decade horizons, 
allowing them to ride out near-term market disruptions without liquidating assets at distressed prices. 
4) Life Insurers intentionally seek out the illiquidity premium where their liability structures naturally align with 
long-horizon investments. In addition, regulatory frameworks ensure robust liquidity buffers and stress testing 
that guard against short-term volatility.

The Issues Paper acknowledges that there is currently limited global financial stability risk stemming from AIR 
and exposure to alternative assets. It also raises important macroprudential questions centering on liquidity risk, 
asset liquidation under stress, systemic interconnections, and broader financial stability risks. While these are 
legitimate areas for supervisory attention, we believe they remain localized and are appropriately addressed by 
existing supervisory frameworks, including Bermuda’s. Oliver Wyman’s Analysis on Systemic Risk in the 
Bermuda Long-Term Sector (“OW Analysis”) offers several nuanced perspectives that may help contextualize 
and refine the discussion: 
 
1. Systemic Risk Scenarios Require Contextualization 
The Issues Paper raises the possibility that mass recapture of AIR or forced asset sales during stress could pose 
systemic threats. However, the OW Analysis concludes that these risks are unlikely to materialize into systemic 
events, given the current scale and structure of the Bermuda long-term reinsurance sector. The OW Analysis 
examined three scenarios closely linked to concerns raised in the IAIS Issues Paper: a mass recapture scenario, 
Revisions done. 
Proportionality 
applies. 
Additional 
insights will be 
considered for 
future work eg 
Application 
Paper.

a confidence shock scenario resulting in heightened lapses and asset sales, and a scenario that contemplates a 
sudden insurer withdrawal from private credit. 
- Scenario 1 (mass recapture): The analysis found no evidence that such an event would jeopardize the solvency 
of the global insurance industry, citing strong capital positions, collateralization, and recapture protections.  
- Scenario 2 (confidence shock): Historical experience and stress testing suggest that Bermudian reinsurers hold 
sufficient liquid assets, and policyholder surrender behaviours would likely be manageable.  
- Scenario 3 (withdrawal from private credit): Insurers play a growing but still modest role in global credit markets. 
Any pullback would likely be absorbed by other investors. Further, Bermuda constitutes a minor portion of the 
total global market across all asset classes, highlighting its less material role and unlikeliness to cause global 
financial instability. 
 
2. AIR is a Risk Transfer Mechanism, not a Risk Creator 
This section of the Issues Paper contemplates that AIR arrangements might amplify systemic risk. The OW 
Analysis instead demonstrates that AIR is primarily a risk transfer tool that can enhance capital efficiency and 
allow insurers to manage long-duration liabilities more effectively. The key risk to the cedent is counterparty 
exposure, not the underlying insurance or market risk that the reinsurer assumes, and this exposure is generally 
mitigated through collateral arrangements, investment guidelines, recapture rights,  and Bermuda Monetary 
Authority (BMA) supervision of the reinsurer, including regulatory capital requirements (e.g., Bermuda’s BSCR 
framework). 
 
3. The Role of Liquidity in Insurance Differs from Banking 
The Issues Paper flags the risk of forced asset sales akin to a bank-style liquidity run. The OW Analysis 
emphasizes the structural differences between insurance and banking, particularly in the nature and timing of 
liabilities which the IAIS itself acknowledges in Section 3.2.1. Insurers’ liabilities are generally long-dated and 
predictable, and 1) most policies do not allow for immediate liquidity; 2) policyholder lapse behaviour has been 
historically stable; and regulatory liquidity stress tests (e.g., Bermuda’s Liquidity Coverage Ratio) show resilience 
even in extreme scenarios. 
 
4. Data Gaps and Transparency Concerns are Valid, but Solutions Should be Risk -Based 
Both the Issues Paper and the OW Analysis agree on the importance of closing data gaps around alternative 
asset exposures and AIR arrangements. The OW Analysis supports increased public transparency and 
disclosures but warns against overly broad or blunt regulatory classifications of “alternative assets.” Instead, it 
advocates a risk-based monitoring approach, considering specific asset and liability characteristics. Instead of 
issuing prescriptive guidance, we recommend further monitoring of Bermuda’s (and other similarly advanced

supervisory authorities) supervisory framework and enhancements and drawing from the BMA’s success to 
formulate best practices.

• The paper seems to overstate the macroprudential risks associated with insurers’ investments in alternative 
assets with AIR and alternative assets are treated as headline systemic risks.  However, data referred to 
indicates that their global share remains modest.  
• As noted earlier in our comments, we suggest for the IAIS to adopt a broader horizon to identify structural risks 
including digital disruption, climate impacts, and longevity shocks.  Future risks scenarios should consider 
longevity and ageing, not just financial contagion. These are the core drivers of life insurance transformation and 
product innovation 
• Life insurers are typically patient, long-term investors not subject to run risk or forced liquidation scenarios that 
might apply to other financial institutions. Their long-dated liabilities allow them to withstand market volatility and 
avoid procyclical investment behaviour. 
• While the draft correctly highlights potential contagion risks associated with insurers’ investments in alternative  
assets, it fails to present a full picture. Banks are, particularly in Europe, a ‘concentration hub’ of systemic risks, 
and a possible engine of risk propagation 
• When systemic / contagion risks are considered, it is important to consider alternative assets relative to 
‘traditional’ assets such as corporate bonds. Within the investment grade public corporate bond universe, 
financial issuers represent 41% of bonds in the EUR investment grade market (or 55% if covered bonds are 
included); and 31% in the USD investment grade market. Insurers focused on ‘traditional’ assets therefore tend 
to be highly concentrated in the financial sector, and banks in particular.  
One may contrast this with the sector representation of corporate lending: financials represent 7% of the USD 
corporate loan universe and 4% of the EUR loan universe (source: LCD index).  
• Given the interconnectedness of banks, this concentration of traditional assets in bank bonds is a potential 
source of systemic risk exposure, particularly when compared to the sector composition of corporate loans: a 
small fraction of the borrower base for loans are in the financial sector. The connectivity of banks and insurers is 
illustrated in the ECB financial stability report (link): “the main euro area banking groups held over EUR 11 billion 
in debt and equity securities issued by insurers”, and “Euro area insurance corporations and pension funds 
(ICPFs) hold around 15% of euro area bank bonds” 
• The three key risks identified (forced liquidation, rapid pullback from lending markets, and disruption from mass 
recapture of AIR) are theoretical concerns that seem to lack empirical evidence and that do not adequately 
consider insurers’ robust risk management frameworks and regulatory safeguards already in place

Council, United 
States 
 
The Issues Paper concludes that there is a need for additional data on alternative asset investments and AIR, 
and that “certain data items are only available in a limited number of jurisdictions.” See Section 3.3.3. Such a 
concession should be sufficient to conclude that additional data is necessary to produce a report that is fit for 
purpose. Although it does appear that the IAIS has collected a limited amount of relevant data (e.g., via the 
GIMAR), which was utilized to conclude that alternative assets “currently do not pose a global financial stability 
concern” (see Section 3.2), there are also abundant objective studies and analyses by scholars, industry groups, 
and others that can be leveraged to develop a more objective report and provide clarificatio n on some of the 
misconceptions referenced therein. For example:   
 
• Macroeconomic Considerations: The Federal Reserve Bank of Boston recently concluded that, in comparison 
to traditional banks, private credit funds are less vulnerable to runs and that overall financial stability risk would 
decrease to the extent that (as the Issues Paper suggests) private credit lending represents a credit substitution 
(i.e., private credit firms are lending to borrowers that might otherwise seek loans from a traditional bank). See 
Federal Reserve Bank of Boston, Current Policy Perspectives, Could the Growth of Private Credit Pose a Risk to 
Financial System Stability?, May 2025. Indeed, objective studies indicate that alternative assets like CLOs “do 
not pose a systemic risk to the U.S. financial system.” See LSTA, Assessing Whether Leveraged Loans and 
CLOs Pose a Systemic Risk., December 2023. To the contrary, alternative asset investments like CLOs are 
unlikely to present a global systemic risk in the future because they are stable, there exists a strong internal 
governance framework with respect to the investments, they will represent a relatively small proportion of insurer 
investment portfolios, and insurers remain obligated to demonstrate adequate capital and liquidity  buffers, among 
others.  
 
• Valuation Uncertainty: There are a number of methods to value alternative assets which are broadly accepted 
and have been used for many years. The International Private Equity and Venture Capital Valuation Guidelines, 
for example, contemplate a wide array of alternative asset valuations.  In addition, insurers and their third-party 
investment managers have long track records of being able to accurately identify and value appropriate 
investments. See e.g., Young Soo Jang and Ginha Kim, Valuation Discipline in Private Credit, March 2025.  
 
• Illiquidity: Although the Issues Paper takes issue with illiquid assets, such assets actually reduce risk for a given 
level of return. See e.g., National Association of State Retirement Administrators, NASRA Issue Brief: Public 
Pension Plan Investment Return Assumptions, updated February 2017; Hamilton Lane data via Morgan Stanley 
Research, How Long Will the Golden Age of Private Markets Last?, February 2021.

We believe structural shifts identified can be well addressed within the IAIS Holistic Framework for systemic risk.  
One key observation in the draft Paper is that current exposures to alternative assets and AIR, at an aggregate 
level, remain relatively small, but the pace of growth in these areas is significant. We think this is an important 
point of context: there is no immediate indication that these trends have already created systemic vulnerabilities 
– rather, they are emerging risks that could materialize in the future if growth continues unchecked or if 
accompanied by inadequate risk management. This suggests that the appropriate macroprudential approach for 
now is heightened surveillance and preparedness, rather than alarm.

Section 5 – Macroprudential and financial stability considerations arising from structural shifts in life insurance  
 
The Issues Paper delves into the macroprudential dimension of the above trends, questioning how greater 
alternative asset exposures and complex reinsurance structures might affect the broader financial system’s 
stability.  However, it is crucial to note the scale and context. Even after several years of growth, alternative 
assets still constitute a minority of U.S. life insurers’ portfolios (the bulk remains in traditional fixed-income 
assets), and AIR transactions, while sizable for some firms, are structured with loss -absorbing collateral that 
mitigates contagion risk. 
 
The macroprudential framing in the draft issues paper sometimes veers toward speculative or precautionary 
language not grounded in either data or real-world supervisory experience. IAIS expresses concern that 
increased exposure to alternative assets and complex reinsurance structures might elevate systemic risks. 
However, these concerns should be rigorously tied to evidence. Indeed, research by the IMF and the Financial 
Stability Board has consistently shown that insurers, particularly life insurers, do not typically amplify 
procyclicality in markets, nor are they prone to sudden runs or rapid liquidation of assets. 
Noted.

While AIR and alternative assets are highlighted as systemic risks, their global footprint remains limited. A 
broader perspective that includes structural risks such as digital disruption, climate change, and longevity would 
better reflect the key forces shaping life insurance transformation and innovation. 
 
We strongly advise IAIS to clearly distinguish between company specific concerns and systemic risk. Innovation 
and structural evolution in the sector should not be conflated with instability. AIR and al ternative assets can 
enhance resilience and reduce macroprudential risk when well-supervised.

We recognize that economic, business, and credit cycles are inherent aspects of financial markets. We also 
believe that life insurers, by their nature of matching long-term liabilities with long-term assets, can offer a 
countercyclical force in credit extension across various markets, provided appropriate incentives are in place.  
 
We believe concerns regarding forced liquidation for insurers are generally overstated. While increased asset 
leverage or illiquidity might elevate certain microprudential risks, which we can effectively manage, this does not 
necessarily lead to broader macroprudential impacts. Similarly, worries about insurers withdrawing from lending 
markets often overlook the availability of other non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs). The growth of NBFI 
lending, in part a response to increased banking regulations, suggests that these entities, including banks, would 
readily step in if insurers were to reduce their private lending activities. 
 
Furthermore, we find concerns about widespread recapture of AIR to be largely implausible, as this scenario 
hinges on the near-simultaneous failure of multiple large reinsurers. In the absence of such an extreme event, 
both the ceding insurer and the reinsurer retain termination options, typically with a 9 0-day notice period. Both 
parties would be incentivized to exercise these options in a manner that preserves their financial strength. 
Additionally, regulators would likely intervene to mitigate financial stress through tailored arrangements, thereby 
alleviating concerns about forced rebalancing.

While ACLI recognizes the IAIS’ interest in surveying and understanding macroprudential risks, we disagree with 
the assertion that the current activity around “alternative” assets and AIR constitutes a macroprudential risk.   
 
This section should be revised to align with the IAIS Holistic Framework on Systemic Risk and ICP 24. The 
Holistic Framework discusses the importance of supervisory policy measures for globally systemic institutions 
whose distress or disorderly failure could cause “significant disruption to the global financial system and 
economic activity. Pages 59-60 do not use the word “global” or include any reference to “significant disruption(s)” 
or the “global insurance sector.” Section 5, in general, appears to be describing a collection of pot ential risks, 
some of which are microprudential, not macroprudential issues. The Paper should clarify what level of risk it is 
addressing. 
 
Some revisions 
done. Additional 
insights can be 
considered for 
future work.

To ensure that Section 5 aligns with the Holistic Framework and ICP 24, Section 5 should include verbiage from 
the Holistic Framework that emphasizing that while the paper is examining potential risks, the insurance 
business model inherently contributes to financial stability.  
 
***Recommended edits to (para. 1) in Section 5.1:  
(1) Add this additional language after the first sentence of the first paragraph: <<“While the focus is on potential 
risks associated with AIR and “alternative” assets, it is important to emphasize that the insurance business model 
inherently contributes to financial stability, including by providing long-term sources of funding to other institutions 
and by promoting risk management through the pooling and diversification of risk.”>>  
 
Failing to include this language could lead outsiders to conclude that the IAIS had undercut its longstanding 
finding that the insurance business model is generally a net contributor to financial stability through its steady, 
long-term investments. Furthermore, this Section should either declare more forcefully that the risks described in 
this section do not currently constitute a systemic risk or threat to financial stability, or provide additional 
empirical analysis demonstrating how these broad, theoretical assertions. 
 
This section is vague and does not provide any evidence that the connections life insurers have to the broader 
financial sector are to such a degree that an event impacting a single or many life insurers would hit the 
“significant disruption” thresholds described in the Holistic Framework for Systemic Risk. 
 
The Issues Paper’s claims about insurer behaviour in a downturn (p. 60) would benefit from additional support or 
analysis. ACLI agrees that insurers are generally countercyclical investors whose long -term assets and liabilities 
create less pressure for insurers to sell in down-markets. However, the Issues Paper makes a relatively 
unsupported claim that evidence of countercyclicality is “mixed” and cites a European Central Bank publicat ion 
that discussed government bond holdings; however, the Issues Paper also notes that the bonds may have been 
sold off after they were downgraded from investment grade. That mixed evidence – from a single region - does 
not appear to support such conclusory statements – particularly on a global level.   
 
Furthermore, the 2nd paragraph under “forced liquidation or fire sales” (p. 60) cites policyholder behaviour and 
the potential for mass lapses as a key risk. This paragraph would also benefit from further a nalysis. The 
concerns cited on page 60 are issues that are jurisdictionally specific – including the nature of the liability (long 
duration or short duration). Deterrents to surrender also vary widely – in the U.S. there are surrender charges, 
tax consequences, and other product features that tend to mitigate against lapse risk. The Paper attempts to 
address the jurisdictional differences in the last sentence – ACLI recommends making a clearer statement that

mass lapses are a jurisdictional-specific risk that is more appropriately addressed at the national supervisory 
level than a global level.  
 
ACLI also cautions against using the (unlikely) possibility of a “market-wide liability-driven event” (p. 61) as a 
trigger that would generate mass recapture because, as noted above, there are jurisdictional and product 
differences that may make the likelihood of such an event more or less likely in a given jurisdiction. While recent 
events have demonstrated that mass lapses can occur in certain jurisdictions, (particularly those where product 
features do not discourage disintermediation and where products are sought for short -term investment-like 
features, as opposed to long-term retirement security), the risk of mass lapse can vary significantly across 
jurisdictions due to product features, tax consequences, and regulatory frameworks. If the Issues Paper retains 
this section, it should be clearer that the applicability of this risk varies by jurisdiction.  
 
As such, we recommend the IAIS to re-evaluate the lapse/surrender risk mentioned in this section. ACLI believes 
that this is a jurisdiction specific risk and should not be characterized as a macroprudential risk for several 
reasons:  
1) Life insurers have a stable liability profile (e.g., annuities with surrender charges, pensions) which 
fundamentally differs from bank deposit bases. Insurers do not face daily redemptions or “runs” like banks do.  
2) Some jurisdictions, like the US, require robust stress testing of coverage ratios for adverse scenarios. Results 
typically show insurers are maintaining ample liquidity, even in the event that stress events prompt greater than 
anticipated lapses.  
3) Life insurers, especially those in the retirement-services space hold liabilities over multi-decade horizons, 
allowing them to ride out near-term market disruptions without liquidating assets at distressed prices. 
4) Insurers intentionally seek out the illiquidity premium where their liability structures naturally align with lo ng-
horizon investments. In addition, regulatory frameworks ensure robust liquidity buffers and stress testing that 
guard against short-term volatility. 
 
As noted in the Paper, the current global activity related to these structural shifts remains limited and 
concentrated within a few highly regulated jurisdictions, including the US and Bermuda. Both jurisdictions are 
already adapting and enhancing their frameworks to address these structural shifts. In light of the finding that 
these activities do not pose a material risk to financial stability, ACLI strongly recommends that the IAIS continue 
to monitor these trends, assess how jurisdictions with significant AIR and “alternative” asset activity are 
addressing evolving risks and supervisory concerns, defer to local supervisory refinements, and continue to 
engage in ongoing dialogue with industry, stakeholders and jurisdictions to potentially identify best practices for 
other IAIS members.

We recommend that this section be amended to read, "The key objective of macroprudential policy for the

shocks. While the focus is on potential risks associated with AIR and “alternative” assets, it more important to 
recognise that the insurance business model inherently contributes to financial stability, including by providing 
long-term sources of funding and by promoting effective risk management through the pooling and diversification 
of risk.” 
 
The Consultation misunderstand the risk of lapse / surrender in the US.  Therefore, we strongly support the 
ACLI's comments, including: 
 
[W]e recommend the IAIS to re-evaluate the lapse/surrender risk mentioned in this section. ACLI believes that 
this is a jurisdiction specific risk and should not be characterized as a macroprudential risk for several reasons:  
1) Life insurers have a stable liability profile (e.g., annuities with surrender charges, pensio ns) which 
fundamentally differs from bank deposit bases. Insurers do not face daily redemptions or “runs” like banks do.  
2) Some jurisdictions, like the US, require robust stress testing of coverage ratios for adverse scenarios. Results 
typically show insurers are maintaining ample liquidity, even in the event that stress events prompt greater than 
anticipated lapses.  
3) Life insurers, especially those in the retirement-services space hold liabilities over multi-decade horizons, 
allowing them to ride out near-term market disruptions without liquidating assets at distressed prices. 
4) Insurers intentionally seek out the illiquidity premium where their liability structures naturally align with long -
horizon investments. In addition, regulatory frameworks ensure robust liquidity buffers and stress testing that 
guard against short-term volatility.

Athene, USA 
Please see response to Q15. 
 
Life-insurers’ liability structure, product design, and consumer protections already neutralize the three risks 
flagged by the IAIS. Forced liquidation scenarios are remote, well-contained by existing Insurance Core 
Principles (ICPs), and do not justify new macro-prudential overlays. 
 
FORCED LIQUIDATION ("SURRENDER") RISK IS STRUCTURALLY REMOTE  
    1. Stable funding model. Insurers such as Athene, USA match long-dated assets to equally long-dated 
liabilities; 85 % of Athene, USA’s liabilities are either non-surrenderable or carry a penalty. Supervisors should 
appropriately focus on insurers taking excessive lapse risks, such as Eurovita, to ensure that firms maintain 
asset-liability management practices and avoid undue exposure to policyholder behavior risks. 
 
Noted. Insights 
can be 
considered for 
future analysis eg 
Application 
Paper.

2. Built-in deterrents. Surrender charges, Market-Value Adjustments (MVAs) and disincentives ensure that 
early exits are economically unattractive; MVAs re-price the liability at current A-rated yields, protecting 
remaining policyholders. 
 
    3. Capital resilience. Internal stress tests can demonstrate that an insurer can withstand draconian lapse 
scenarios without breaching liquidity or solvency thresholds. 
 
    4. Market stabilizers. Like pensions, life-insurers are buy-and-hold investors; empirical work finds they act 
counter-cyclically, not pro-cyclically.  (See e.g., Timmer, Cyclical Investment Behavior Across Financial 
Institutions, 2017, pp. 1-4). 
 
CONSUMER BEHAVIOR FURTHER DAMPENS SURRENDER SPIKES 
    1. Fee-averse policyholders. Annuity buyers value principal protection, tax deferral, and guaranteed income; 
historic lapse data confirm reluctance to pay surrender charges. 
 
    2. Suitability standards. U.S. advisers must document that any replacement is in the client’s best interest, 
sharply limiting opportunistic switches while charges apply. 
 
PRODUCT DESIGN RECYCLES LAPSES INTO STONGER PORTFOLIOS  
Once the charge period ends, insurers typically lower credited rates; customers who surrender can reinvest at 
new-money rates, while the insurer redeploys released capital into fresh, long-duration business-raising overall 
portfolio quality. (Appendix, slides 15-20). 
 
RAPID PULLBACK OF INSURERS FROM KEY LENDING MARKETS DUE TO DEFAULTS AND 
DOWNGRADES 
The scenario outlined in section 5.1 presumes that downgrades or defaults could trigger a swift, sector-wide 
retreat by life insurers from private credit markets, leaving a financing gap large enough to systemically threaten 
financial stability. Our analysis suggests this outcome is remote. Four contextual po ints may help the IAIS 
calibrate the actual risk. 
 
    1. Depth and substitutability of the credit ecosystem. Insurers are important providers of long -dated capital, yet 
they operate alongside banks, asset-management vehicles, business-development companies, and specialty 
finance firms. In prior credit cycles these participants have stepped in – often rapidly -– when relative-value 
dynamics shifted. The depth and diversity of today’s private credit market therefore dilute the systemic

consequences of any temporary insurer retrenchment. 
 
    2. “Hold-to-maturity” portfolio construction. Life insurers structure their balance sheets around asset liability 
matching. At Athene, USA, for example, asset cash flows are closely aligned with liability outflows, leaving little 
expectation or incentive for mid-cycle asset sales. Portfolios are predominantly investment grade, senior-secured 
and covenant-rich, and are supported by high quality liquid asset buffers established through ORSA-based 
stress testing. These design features materially reduce the likelihood that downgrades would force disorderly 
deleveraging. 
 
    3. Historical drivers of insurer stress. Empirical experience shows that past insurer failures have nearly always 
originated on the liability side (e.g., mis-priced guarantees) rather than from broad-based credit losses. Even 
during periods of elevated default rates, well underwritten investment grade assets have proved resilient, 
reinforcing the view that credit downgrades alone are unlikely to precipitate mass exits from lending markets. 
 
    4. Limited macro-prudential relevance of recapture risk. Concerns about “mass recapture” of asset intensive 
reinsurance (AIR) arrangements understate existing safeguards. Funds withheld and modified coinsurance 
structures keep collateralized assets ring fenced, often within the ceding company’s home jurisdiction, while 
contractual triggers, reserving rules and rating-based collateral cushions are designed to support an orderly run-
off rather than forced liquidation. These mechanisms contain potential liquidity shocks at the firm level and 
prevent them from propagating across markets. 
 
SUGGESTED REFINEMENTS TO SECTION 5.1-- ADDITIONAL POINTS FOR IAIS CONSIDERATION 
    1. Ground conclusions in evidence, not assumptions 
       - Catalogue the observed frequency and severity of past insurer-related liquidity events before attaching 
systemic significance. 
       - Distinguish institutional reinsurance flows from retail policyholder behavior when evaluatin g “run” dynamics. 
       - Test whether existing safeguards (ALM discipline, ORSA-driven liquidity buffers, and stress-testing) would, 
in fact, prove inadequate in a severe but plausible scenario. 
       - Separate the long-dated ALM portfolio from the pre-positioned high-quality liquid-asset (HQLA) buffer that 
ORSA already requires; the two serve different purposes and should be analyzed accordingly.  
 
    2. Acknowledge the depth and substitutability of private credit markets. Insurers are meaningful lende rs, but 
banks, asset managers, and specialty finance firms routinely provide alternative funding. Historical episodes 
show these actors can and do replace insurer supply when relative value shifts.

3. Re-calibrate “mass recapture” assumptions. Sector-wide recapture of asset-intensive reinsurance is not a 
credible macro-prudential threat: 
       - Funds withheld and modified coinsurance structures ring-fence collateral under the ceding insurer’s control. 
       - Contractual run-off provisions, collateralization, and rating-based termination clauses promote orderly 
settlement rather than forced liquidation. 
 
   4. Recognize the cushioning role of reinsurance interconnectedness. By pooling long -dated risks across 
geographies and market cycles, reinsurance spreads shocks rather than amplifying them, further lowering the 
probability that an individual stress event propagates into a systemic one.

While the Issues Paper highlights potential risks, including forced asset liquidations, increased 
interconnectedness, and market contagion, the Oliver Wyman Analysis of Systemic Risk in the Bermuda Long -
Term Insurance Sector (“OW Analysis”) offers analytical findings that demonstrate these risks, while worth 
monitoring, are overstated in terms of potential systemic impact.  
 
Regarding specific elements of the section, we note that concerns about forced liquidation (“run on the bank”) 
scenarios are typically overstated for insurers.  While it is true that asset leverage or illiquidity may increase 
certain microprudential risks (that can be managed effectively), it is unclear that they translate into 
macroprudential effects. The OW Analysis’ second scenario explores this possibility and finds it unlikely that 
Bermudian reinsurers would be forced into liquidation of illiquid assets, even under extreme stress. A substantial 
portion of liabilities reinsured in Bermuda either do not offer policyholder liquidity or include contractual features 
Noted for further 
analysis. 
Proportionality 
applies. 
Supervisory 
approaches and 
other 
improvements 
can be 
considered in an

that discourage surrender. Concerns about forced liquidation can be addressed by effective liquidity risk 
management and supervisory oversight. For instance, the Bermuda Monetary Authority requires reinsurers to 
maintain an LCR, and current stress test results indicate median post-stress liquidity levels in excess of 360%, 
well above regulatory minimums. Furthermore, the total surrender value of liabilities across the Bermuda market 
is less than the value of liquid assets held, suggesting that even in a full surrender scenario, liquidity demands 
could be met without triggering fire sales of alternative assets. We also believe the possibility that multiple 
insurers would have near-simultaneous liquidity-based failures, leading to real economy impacts, is extremely 
remote. 
 
Similarly, concerns about the mass recapture of AIR seem premised on a near -simultaneous distress or failure of 
multiple large reinsurers, triggering recapture clauses. We believe the likelihood of this is also extremely remote, 
however, the OW Analysis directly addresses this scenario, concluding that even under a severe credit crisis 
leading to widespread recapture from Bermuda reinsurers, there is no indication that such an event would 
materially threaten the solvency of cedents or the broader global insurance market. This conclusion rests on 
several structural safeguards common to AIR transactions (e.g., investment guidelines, collateral requirements, 
strong capital positions, recapture rights, etc.) that are not adequately considered by t he Issues Paper. The 
Issues Paper also does not adequately consider jurisdictional supervision measures in place to mitigate these 
risks (e.g., solvency and liquidity requirements and stress testing, macroprudential analysis, etc.). Even when 
such clauses are triggered, a 90-day notice is typically required, giving cedents time to evaluate, address, and 
mitigate the effects of recapture. Involved supervisors would have an incentive to work together and to allow time 
for appropriately measured actions. These provisions, along with regulatory cooperation and coordination, would 
mitigate recapture-related macroprudential concerns, such as concerns about forced rebalancing.  
 
Finally, we believe the concerns about the pullback from lending markets are premised on a lack of 
substitutability. While acknowledging insurers’ growing presence in private credit, the OW Analysis finds that the 
Bermuda long-term insurance sector comprises less than 1% of global credit markets. As such, any reduction in 
their private credit activity is unlikely to have systemic effects. Moreover, the infrastructure of the private credit 
ecosystem – originations, structuring, and administration – is supported by a broad range of financial institutions, 
which can readily absorb insurer retrenchment if credit spreads widen. Market dynamism is normal, and we 
believe should not be inherently a cause for concern. 
 
Taken together, the findings in the OW Analysis support a supervisory approach grounded in targeted monitoring 
and enhanced transparency rather than restrictions. The Issues Paper’s concern about systemic risk should be 
matched with proportional oversight that differentiates between firm-specific risks and system-wide exposures. 
Application 
Paper.

As the OW Analysis recommends, improvements in public disclosure and counterparty risk frameworks are 
prudent, but regulatory intervention based on overly broad definitions of “alternative assets” or blanket 
assumptions about AIR structures risks unintended consequences for both market functionality and policyholder 
protection.

In analysing the insurance sector from a macroprudential perspective, we encounter an inherent paradox 
concerning interconnectedness. Reinsurance, a key form of interconnectedness, is designed to spread risk, 
which is generally viewed as beneficial for financial stability. However, this same interconnectedness also carries 
the potential for stress to propagate more broadly across the system, leading to systemic effects. It is important 
to note that the interconnectedness observed in asset-intensive reinsurance (AIR) arrangements is consistent 
with the interconnectedness found in other forms of reinsurance.

 The limited global impact indicates that insurers’ interconnections with other parts of the financial markets may 
have quantitatively increased, but they have not risen to the point that they pose a systemic risk or threat to 
financial stability. With that in mind, producing an educational issues paper analysing these issues is a positive 
step for the IAIS.  However, it would be premature for the IAIS to issue global policy or modify the Insurance 
Core Principles in the absence of a more complete, data-informed analysis of insurers’ interconnections with 
other parts of the financial sector.
