Athene, USA 
The Issues Paper suggests that life insurers’ growing participation in “alternative” asset markets heightens 
contagion risk, chiefly through leverage and cross-sector links. A closer look at market structures, balance sheet 
design, and existing safeguards indicates that the systemic threat is far lower than implied. 
 
   1. Liability structure anchors stability. Life insurers fund themselves with long-duration, stable liabilities, and 
unlike banks, do not engage in maturity transformation. This balance-sheet profile makes insurers natural shock 
absorbers rather than amplifiers.   
 
   2. Private-credit vehicles are ring-fenced and lightly levered 
       - Modest leverage. Typical private-debt funds employ ≤ 1.5× leverage—an order of magnitude below the 10-
12× range common to banks. 
       - Legal separation. Funds are bankruptcy-remote entities; any distress is contained within the vehicle, 
shielding both the sponsor and third-party investors. 
       - Market footprint. Middle-market sponsor lending—often cited as a pressure point—represents barely 3% of 
outstanding U.S. credit (approximately USD 1.7 trn versus approximately USD 50 trn total) and is spread across 
more than 1,000 funds, limiting concentration risk. 
 
   3. Post-crisis structured-credit reforms curb pro-cyclicality

factors. Risk

- Lower advance rates and wider collateral buckets have reduced leverage and improved diversification.  
       - Market-value triggers that once forced fire-sales have largely disappeared. 
       - Long-dated funding now predominates, eliminating the rollover risk that fed earlier crises.  
    
   4. Insurer-level safeguards further mute contagion channels.  Athene, USA and peers operate within 
reasonable leverage limitations, portfolio diversification limits, independent risk committees and strict arm’ s-
length rules for affiliate transactions. ORSA-driven stress testing and liquidity buffers add another layer of 
protection. 
 
   5. Implications for the IAIS assessment 
       - Calibrate leverage concerns to empirical data. Private credit leverage is a fraction of bank levels, and 
current structures are bankruptcy remote. 
       - Recognize the limited footprint of middle-market lending. Its small, highly distributed share of global fixed-
income markets limits systemic spillovers. 
       - Factor in post-crisis structural reforms. Today’s “alternative”-asset vehicles are less levered, longer funded 
and devoid of forced-sale triggers. 
       - Build on existing insurer safeguards. ALM discipline, ORSA liquidity buffers and affiliate -transaction rules 
already inhibit cross-sector contagion. 
 
Taken together, these points suggest that life-insurer involvement in “alternative” assets is unlikely to transmit 
material shocks through the financial system. We therefore recommend that any additional macro -prudential 
measures remain proportionate, data-driven, and focused on clearly demonstrated residual risks.

(BILTIR), Bermuda 
This section identifies increased interconnectedness between life insurers and broader financial markets – 
particularly through growing allocations to alternative assets and the expanded use of AIR – as a potential 
source of systemic risk. While interconnectedness is a legitimate area of macroprudential concern, the Oliver 
Wyman Analysis of Systemic Risk in the Bermuda Long-Term Insurance Sector (“OW Analysis”) offers analysis 
that demonstrates that the degree and nature of such interconnectedness in the Be rmuda long-term sector does 
not present a material systemic threat. 
Points specific to 
Bermuda are 
noted. Some 
issues are 
addressed in

The Issues Paper considers the risk that financial stress in one part of the system – such as an offshore 
reinsurer – could transmit instability across institutions or jurisdictions. The OW Analysis explores this possibility 
in detail by examining a hypothetical credit market crisis triggering widespread recapture of liabilities from 
Bermuda-based reinsurers. Crucially, the report concludes that Bermuda-based reinsurers do not hold 
fundamentally riskier or more concentrated portfolios than peers in other jurisdictions. Asset allocation profiles, 
including exposure to private credit and other alternative assets, broadly resemble those of U.S. -domiciled life 
and annuity insurers. In short, there is no unique concentration of risk within Bermuda that would amp lify 
transmission effects through interconnectedness beyond what exists elsewhere in the global life sector.  
 
Moreover, AIR transactions typically include contractual mechanisms designed to mitigate risks to the cedent. 
These include collateralization and clear investment guidelines, and recapture rights. The Bermuda regulatory 
regime, specifically the BSCR, also helps ensure that reinsurers maintain sufficient capital buffers to withstand 
adverse market scenarios. The OW Analysis emphasizes that most Bermuda reinsurers maintain capital levels 
that exceed the 1-in-200 stress thresholds required by the BSCR. Consequently, the probability of a failure 
triggering cascading effects through the insurance value chain is mitigated both by transaction -level safeguards 
and by firm-level solvency positions. 
 
Additionally, the OW Analysis stresses that the proper framing of interconnectedness should distinguish between 
direct and indirect exposures. While it is true that AIR shifts the risk profile of the cedent from the underlying 
insurance or market risk associated with policies to counterparty risk, this does not inherently increase system -
wide interconnectedness if the reinsurer is well-capitalized, collateralized, and regulated under an equivalent 
solvency regime. Bermuda’s regulatory framework has been deemed equivalent by several key jurisdictions, 
including the European Union under Solvency II, and is subject to ongoing supervision, stress testing, and public 
disclosures. 
 
We also believe concerns about interconnectedness surface an inherent contradiction in the macroprudential 
analysis of the insurance sector.  Reinsurance (a form of interconnectedness) spreads risk, which should be 
considered a positive. While this creates the possibility that a stress spreads to a greater number of participants 
in the system, it is a longstanding practice that is subject to robust underwriting, active risk management, and 
regulatory oversight, as noted above. In general, the interconnectedness of AIR is no different than the 
interconnectedness of other forms of reinsurance. We recommend the Issues Paper clarify that this concern is 
not specific to AIR.  
 
other sections of 
the paper.

Further, we note that affiliation between insurers and asset managers is not new or unique to AIR or alternative 
assets.  Insurers have long employed and benefited from the ability to leverage financial expertise within the 
group to execute asset management responsibilities.  We recommend the paper make the distinction that such 
affiliated relationships are not unique to the structural shifts in the life sector.  
 
In summary, while Section 5.2 appropriately identifies interconnectedness as a transmission cha nnel worth 
monitoring, the evidence presented in the OW Analysis supports the conclusion that interconnectedness 
stemming from Bermuda-based AIR does not currently represent a systemic concern. A more nuanced and 
evidence-based approach would acknowledge the structural protections embedded in these transactions and the 
comparability of Bermuda insurers’ risk profiles to their onshore peers. Supervisory dialogue and transparency 
are warranted, but there is limited justification for broad-based regulatory intervention based solely on 
jurisdictional assumptions or the presence of cross-border AIR activity.

ACLI agrees that the current exposure to “alternative” assets and AIR in the global insurance sector poses a

As noted in the Paper, the current global exposure to these risks remains relatively limited and concentrated 
within a few highly regulated jurisdictions. Rather than producing additional prescriptive guidance or universal 
rules that may conflict with or undermine targeted local approaches, ACLI encourages the IAIS to track how 
jurisdictions with material “alternative” assets and AIR exposure continue to refine their treatment of “alternative” 
assets and AIR. ACLI recommends that the IAIS continue to assess these trends and how jurisdictions with 
significant AIR and “alternative” asset activity are addressing evolving risks and supervisory concerns, and 
continue to engage in ongoing dialogue with industry, stakeholders and jurisdictions to potentially identify best 
practices.

Athene, USA 
The Issues Paper's own findings acknowledge that neither asset-intensive reinsurance nor increased allocations 
to “alternative” assets pose a systemic risk today. This assessment is echoed by the Financial Stability Board 
(FSB), the European Systemic Risk Board (ESRB), EIOPA, and national regulators including the NAIC. Current 
exposure stays limited and concentrated within a few highly regulated jurisdictions.  
 
However, the Issues Paper downplays this fact, barely acknowledging that most AIR activity and i ncreased 
private asset allocation is occurring in two jurisdictions: the US and Bermuda. These are jurisdictions with mature 
regulatory regimes, supervisory cooperation mechanisms, and proven capital adequacy frameworks.  
 
As the Issues Paper acknowledges, the empirical record to date shows no evidence of systemic fragility arising 
from these trends; indeed, they have delivered greater retirement security for policyholders, curtailed de -
capitalization of life insurers, and provided long-term financing for real-economy projects from infrastructure 
buildouts to advanced-manufacturing capacity. 
 
Supervisory frameworks should be informed by jurisdictional experience and empirical data. Neither private 
credit nor asset-intensive reinsurance (AIR) currently pose systemic risk. Supervisory concerns are real, but they 
remain localized and containable under current frameworks. Any proposed policy response should follow 
thorough monitoring and coordination, not precede it.  
 
Long-term, stable liabilities from insurers and investors provide stability to the financial system. These entities do 
not engage in maturity transformation, do not have access to the Fed window, and do not benefit from US 
government guarantees, reducing systemic risks. 
 
Additionally, private credit fund managers must adhere to accounting standards, with valuations reviewed by 
third-party pricing services and external auditors. This rigorous valuation process helps mitigate information risks 
and enhances the stability of the financial system.

(BILTIR), Bermuda 
This section considers the long-term implications of structural shifts in the life insurance sector for global financial 
stability. While this forward-looking focus is understandable, the Oliver Wyman Analysis of Systemic Risk in the 
Bermuda Long-Term Insurance Sector (“OW Analysis”) provides empirical evidence and scenario-based 
modeling that suggests current trends, specifically within the Bermuda long-term reinsurance sector, do not 
meaningfully increase the risk of global financial instability. Importantly, the report proposes practical 
enhancements to regulatory oversight and risk management that can help address emerging issues without 
overestimating the likelihood or scale of systemic fallout.  
 
We appreciate the IAIS’ recognition that “[d]ue to the limited size of current exposure to alternative assets and 
AIR in the global insurance sector, the risk to global financial stability is limited at present.” The OW Analysis also 
finds that systemic risks remain limited in the current environment. The section goes on to note that “the rapid 
pace of growth may increase the risks to global financial stability” which is highly speculative and lacks empirical 
support. The OW Analysis agrees with the need for monitoring but challenges the implication that scale a lone is 
a sufficient indicator of systemic risk. For example, Bermuda reinsurers account for only a small fraction of global 
private credit activity – less than 1% by the report’s estimate. Moreover, insurers’ investments in alternative 
assets are typically long-dated and liability-matched, limiting procyclical pressures or the likelihood of disorderly 
market exits. Even in extreme stress scenarios, as modeled in the report, capital and liquidity buffers among 
Bermuda-based reinsurers are sufficient to absorb shocks without triggering spillovers into broader markets.  
 
Notably, the OW Analysis’s third scenario – examining the consequences of a sudden withdrawal of Bermuda 
insurer demand for private credit – finds that while insurers do contribute stable long-term funding to private 
markets, their exit would not disrupt global capital flows. As we described previously, other investor classes such 
Revisions done. 
Proportionality 
applies. The 
focus in this 
section is 
premised by the 
rapid pace of 
growth in 
connection with 
these shifts.

as pension funds, sovereign wealth funds, and endowments are likely to step in if spreads become more 
attractive, mitigating any potential funding gaps. In this way, the OW Analysis presents a case for market 
adaptability and substitutability, a key macroprudential lens that supports the conclusion that insurers, even as 
they grow in scale, are not irreplaceable actors in the private asset ecosystem.  
 
A supervisory approach grounded in targeted monitoring and enhanced transparency, rather than restrictions,  
would be the more effective and appropriate tool to mitigate the future risk landscape. We believe encouraging 
transparency, stress resilience, and firm-level planning will serve the dual goals of financial stability and 
continued market innovation. 
 
With respect to the discussion in this section, we are concerned with the assertion that increased allocation to 
alternative assets and the use of AIR is a negative or inherently “risky.”  As noted earlier in our response, the 
structural shifts present both risks and opportunities and both the industry and supervisors are actively employing 
and enhancing tools to understand and manage risks associated with these trends.   
 
We also note that the key risks are not unique to alternative assets or AIR (e.g., cedants may experience 
significant lapse experience on business that is not reinsured, insurers that are not investing in alternative assets 
could experience a liquidity stress and need to sell assets and insurers are unlikely to start by attempting to sell 
alternative assets to address liquidity demands, etc.).  The Issues Paper should acknowledge these points.  In 
addition, we believe the Issues Paper should acknowledge the role of these practices in narrowing the retirement 
savings/pension protection gap, which could provide macroeconomic stability.  
 
Further, we observe a disconnect in the IAIS’ messaging within this section.  The section opens noting that the 
size of the current exposure to alternative assets and AIR in the global insurance sector is limited,  but later notes 
that it is in many cases leading to herding behaviour among insurers.  If retained, the IAIS must further define 
what it considers to be “herding behaviour” and better articulate how the point aligns with the broader 
perspectives included in the section.  As a more general matter, we note that the label “herding behaviour” 
incorrectly implies that insurers are acting thoughtlessly. AIR reinsurers are not homogenous, they reinsure many 
different products and utilize varied investment strategies, and substantial consideration is given to these 
practices within the context of the insurers’ diverse business models and risk management frameworks.

Institute of

States 
The existing supervisory framework is fit-for-purpose when it comes to addressing the risks identified in the 
Paper. Many of the supervisory tools already in place across various jurisdictions, including those developed 
through the activities-based approach (ABA), already address the concerns raised in the Paper. Many of the 
potential areas for enhancement set forth in Section 6.3 are already reflected in the Insurance Core Principles 
(ICPs) and related guidance and do not need further elaboration. For example, ICP 3 regarding the inclusion of 
non-insurance supervisors is sufficiently broad to cover arrangements with other sectoral supervisors, while ICP 
7 regarding conflicts of interest is well within existing supervisory mandates and part of regular review s. 
We are concerned that the Paper indicates potential changes to at least ten different ICPs, which appears 
inconsistent with the IAIS's strategic shift away from standard-setting toward implementation. Such a broad-
based revision of supervisory standards seems particularly disproportionate given the Paper's own 
acknowledgment that the identified trends do not present immediate macroprudential risks and involve a 
relatively limited portion of the global insurance market. Much of the strength and credibilit y of the ICPs is derived 
from their consistency and stability over time, as well as their intended role as globally applicable standards that 
provide a consistent framework across all jurisdictions. A stable regulatory environment is essential for enabling  
insurers to make confident long-term strategic decisions and capital commitments. Before considering potential 
enhancements, there should be compelling evidence demonstrating the need for ICP enhancements.  
We believe the focus should be on applying established tools—such as liquidity stress testing and other risk 
assessment methodologies—rather than creating new requirements. We encourage the IAIS to thoroughly 
consider how existing ICPs may address the supervisory concerns raised in the Paper in order to avoid 
potentially duplicative measures. This approach respects the principles of proportional, risk -based regulation that 
leverages existing regulatory tools before developing new ones.  
We note that Section 6.3 identifies ICP 25 on Supervisory Cooperation and Coordination as an area for potential 
enhancement. Given the cross-border nature of reinsurance, effective supervisory coordination is essential, and 
we support strengthening existing mechanisms rather than creating new standards. Specifically, we recommend 
enhancing ICP 25 to improve information sharing and communication of best practices while encouraging 
supervisors to utilize data already available through established channels such as trade repositories and 
thematic discussion forums, while reducing barriers to information sharing through frameworks such as 
Memoranda of Understanding (MoUs). Such collaborative approaches could help address data gap concerns 
without imposing additional reporting requirements on insurers, while ensuring supervisors a cross jurisdictions 
are better equipped to respond as these market trends continue to evolve.  
Regarding public disclosure, potential enhancements under ICP 20 should acknowledge that relevant disclosure

frameworks are often a shared responsibility of insurance supervisors and other markets regulators, and that the

Complex reinsurance arrangements, by their nature, can be difficult to interpret even for sophisticated market 
participants, potentially leading to misunderstandings that undermine rather than enhance market confidence. 
We recommend prioritizing effective supervision over expanded public disclosure requirements. A regulatory 
framework that combines robust supervisory oversight with meaningful supervisor -industry interaction can inspire 
sufficient confidence that policyholders and market participants need not conduct their own technical 
assessments of complex arrangements. 
Rather than creating additional layers of disclosure that may create unwarranted confusion, we believe an 
integrated and proportionate approach that emphasizes information sharing, utilizing existing information 
channels, and removing barriers to supervisory collaboration will address supervisory concerns while supporting 
continued market evolution. To this end, the IAIS could consider developing a "toolbox" approach that provides 
supervisors with resources to understand and address risks, rather than prescriptive standards that may not be 
appropriate for all markets or business models. This would enable supervisors to apply proportionate reviews, 
ensuring that any supervisory response is targeted to those material risks identified in specific transactions or 
markets rather than imposing broad requirements that may not be relevant in all contexts.  
Any framework must carefully balance the public interest in transparency with the legit imate need to protect 
confidential business information. The most effective disclosure system provides appropriate context that 
enables stakeholders to understand relevant risks without requiring specialized expertise to interpret highly 
technical details.

IAIS underreports the weaknesses of public funds 
In addition to underplaying the many strengths of private funds, IAIS appears to ignore many of the reported 
weaknesses of public fund alternatives. The authors of this paper fail to explain the consequences of 
overreliance on Treasury bonds, particularly during prolonged periods of interest rate hikes. A key example of 
this overreliance can be seen with the dramatic financial collapse of Silicon Valley Bank (SVB), which used these 
bonds as their primary source of liquidity for bank withdrawals. To its detriment, SVB relied mostly on holding 
government and government-backed securities to finance its sustainability projects. 
   
With the Federal Reserve Board’s repeated interest rate hikes, SVB’s Treasury bonds continued to lose value 
over time, diminishing the bank’s ability to retain deposits with enough cash in reserve. This bank failure provides 
a dramatic and highly visible risk to relying on government bonds in times of market volatility. While bonds are 
regarded as a relatively “safe” form of investment, they carry its own set of risks that can be inflamed by inflation 
and interest rate hikes. IAIS neglected to mention this relative to life insurance and risks misleading readers 
about the pitfalls of the bond market. 
Beyond bonds, I found it troubling that IAIS made no mention of the potential risks associated with stock options 
like mutual funds and exchange-traded funds. There seems to be no formal mention of these investments in the 
paper aside from classifying the private fund alternative to mutual funds: alternative mutual funds. While mutual 
funds are popular and can offer a range of benefits for life insurance customers, they are not without their own 
set of problems. Some of these include high expense ratios/sales charges, poor trade execution, and tax 
inefficiencies.  Additionally, mutual funds offer a greater risk of mismanagement from one’s financial manager 
that can see an excessively frequent replacement of funds or the unnecessary trading of fund s. This shaky back-
Noted for future 
work.

and-forth may not be suitable for maintaining a stable life insurance policy that depends on more steady growth 
over time. 
  
ETFs, on the other hand, provide investors with a host of benefits, including a set of cheaper, more flexible 
alternative to mutual funds. While ETFs have become more appealing to middle -income investors over the past 
32 years , they face their own risks and shortcomings. These include reportedly higher than normal fees for 
actively managed ETFs and not guaranteeing diversification via asset concentration. To the latter point, some 
ETFs may possess assets that are heavily concentrated in only two or three market positions, which may be less 
diversified than certain rival mutual funds. Another major concern that should have been covered  by the IAIS’s 
paper is that ETFs do carry illiquidity concerns that may hinder trade transactions. This flaw undermines the 
IAIS’s criticism of private funds being characterized by their inherent illiquidity relative to public funds.  
  
The reality is that even highly flexible and cheap public funds like ETFs can lack liquidity if they do not meet all of 
the underlying standards. Liquid ETFs typically carry a greater market depth (the number of available shares to 
trade at the quoted market spread), a reasonable quoted “bid-ask” spread for ETFs on the secondary market, 
and a relatively liquid set of underlying securities comprising an ETF’s basket.  This is all to say that ETFs do not 
automatically carry a liquidity advantage over the private funds criticized in the IAIS’s paper. A final draft of the 
paper should do proper justice by weighing the relative costs and advantages across multiple public fund options 
relative to their private fund counterparts, rather than merely focusing on the risks of alternativ e assets.

We recommend the IAIS take a measured approach to any potential changes to the ICPs and avoid overly 
prescriptive enhancements to preserve the IAIS’ proportionality principle. We specifically believe the IAIS should 
avoid the following: 
 
1. Creating an enduring new class of assets called “alternative assets.”  While this labelling may ultimately be 
deemed necessary for purposes of the Issues Paper, the paper itself notes a lack of consensus around which 
assets fall into the “alternative” bucket. Moreover, the various assets that fall into the alternative assets bucket 
have different risk profiles, undermining the practical benefits of such a definition.  
 
2. Creating a new class of reinsurance contracts called “asset-intensive reinsurance.” The supervisory measures 
that are relevant to AIR should be consistent with measures that are relevant to all forms of reinsurance.  
 
3. Creating a new risk called “recapture risk.” While recapture has the potential to create some temporary strain, 
there are a number of elements that materially mitigate this exposure including funds-withheld/ModCo collateral 
structures (about 80 % of Bermuda AIR), 90-day notice requirements for recapture (which are typical), and cross-
border supervisory cooperation. The IAIS should therefore evaluate recapture within the broader context of credit 
quality and collateral design rather than as a stand-alone risk.

Pinpoint Policy 
Institute, United 
States 
Section 6 – Supervisory ConsiderationsThe final section of the Issues Paper contemplates potential 
enhancements to supervisory material to address perceived risks from alternative assets and AIR. The IAIS 
identified areas such as improving cross-border supervisory cooperation, ensuring that supervisors have the 
necessary expertise and data to oversee complex investments, managing conflicts of interest (for instance, when 
insurers invest in assets originated by affiliated entities or reinsure within the same group), and updating 
reporting requirements to capture these evolving exposures.   The U.S. regulatory community has likewise 
prioritized many of these areas. For example, state insurance departments have been investing in analytical 
expertise (hiring financial analysts and examiners skilled in structured finance, private equity, and other 
alternative asset classes) to better evaluate insurers’ portfolios. Additionally, U.S. regulators actively participate 
in supervisory colleges and global information exchanges for internationally active insu rers, consistent with 
Insurance Core Principles (ICP) coordination expectations.However, any enhancements or new standards that 
Noted for future 
work.

emerge from the IAIS must respect jurisdictional sovereignty and the primacy of domestic regulatory frameworks, 
particularly in countries like the United States.  Indeed, it is important to emphasize that while U.S. entities like 
the Federal Reserve, FIO, and NAIC participate in IAIS deliberations, they do so without formal public 
accountability mechanisms. It is essential that these forums remain advisory and do not evolve into backdoor 
rulemaking bodies.One concrete example of the IAIS exerting overreach is its erroneous and unsubstantiated 
concern over so-called “credit rating shopping.” The IAIS raises this issue in a footnote discussing the NAIC’s 
Securities Valuation Office (SVO), which is developing its own standards to determine when an external 
investment risk assessment may be deemed unreasonable for regulatory purposes. This critique reflects a 
misunderstanding of the U.S. framework and discriminates against private securities merely because their 
structure differs from public securities. Such a stance ignores established U.S. legal principles: under American 
common law, private funds are distinct from public funds and are appropriately treated differently.  International 
bodies should not attempt to override these well-founded distinctions.Any attempt by the IAIS to effectively 
mandate specific capital treatments or prohibitions would directly conflict with U.S. sovereignty over domestic 
insurance regulation. Notably, the IAIS’s ongoing effort to develop an Insurance Capital Standard (ICS) h as 
correctly been scrutinized as an attempt to impose a “European style” regulatory model not suited to the U.S. 
market. Congress required a formal study on the impact of the ICS on U.S. consumers and markets, reflecting 
the importance of this issue.  The message is clear: the IAIS has no supranational authority over U.S. insurance 
regulation, and any attempt to pressure U.S. regulators into adopting rules that have not been vetted by U.S. 
elected officials would be inappropriate.IAIS should pursue the constructive path of information-sharing and peer 
learning, rather than prescriptive rulemaking in these areas. The American approach to regulation, characterized 
by a mix of regulatory oversight and market discipline, has yielded a resilient insurance sector th at weathered the 
2008 financial crisis and the COVID-19 shock without system-wide failure. It would be ill-advised to undermine 
this framework by importing rules that are misaligned with our risk environment. Ultimately, the state-based 
regulatory system in the U.S. must retain the final say in adapting any international recommendations to our local 
context. Such an approach will ensure that enhancements to oversight of alternative assets and reinsurance are 
effective and sustainable.

Financial Group, 
United States

When considering the enhancement of the ICPs to address supervisory concerns related to alternative assets 
and AIR, it should be based on the understanding of actual transactions in each jurisdiction and entity, 
considering the highly bespoke nature of alternative assets and AIR transactions, which depends on local 
markets and regulatory environments as well as business models of each entity.  
 
Even when the IAIS considers developing globally universal regulatory requirements including capital and 
reserve requirements, these should be proportionate to the risks based on sufficient communication between 
supervisors and insurers. 
 
For example, enhancements to ICPs 9 and 20, that may require additional supervisory review and enhanced 
requirements for quantitative disclosure of metrics such as sensitivities to risks for ceding insurers and to 
regulations/legislation, may result in increased practical burdens. In considering reporting content and frequency, 
the IAIS should be cautious to take a balanced approach taking into consideration limitations of data and 
practical burdens. Additionally, reinsurance contracts are related to life insurance pricing and include sensitive 
pricing information. In enhancing ICPs 9 and 20, the IAIS should also take note of the confid entiality of such 
information.

- While there will be a subsequent public consultation on the amendments to the ICP, the Issues Paper will 
benefit from greater details on the likely amendments to be made to the ICPs. Using ICP 13 as an example, 
there are a number of areas identified for enhancements which do not describe the nature of the enhancements. 
The enhancements being considered and their background (such as whether it relates to the rise of AIR or other 
developments) can be made clearer – e.g.  
o “Assess whether ICP 13 could better address reinsurers and assuming jurisdictions.” It is not clear what IAIS 
means by better addressing reinsurers and assuming jurisdictions.  
o On “Sidecars: The description of sidecars and the focus on property and casualty”, we are not clear of the 
association between sidecars and property and casualty (P&C) in the context of AIR. It is quite clear from earlier 
text in Section 4.1 that AIR is applicable to life business and not (P&C business), and mention of sidecars in the 
same section also relates to AIR in life business.   
o On “Mitigating the Reinsurer’s Credit Risk”, What is the scope of enhancement being considered? Would it go 
beyond asset quality and cover other relevant considerations such as safeguards around the assets (e.g. trust or 
collaterals)?  
 
- We had also noted that there are some proposed enhancements that may not seem necessary. For instance, 
ICP 3. We note that the current list of information in ICP 3.1.1 is broad enough and would not preclude the 
sharing of information of reinsurance treaties etc. As such, the proposed enhancement does not appear to be 
necessary. 
 
- On ICP 14, we note that IAIS made the point that “Jurisdictional differences in regulation and accounting 
complicate consistency and harmonisation efforts …..A harmonised approach to liability valuation is crucial to 
ensure consistent risk treatment and policyholder protection. In AIR, simultaneous asset and liability valuation is 
vital to ensure soundness and prevent regulatory arbitrage.” Firstly, it is not clear what a “ simultaneous asset and 
liability valuation” means. Secondly, the main text in sections 4.2, just alluded to the fact that differences in 
reserve valuation, capital requirements and investment flexibility in different jurisdictions can drive AIR activity. I t 
had not suggested that a harmonised approach is necessary. In fact, given that these jurisdictional differences 
already exist and will continue to exist, it is not clear to us that the use of AIR requires a harmonised approach. 
The key is for supervisors to better understand the differences so that we can assess if we have any concerns 
with the motivation behind AIR.

The Geneva 
Association, 
International 
The paper provides no clear basis for making changes to the current ICPs to address structural shifts and we do 
not support changes at this time. 
 
Overall, we caution against introducing new categories of “alternative assets” and “asset -intensive reinsurance” 
in supervisory material. 
 
Regarding ICP 8, we suggest that the IAIS specify why the risk management function should be responsible for 
assessing legal and jurisdictional differences. Additionally, clarification is needed on the types of controls 
required for the actuarial function concerning reinsurance activity and whose technical provisions are being 
referenced. We believe the cedent’s actuarial function should not be responsible for the reinsurer’s actuarial 
function. 
 
For ICP 9 and ICP 13, we contend that the focus on recapture risk is overstated. Recapture clauses are typically 
triggered only under extreme circumstances and their effects would be confined to immediate counterparties, 
unlikely to pose a systemic concern. In cases of intra-group reinsurance, it is improbable that a group would 
jeopardize one of its own entities. We recommend against introducing recapture as a discrete risk in this section, 
as it is an option granted to the cedent rather than a stand-alone prudential threat. 
 
Regarding ICP 13, we do not support introducing the concept of jurisdictional "concentration" risk, as it appears 
to target specific markets and could lead to unreasonable outcomes. We also believe that theoretical concerns 
about recapture risk have not been adequately substantiated; this "risk" should not be introduced, as recapture is 
an option held by the ceding party. Finally, due to fundamental differences in market and book accounting, the 
usefulness of a point-in-time comparison of total asset requirements may be limited. Meanwhile, other proposed 
enhancements to ICP 13 do not seem necessary, as they are already addressed elsewhere in the ICPs.  
 
In ICP 16, it is unclear why the existence of reinsurance should alter risk limits, as reinsurance serves as a tool t o 
keep risks within those limits. Further clarification is needed on what it means to concretely consider 
"macroeconomic factors and the interdependencies in risk transfer contracts."  
 
For ICP 20, we believe that public disclosure of "assumptions" may be excessively granular. We also suggest 
that alternative assets should not be singled out as a special category for disclosure. Publicly reporting details 
before and after transactional effects is not necessarily appropriate, nor is it clear what is meant by "sensitivities 
to regulatory and legal regimes." Finally, we consider "identify potential conflicts of interest" a more realistic 
standard for public disclosures than enabling stakeholders to "evaluate" them fairly.

In ICP 24, we have concerns about the introduction of jurisdictional concentration risk, as this seems targeted at

Finally, in ICP 25, we reiterate our suggestion to avoid references to recapture risk.

ACLI agrees with the Paper’s findings that the current Insurance Core Principles (ICPs) adequately address the 
supervisory concerns raised in the paper.  
 
With respect to the proposed enhancements discussed in Section 6.3, upon review, it seems that many of the 
areas highlighted for potential enhancements in the IAIS supervisory and/or supporting material are already 
incorporated into the existing ICPs. 
 
I. Comments on the proposed enhancements to ICP 14, valuation (p. 67): 
ACLI agrees that consistent valuation of assets and liabilities is an essential concept. However, we are 
concerned with the statement that implies jurisdictional differences complicate “harmonization efforts” and that “a 
harmonized approach to liability valuation is crucial to ensure consistent risk treatment and policyholder 
protection.” While we are sure it was not meant to imply that the IAIS was recommending global convergence 
with respect to liability valuation, we are recommending two minor edits to eliminate any confusion.  
***Recommended edit to the section on ICP 14: Valuation (p. 67) 
(1) Delete "and harmonisations efforts" in the second sentence in the "Relevance" text.  
(2) Delete the next-to-last sentence in the Relevance text, that reads: "A harmonised approach to liability 
valuation is crucial to ensure consistent risk treatment and policyholder protection."  
 
II. Comments demonstrating how the proposed enhancements may overlap with existing ICPs:  
 
- ICP 3: The Paper suggests enhancing ICP 3 (Information Sharing and confidentialit y) to enhance information 
sharing information among insurance supervisors and with non-insurance supervisors. However, ICP 3.1.1 
already provides broad guidance encompassing information sharing on a broad range of topics that could include 
reinsurance treaties outsourcing, etc. ICP 3.1.2 expressly contemplates the insurance supervisor sharing 
information among supervisors and with non-insurance supervisors. ICP 3.2 also provides guidance on cross -
border collaboration. 
- ICP 7: the recommended enhancements target conflicts of interest between insurers and related parties. 
However, addressing conflicts of interests falls squarely within the existing supervisory mandates and periodic 
reviews. For example, ICP 7.9 requires insurers to provide supervisors with ti mely information about material 
Noted for future 
work. Revisions 
done in the text 
relating to ICP 
13.

related party transactions, “major ownerships and group structures and any significant affiliations and alliances;” 
ICP 7.4 addresses conflicts of interest between individual Board members and the insurer (ICP 7.4). ICP 7.1 
requires the Board to establish a “rigorous process” for setting, approving, and overseeing  
- ICP 8: The proposed enhancements to ICP 8 are often duplicative of existing ICPs. For example, a proposed 
enhancement to actuarial function was “How asset-liability management ensures assets and future revenues 
cover obligations to policyholders and capital requirements, especially in AIR agreements.” ICP 8.6.4 already 
requires the actuarial function to advise on asset-liability management “with regard to the adequacy and 
sufficiency of assets and future revenues to cover the insurer’s obligations to policyholders and capital 
requirements, etc.” Similarly, ICP 8.6 already requires insurers to have actuarial functions capable of evaluating 
and advising on technical provisions, premium, and pricing activities, capital adequacy, reinsurance and 
compliance. ICP 8.1.3 is already clear that risk management systems must include risks from investments, 
asset-liability management, liquidity, concentration and reinsurance (among others). Those are just a fe w 
examples where the existing ICPs already address issues raised in the proposed enhancements to ICP 8.  
- ICP 13: The Paper recommends enhancing “Supervisors understanding the supervisory regime of another 
jurisdiction and its impact on the total asset requirement for AIR transactions.” ICP 13.4 recommends the cedent 
regulator review the supervisory framework and practices in the jurisdiction of the reinsurer or engage in 
supervisor-to-supervisor dialogue. ICP 13.3 requires the cedent to demonstrate the economic impact of the risk 
transfer originating from their reinsurance contract. To the extent that the IAIS determines high -level updates to 
ICP 13 are needed, those updates should be informed by ongoing dialogue between regulators and the 
development of industry and regulatory best practices.

With respect to potential enhancements to the Insurance Core Principles (ICPs), we do not believe the draft has 
demonstrated that any changes are necessary, and it would be premature in the absence of a careful, data -
driven analysis that adequately reflects the needs, products, and demonstrated success of supervisory 
frameworks in individual jurisdictions. Rather, any outcomes from this work should focus on providing guidance 
that illustrates what constitutes sound and reasonable practices. The current ICP framework already enables 
effective global monitoring and supervisory review, ensuring that life insurers understand and appropriately 
manage the investment risks they assume. This paper does not present a compelling, holistic case, especially 
when considering stakeholder feedback, which would justify formal amendments. A rush to amend the ICPs 
could inadvertently create the appearance of favouring certain jurisdictions' supervisory approaches over others, 
in the absence of hard data that a problem exists.  
 
Regarding the specific ICPs mentioned in the IAIS Consultation, here are our views:  
 
ICP 3: No comment.

ICP 7 (Conflicts of Interest): The proposed changes are unnecessary, as ICP 7 already addresses conflicts of 
interest, including those involving controllers and the scope of supervision. 
 
ICP 8 (Risk Management and Internal Controls): The issues raised have already been addressed under the 
existing ICP wording, including aspects related to structural shifts, which are incorporated into supervisory stress 
testing. 
 
ICP 9 (Supervisory Review and Reporting): It is unclear that any proposed changes are justifi ed, especially as 
many of these elements have already been well developed. 
 
ICP 13 (Reinsurance): No specific justification provided for changes. 
 
ICP 14 (Valuation): Supervisors and insurers should retain the ability to conduct internal valuations rather than 
rely solely on external providers. 
 
ICP 15 (Investments): In our view, no changes are needed, as the relevant issues are already addressed within 
the current framework. 
Athene, USA 
Athene, USA respectfully submits that, at this stage, wholesale revisions to the Insurance Core Principles (ICPs) 
are premature. Instead, we recommend that the IAIS continue to monitor evolving market practices and gather 
targeted best practices from key jurisdictions – particularly the United States and Bermuda – before considering 
any formal changes.THE EXISTING ICP FRAMEOWRK ALREADY ACCOMODATES EMERGING RISKS The 
IAIS’s own analysis concludes that the current ICPs and ComFrame “are designed to broadly encompass the 
various risks that could potentially arise from increased capital allocation to alternative assets and AIR.”  In 
particular, ICP 3 through ICP 25 already address core areas - information sharing, governance, risk 
management, valuation, investments, ERM, public disclosure, macroprudential supervision, and supervisory 
cooperation - relevant to structural shifts in life insurance. Any perceived gaps are better met through 
supplementary guidance or application papers, rather than by reopening the text of more than 40 
ICPs.SIGNIFICANT JURISDICTIONAL REFORMS ARE ALREADY UNDERWAY Both the U.S. and Bermuda 
have recently enhanced their regimes in ways that directly address the supervisory concerns underlying Section 
6.3 - without imposing undue rigidity or stifling innovation. Examples include:       - United States: Modernization 
of statutory reserves and RBC, soon to be adopted asset adequacy testing for reinsurance, expanded 
macroprudential tools, and new stress-testing requirements for liquidity and credit exposures.       - Bermuda: 
Introduction of enhanced solvency standards (including updated collateral structures for AIR), a scenario -based

liquidity regime, and strengthened board-level governance expectations.Rather than duplicating these efforts, the 
IAIS should account for from these reforms and refine its supervisory materials to spotlight proven best practices 
and data collection approaches. A "MONITOR AND LEARN' APPROACH WILL YIELD BETTER OUTCOMES    -  
Given the still-developing nature of the market for “alternative” assets and AIR; a phased approach is preferable. 
This approach will allow the IAIS to capture real world evidence on risk drivers, supervisory efficacy, and 
policyholder outcomes before embarking on any formal standard setting.    - Ongoing monitoring of how the U.S. 
and Bermuda (and similarly advanced supervisory systems) continue refining their oversight of private credit and 
AIR.     - Additional monitoring: While the bulk of AIR activity remains in thoroughly supervised markets such as 
the United States and Bermuda, we acknowledge that a small but growing share is migrating to jurisdictions 
whose supervisory frameworks are still maturing – e.g., the Cayman Islands.  Most of these jurisdictions also do 
not participate in the IAIS’s Global Monitoring Exercise, so transparency is an issue.  The IAIS should encourage 
these jurisdictions to participate in the GME to help ensure that safeguards proven elsewhere are adopted 
consistently, limiting any future contagion channels.     - Assessment of emerging metrics (for recapture 
frequency, liquidity-stress test outcomes, governance-model effectiveness).     -  Consider “light touch” 
application guidance (e.g., illustrative templates for information-sharing protocols under ICP 3, or conflict-
management frameworks under ICP 7).     - Holistic integration of lessons learned into supplementary 
supervisory guidance, rather than rewriting ICP text.     - Targeted enhancements - if and when warranted - 
should be proportionate. Should the IAIS identify persistent supervisory gaps after this learning phase, any 
subsequent enhancements should be narrowly tailored. For example, (1) under ICP 9, publish non-prescriptive 
reporting templates for cross-border AIR exposures; (2) under ICP 15, issue guidelines on in-house expertise 
and concentration limits calibrated to local market depth; (3) under ICP 24–25, develop best practice toolkits for 
macroprudential data collection and supervisory college coordination on complex reinsurance arrangements.      - 
By contrast, broad-brush amendments to multiple ICPs would risk unintended conflicts with well-established 
national frameworks (e.g., U.S. RBC and Solvency II type market-consistent capital approaches) and could 
hamper insurers’ ability to support long-term guarantees efficiently.Athene, USA believes the current ICPs 
remain fit-for-purpose. The IAIS should defer any formal changes and instead pursue a deliberate “monitor, 
collect, assess” strategy – drawing on the regulatory innovations already underway in the United States and 
Bermuda, and leveraging targeted supervisory guidance to disseminate effective practices globally.

(BILTIR), Bermuda 
This section identifies potential enhancements to supervisory and supporting materials in light of the evolving 
investment and reinsurance strategies within the life insurance sector. This includes reassessing how risks 
related to alternative assets and AIR are addressed through ICPs, ComFrame, and associated guidance. We 
believe several proposed enhancements are already captured within the ICPs; therefore, we do not believe 
changes to the IAIS’ supervisory material are necessary. Additionally, the ICP fram ework is structured at a high 
level to allow for broad application to allow for proportionate application.  ICPs should not focus on specific 
issues related to sub-segments of the market. 
 
In considering enhancements, we believe it is important for the IAIS to distinguish between structural shifts that 
may warrant supervisory modernization and those that merely reflect healthy market adaptation. The ICPs and 
framework should be jurisdictionally agnostic, but the IAIS should acknowledge market depth, supervisory quality 
and advanced knowledge base of the jurisdiction in its evaluation. The Oliver Wyman Analysis of Systemic Risk 
in the Bermuda Long-Term Insurance Sector (“OW Analysis”) provides several findings that can help inform this 
calibration, which we encourage the IAIS to take into consideration: 
 
1. The OW Analysis supports the IAIS’ focus on increasing transparency and closing information gaps related to 
alternative assets and AIR transactions. However, it strongly cautions against the adoption of bro ad definitions or 
overly prescriptive classifications of “alternative assets.” Instead, the OW Analysis advocates for a risk -based 
and principle-driven approach. Such an approach should consider asset-specific characteristics while also taking 
into account liability profiles and insurer business models. This proportionality is critical to avoid inadvertently 
constraining sound and well-managed investment strategies that align with long-term obligations. 
 
2. Regarding the call for enhanced guidance on cross-border reinsurance risk in Section 6.3, but the OW 
Analysis suggests that the focus should remain on counterparty risk governance and recapture planning. AIR, 
when implemented with robust collateralization, clear investment guidelines, and strong reinsurer  supervision is a 
risk-transfer mechanism, not a systemic risk catalyst. Enhancements to supervisory material should therefore 
Noted for future 
work. Revisions 
done in the text 
relating to ICP 
13. The paper 
does not propose 
changes to ICPs 
and it is 
premature for the 
next steps to be 
covered in the 
paper.

emphasize good practices in counterparty risk assessment, collateral monitoring, and stress testing, rather than 
suggest restrictions based on jurisdiction or structure alone. 
 
3. To the extent that IAIS supervisory material is updated, it should incorporate lessons from recent empirical 
stress testing, such as those conducted by the Bermuda Monetary Authority (BMA), which found that Bermudian 
reinsurers hold sufficient liquidity to meet surrender demands without triggering forced sales of illiquid assets, 
even under extreme conditions. This empirical evidence reinforces the need for supervisory materials to 
distinguish between theoretical risk pathways and those that are substantiated by data and actual firm behaviour.  
 
4. The OW Analysis recommends that supervisory enhancements prioritize disclosure regimes that improve 
visibility into reinsurance counterparties, collateral arrangements, and asset composition without creating 
duplicative or inconsistent reporting burdens. This aligns with the BMA’s own proposed enhancements to public 
disclosure and would help supervisors more accurately assess sector-wide exposures without introducing blunt 
supervisory instruments. 
 
In addition, we offer the following comments and suggested revisions for consideration regarding specific ICP 
enhancements: 
 
ICP 3 (Information Sharing and Confidentiality Requirements): We recommend clarifying what is meant by “non -
insurance supervisors.” 
 
ICP 8 (Risk Management and Internal Control): On cross-border activity and concentration risks, it is unclear why 
the risk management function should be responsible for assessing legal and jurisdictional differences. We 
recommend removing or adding clarity.  On quantitative implications, it is unclear why the actuarial function 
needs controls “on the implications of reinsurance activity on technical provisions, premiums, pricing, and capital 
adequacy.”  We recommend providing further information on the types of controls contemplated by this 
enhancement as well as whose technical provisions. We do not believe the cedent’s actuarial function should be 
responsible for the reinsurer’s actuarial work products. 
 
ICP 9 (Supervisory Review and Reporting): We view the focus on recapture risk as likely misguided and 
overstated. Recapture clauses are triggered only in extreme circumstances (e.g., war, insolvency, failure to fulfil 
contractual obligations). In general, the only “forced” recapture involves a failure of the cedent to pay premiums.  
 
ICP 13 (Reinsurance and Other Forms of Risk Transfer): We believe ICP 13, as it stands, is sufficient. While

some potential areas of enhancement are reasonable, they are covered by existing ICPs. Other enhancements 
appear to be overreaching or misguided. 
 
As an overarching matter, we do not support labelling a subset of reinsurance transactions as “asset -intensive” 
and imposing enhanced supervisory requirements solely on transactions with the “AIR” label.  
 
Proposed enhancements that are covered by existing ICPs: 
- We agree that risk management of assets associated with reinsurance agreements is important, which i s why 
treaties commonly establish various investment restrictions (not limited to credit). Although such restrictions are 
not given as a specific example, they are encompassed by ICP 13.2, which includes all factors associated with 
credit risk posed by the reinsurer. 
- Counterparty credit risk, from an ERM standpoint, is already addressed in ICP 16.  
- The connection between a reinsurance programme and an overall business strategy is encompassed by ICP 
8.1.2 and ICP 13.1. 
- We agree that supervisors can benefit from “understanding the supervisory regime of another jurisdiction,” but 
this is already covered implicitly by ICP 13.4. 
- Both internal and third-party reinsurance are encompassed by the group-wide supervisory assessment 
referenced in ICP 25.2.2. 
- We see no obvious reason to “enhance” ICP 13 to “better address reinsurers and assuming jurisdictions,” as 
the ICPs that are relevant to direct writers and ceding insurers have corresponding application to reinsurers.  
 
Enhancements we do not support: 
- We object to introducing the concept of jurisdictional “concentration” risk, as it targets smaller jurisdictions with 
developed reinsurance markets and could lead to unreasonable outcomes if applied globally.  
- The theoretical concerns about recapture risk have not been adequately substantiated. This “risk” should not be 
introduced, as recapture is an option held by the cedent. 
- Because of fundamental market/book accounting differences, the usefulness of a point -in-time comparison of 
total asset requirements may be limited, and we do not believe the IAIS should codify a potentially misleading 
analysis as a supervisory best practice. 
 
ICP 14 (Valuation): We have concerns with the broad nature of the proposed enhancements.  
 
ICP 15 (Investments): The section on reinsurance asset management includes another reference to recapture.  
The effect of this may be to overemphasize this possibility.

ICP 16 (Enterprise Risk Management for Solvency Purposes): Any changes should avoid measures broadly 
applied to “alternative assets,” instead focusing on proportionate, risk-based measures, as stated elsewhere in 
our response and supported by the OW Analysis. In addition, it is unclear what it means to consider 
“macroeconomic factors and the interdependencies in risk transfer contracts” – we recommend clarifying. 
 
ICP 20 (Public Disclosure): The public disclosure of “assumptions” may be excessively granula r for public 
disclosure.  In addition, we do not believe alternative assets should be singled out for disclosure purposes as a 
special category of “risky” assets. 
 
Due to the proprietary nature of transactions, we do not believe it is necessarily appropriate to publicly report 
before/after effects of transactions. We also recommend clarifying what is meant by “sensitivities to regulatory 
and legal regimes.” 
 
On conflicts of interest, we do not believe it is realistic for public disclosures to be sufficient to allow stakeholders 
to fairly “evaluate” conflicts of interest.  We recommend changing “evaluate” to “identify.”  
 
ICP 24 (Macroprudential Supervision): We believe the proposed enhancement of “concentrations toward specific 
… jurisdictions,” which seems to be targeting Bermuda, is problematic and unsupported. If jurisdictional 
“concentrations” posed a risk to financial stability and the global economy, then every major insurance 
jurisdiction across the globe, including many with vastly larger sums of insurance risk than Bermuda, would pose 
an even greater threat to financial stability than Bermuda. Regardless of the jurisdiction, the key to policyholder 
protection and financial stability is adequate and appropriate governance, risk management, internal con trols, 
and supervision. ICP 24 already supports assessment of concentration risk, including with respect to markets, 
sectors, and geographical areas and do not believe further enhancements geared solely toward AIR are 
necessary or appropriate. 
 
We also recommend clarifying what “reinsurance factors” are. 
 
ICP 25 (Supervisory Cooperation and Coordination):  We recommend removing reference to recapture risk.  
Athora, Bermuda

Moody’s Ratings,

The following comments concern two specific supporting guidelines, ICP 9 and ICP 20.  
 
ICP 9 
ICP 9 focuses on supervisory review and reporting, outlining how supervisors should utilize off -site monitoring 
and on-site inspections to evaluate insurers’ business operations, financial condition, governance structures, and 
overall risk profiles. Key elements of this process rightly include supervisory planning, thorough analysis, 
providing feedback, timely intervention when necessary, and collaboration with othe r relevant authorities. To 
enhance the effectiveness of these supervisory activities, we recommend adding oversight and review of the use 
of credit ratings by insurers to the list of focus areas for both off-site monitoring and on-site inspections. 
Particular attention should be given to any discernible differences in how insurers select credit rating providers 
and subsequently apply ratings from different providers within their internal risk management processes versus 
their regulatory submissions (e.g., for purposes of assigning risk-based capital charges on insurers’ 
investments). Attention should also be paid to any discernible differences in how insurers use public and private 
credit ratings.  
 
ICP 20 
ICP 20 appropriately addresses supervisor-required public disclosures, detailing the information insurers should 
make available regarding their business activities, governance frameworks, risk profiles, financial condition, 
investment portfolios, asset-liability management, and capital adequacy. To further strengthen transparency and 
market understanding in this area, we recommend the following additions to the disclosure requirements:  
1. Insurers should provide enhanced visibility into the nature of their private investments, more clearly identifying 
the nature and structure of their assets.  
2. When insurers use third-party credit ratings for regulatory purposes, including to support regulatory filings or 
internal risk assessments, regulators should consider encouraging insurers to use public ratings. Going  forward, 
to enhance transparency in the case of a private rating being used for regulatory capital purposes, regulators 
could consider encouraging the disclosure of the identity of the rating provider, where permitted.

Demographic ageing has already generated a significant retirement-savings and protection gap; life insurers’

ACLI emphasizes that this Issues Paper acknowledges that neither “alternative” assets nor AIR pose any 
systemic risk. We recommend clarifying this point in the conclusion of the paper. Given that systemic risk 
appears to be highly unlikely, it is reasonable to consider this issue as a “microprudential” matter, allowing 
jurisdictions more latitude to address it according to their specific contexts.  
 
ACLI strongly recommends providing additional context to the Conclusion (and Executive Summary) to recognize 
the growing demand from an ageing society for asset-intensive retirement products. Section 4 acknowledges this 
by observing that “demographic trends in developed markets have driven increasing demand for insurance 
products that are designed for retirement saving” and that AIR is an additional source of capital necessary to 
support the growth of these asset-intensive lines of business. The significance of this demographic demand for 
asset intensive products, discussed in section 4 (p. 33), warrants mention in the Executive Summary. We 
recommend including similar language in the Executive Summary to provide additional context for why these 
shifts are occurring in the life insurance industry.

As noted in the Paper, the current global exposure to these risks remains relatively limited and concentrated 
within a few highly regulated jurisdictions. Rather than producing additional prescriptive guidance or universal 
rules that may conflict with or undermine targeted local approaches, ACLI encourages the IAIS to track how 
jurisdictions with material “alternative” assets and AIR exposure continue to refine their treatment of “alternative” 
assets and AIR. ACLI urges the IAIS to take a measured, evidence-led approach to any future changes. We note 
that key jurisdictions, including the U.S. and Bermuda, have been actively reviewing their supervisory 
frameworks and introducing enhancements in response to the structural shifts. Given the heterogeneity of 
insurance markets and importance of preserving the ability of insurers to support long -term economic needs, we 
believe jurisdictional supervisors are best positioned to lead on assessing and responding to the structural shifts 
and the IAIS should be deferential to their work. 
 
ACLI recommends that the IAIS continue to assess these trends and continue to engage in ongoing dialogue 
with industry, stakeholders and jurisdictions to potentially identify best practices. The IAIS is well positioned to 
gather information about these issues, jurisdictional responses, and disseminate this information to regula tors to 
promote dialogue on these issues. However, it would be premature to develop global policy until supervisors 
have had the opportunity to evaluate how regional reforms are playing out.  
 
***Recommended edits, Section 7 (3rd para., p. 71):

need to evaluate assets relative to liabilities they are supporting. After "...uncertainty, illiquidity, and complexity..." 
insert <<"keeping in mind that an evaluation of assets should be relative to the liabilities they are supporting and 
therefore consider the duration, liquidity, and other characteristics of the associated liabilities, both in normal 
times and in times of stress. >> 
(2) Strike "indicative" from the second sentence of the third paragraph of Section 7.  
 A principles-based approach should not result in a list of “indicative” assets that are deemed “alternative” on the 
basis of an aggregated survey of supervisors who operate in jurisdictions with vastly different capital and private 
markets. This section should highlight that it is ultimately up to each supervisor to identify “alternatives.” 
Jurisdictional perspectives on whether an asset is “alternative” can vary significantly because the r isk 
characteristics of the same asset may vary by jurisdiction due to differences in capital markets and secondary 
markets.

Athene, USA thanks the IAIS for recognizing the vital role that life insurers can play in closing the global 
retirement income gap. We welcome the acknowledgment that private credit and asset -intensive reinsurance 
strategies, when structured prudently, offer genuine diversification benefits, capture illiquidity premia for 
policyholders, and free up capital to finance long-term infrastructure and productive investments. 
 
We support the IAIS’s recognition – drawn from U.S. and Bermuda experience – that modern private credit

transactions to date have been collateralized, conservatively leveraged, and subject to pre -transaction 
supervisory approval. These real-world outcomes confirm that such strategies can be woven safely into insurers’ 
asset liability management frameworks without creating systemic vulnerabilities.  
 
Consistent with our submission, we fully support the IAIS’s commitment to a principles -based supervisory

Competitive 
Enterprise 
Institute, United 
States 
Conclusion 
In closing, the IAIS’s draft issue paper on alternative assets needs to be revised. For the above reasons, the 
paper focuses too heavily on the speculative risks of alternative assets with zero mention of the risks that public 
funds carry. IAIS also fails to provide any concrete evidence that a reliance on private fund investments  is 
leading toward a market crisis or systemic disruption of the life insurance industry. We simply do not have any 
clear reason to be concerned with the investment shift toward private funds. 
  
Additionally, the way IAIS brands its paper, one would assume that private equity, hedge funds, alternative 
mutual funds and real estate are all highly risky and unrefined assets that should be approached with extreme 
caution. By contrast, there is hardly any criticism for stocks and bonds, which are by no means perfect 
investment vehicles by comparison. This provides a lopsided and disingenuous portrayal of the public vs private 
markets that risks inviting misleading conclusions for your members. 
 
Lastly, the IAIS situates its criticisms toward an inescapable conclusion: private funds need to be compelled to 
be more transparent. This dangerous view invites greater regulation of private funds as if they were offered by 
publicly traded companies on an exchange. The IAIS should remove any such insinuation from its final paper, 
backtracking its calls to have greater transparency from private funds. This is another way of calling for entities 
Noted for future 
work eg 
Application 
Paper.

like the SEC to pass rules compelling private fund managers to disclose reams of information in the way a large 
public company would be expected to.  
 
This is simply not the way the private markets should be treated and if there is any doubt about that, look to the 
Fifth Circuit Court’s recent decision to strike down the SEC’s Private Fund Rule.  The new chair of the SEC, Paul 
Atkins, has also encouraged broadening access to private funds in the US, home of the largest financial markets.  
The IAIS shouldn’t pursue the opposite path through alienating access to private funds for life insurers. The IAIS 
has much work to do to revise this paper in a manner accurately describes alternative assets, their inherent 
benefits, the competitive advantages of private funds for long-term investments, and the downsides of public 
fund options. I hope you will do so when considering comments from the public. Thank you, and I look forward to 
reading the final draft.

Athora, Bermuda 
We do not support the conclusions made for the reason and comments made above. The conclusion needs to 
emphasise that life insurers are adapting to a changing macro and demographic landscape, not simply chasing 
yield or exploiting gaps. Innovation in AIR and adaptation to asset liability management strategies is a relevant 
evolution to protect policyholders. 
Demographic trends, underfunded pensions, public funding constraints and the growing protection gap should be 
considered as major drivers of AIR and asset-intensive insurance. These structural needs justify innovation in 
capital management.  
The protection gap in the life and annuity sector is a global challenge that affects both developed and emerging 
markets. There is increasing recognition that people and businesses have far less protection than they need. 
This gap has significant implications for the well-being and resilience of individuals, households and businesses.  
According to GFIA, the global pension protection gap was estimated at USD 51 trillion in 2023 and is expected to 
continue to grow due to the ageing population, increased life expectancy and having fewer people in the 
workforce to support the inflow of funds into the pension schemes due. This results from falling birth rates, 
coupled with the increasing number of people receiving disbursements and high living standards that increase 
the need for higher pensions. 
It is important that the conclusion is reiterated that AIR is not a systemic risk, as the IAIS’s own analysis 
confirms. This will provide clarity to stakeholders and prevent misinterpretation by markets or press. The Issues 
Paper acknowledges that the risk to global financial stability is limited, thus should also be reflected in the 
conclusion section. 
Revisions done.

Pinpoint Policy 
Institute, United

Conclusion 
 
While IAIS’s in-depth examination of structural shifts in the life insurance sector offers some insights, from a U.S. 
perspective, these shifts are being managed through a combination of prudent regulation, industry risk 
management, and market forces. The increasing allocations to alternative assets and use of asset-intensive 
reinsurance have contributed to the strength and competitiveness of the U.S. life insurance industry, allowing 
insurers to offer attractive guarantees and products to consumers while maintaining solvency.  
 
The U.S. stands as an example of how a market-oriented yet tightly supervised insurance sector can thrive. It is 
vital that international standards do not push U.S. regulators toward adopting overly restrictive or ill -suited 
regulations under the banner of global uniformity.
